### Question 101: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 01 (response)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the cellular and molecular mechanisms of acute cellular rejection in solid organ transplantation, specifically the role of T lymphocytes (CD8+ cytotoxic T cells and CD4+ helper T cells) and their recognition of alloantigens presented by MHC molecules, leading to graft damage. (理解实体器官移植中急性细胞性排斥的细胞和分子机制，特别是T淋巴细胞（CD8+细胞毒性T细胞和CD4+辅助T细胞）的作用及其对MHC分子呈递的异体抗原的识别，导致移植物损伤。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with one HLA mismatch at the A locus. Post-transplant, he is maintained on immunosuppressive therapy consisting of tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks after transplantation, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals interstitial edema, inflammatory cell infiltrates primarily composed of lymphocytes, and evidence of tubular epithelial cell necrosis. Immunofluorescence staining shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the peripheral blood shows a significant increase in CD3+ T cells, with a predominance of CD8+ T cells expressing granzyme B. Which of the following cellular mechanisms is MOST directly responsible for the observed graft injury?

(A) Antibody-mediated complement activation leading to endothelial damage.
(B) CD4+ T helper cells secreting IL-2, promoting B cell activation and antibody production against donor HLA antigens.
(C) CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes directly killing graft parenchymal cells (tubular epithelial cells) expressing donor MHC class I molecules.
(D) Macrophages phagocytosing graft cells opsonized by donor-specific antibodies.
(E) Neutrophils releasing proteases and reactive oxygen species, causing acute tubular necrosis.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for potential transplant complications.
- **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus (calcineurin inhibitor), mycophenolate mofetil (purine synthesis inhibitor), prednisone (corticosteroid). Despite immunosuppression, rejection can still occur.
- **Timeline:** 3 weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is typical for acute rejection, particularly acute cellular rejection (ACR).
- **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine. These are classic signs of acute graft dysfunction, suggestive of rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Interstitial edema, lymphocyte infiltrates, tubular epithelial cell necrosis. This points towards cellular injury within the graft parenchyma.
- **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. While C4d is a marker of antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), it can also be seen in ACR, especially in cases with mixed rejection or as a consequence of inflammation. However, the predominant cellular infiltrate is lymphocytes.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD3+ T cells, predominance of CD8+ T cells expressing granzyme B. This strongly indicates a T cell-mediated response, specifically cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs). Granzyme B is a key effector molecule released by CTLs to induce apoptosis in target cells.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST direct cellular mechanism responsible for graft injury.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** This is a T cell-mediated immune response against donor alloantigens (primarily MHC molecules) expressed on the graft. It typically occurs within the first few months post-transplant.
- **T Cell Subsets:**
    - **CD4+ T helper cells:** Recognize donor MHC class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells (APCs) within the graft. They secrete cytokines like IL-2 and IFN-γ, which activate other immune cells, including CD8+ T cells and macrophages.
    - **CD8+ Cytotoxic T Lymphocytes (CTLs):** Recognize donor MHC class I molecules expressed on graft parenchymal cells (e.g., tubular epithelial cells in the kidney). Upon recognition, CTLs directly kill these cells through mechanisms involving perforin and granzyme B release, leading to apoptosis of the target cell.
- **Mechanism in this Case:** The biopsy shows tubular epithelial cell necrosis, and flow cytometry shows increased CD8+ T cells expressing granzyme B. This directly implicates CD8+ CTLs as the primary effector cells causing parenchymal damage by recognizing donor MHC class I on tubular cells and inducing their death. The presence of C4d suggests some antibody involvement or inflammation, but the dominant cellular infiltrate and the specific CTL phenotype point towards ACR as the primary process, with CTL-mediated cytotoxicity being the direct mechanism of parenchymal injury.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Antibody-mediated complement activation leading to endothelial damage. This describes Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR). While C4d deposition can be seen in AMR, the primary histological finding here is tubular epithelial cell necrosis and lymphocyte infiltration, not primarily endothelial damage. The flow cytometry data strongly supports a T cell response, not an antibody response. AMR typically involves donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) targeting HLA antigens on endothelial cells, leading to complement activation, endothelial injury, and microvascular inflammation.
- (B): CD4+ T helper cells secreting IL-2, promoting B cell activation and antibody production against donor HLA antigens. This describes a mechanism contributing to AMR, where CD4+ T cells help B cells produce DSAs. While CD4+ T cells are involved in ACR (by activating CD8+ T cells and macrophages), the direct killing of parenchymal cells (tubular epithelial cells) is primarily mediated by CD8+ T cells in this scenario, as evidenced by the flow cytometry and biopsy findings. This option focuses on the humoral response pathway, which is less directly supported by the data.
- (D): Macrophages phagocytosing graft cells opsonized by donor-specific antibodies. This is another mechanism associated with AMR, where antibodies opsonize graft cells (often endothelial cells), leading to their phagocytosis by macrophages. Again, the clinical picture and flow cytometry data point more strongly towards T cell-mediated cytotoxicity.
- (E): Neutrophils releasing proteases and reactive oxygen species, causing acute tubular necrosis. This describes acute tubular necrosis (ATN) due to ischemia-reperfusion injury or nephrotoxic drugs, not immune-mediated rejection. While inflammation can involve neutrophils, the predominant infiltrate in ACR is lymphocytes, and the specific mechanism involves T cell cytotoxicity, not neutrophil-mediated damage.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR)                                  | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR)                               | Ischemic Acute Tubular Necrosis (ATN)                          |
|----------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Primary Mediator** | T lymphocytes (CD4+ helper, CD8+ cytotoxic)                       | Donor-Specific Antibodies (DSAs), Complement                    | Ischemia, Reperfusion Injury                                   |
| **Key Cells**        | CD3+ T cells (esp. CD8+ CTLs), Macrophages                       | B cells, Plasma cells, Endothelial cells, Macrophages, Neutrophils | Tubular epithelial cells, Neutrophils                           |
| **Histology**        | Lymphocyte infiltrates, Parenchymal cell necrosis (tubules)       | Endothelialitis, Microvascular inflammation (C4d deposition)     | Tubular cell injury/necrosis, Casts, Minimal inflammation      |
| **Mechanism**        | CTL-mediated cytotoxicity (perforin/granzyme), Cytokine release | Complement activation, Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC), Phagocytosis | Hypoxia, ATP depletion, ROS production, Inflammatory mediators |
| **Flow Cytometry**   | Increased CD3+, CD8+ T cells, Granzyme B expression             | Increased B cells, Plasma cells (less common)                   | Not typically diagnostic for rejection                          |
| **C4d Deposition**   | Can be present, but less specific than in AMR                     | Hallmark finding in peritubular capillaries                     | Absent                                                          |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute cellular rejection is primarily mediated by T lymphocytes, particularly CD8+ cytotoxic T cells, which directly kill graft parenchymal cells expressing donor MHC class I. Distinguish ACR from AMR based on histology (lymphocyte infiltrate vs. endothelialitis/C4d), flow cytometry (T cells vs. B cells/plasma cells), and mechanism (CTL cytotoxicity vs. antibody/complement). Remember that C4d deposition can occur in both ACR and AMR, but its location (peritubular capillaries) is more characteristic of AMR.

### Question 102: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 02 (histocompatibility)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of MHC class I and class II molecules in T cell activation and graft rejection, specifically the difference in presentation pathways and the types of T cells involved. (理解 MHC I 类和 II 类分子在 T 细胞活化和移植物排斥中的作用，特别是呈递途径的差异以及参与的 T 细胞类型。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 55-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor. The donor's blood type is A+, and the recipient's blood type is O+. Both are HLA-A2 positive, but the donor is HLA-B27 positive, while the recipient is HLA-B7 positive. The recipient is also HLA-DR3 positive, while the donor is HLA-DR4 positive. The patient is started on standard immunosuppressive therapy including a calcineurin inhibitor and mycophenolate mofetil. Three weeks post-transplant, the patient develops fever, flank pain, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals acute tubular necrosis and interstitial inflammation with infiltration of mononuclear cells. Immunofluorescence staining shows deposition of C4d in the peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the peripheral blood shows a significant increase in CD8+ T cells expressing the activation marker CD69.

Which of the following mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for the acute rejection observed in this patient?

(A) Antibody-mediated rejection targeting donor HLA-DR molecules on endothelial cells.
(B) T cell-mediated rejection primarily involving CD4+ T cells recognizing donor HLA-B27 presented by recipient antigen-presenting cells.
(C) T cell-mediated rejection primarily involving CD8+ T cells recognizing donor HLA-A2 presented by recipient antigen-presenting cells.
(D) T cell-mediated rejection primarily involving CD8+ T cells recognizing donor HLA-B27 presented by recipient antigen-presenting cells.
(E) Antibody-mediated rejection targeting donor HLA-A2 molecules on podocytes.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 55-year-old male, end-stage renal disease (ESRD) from diabetic nephropathy, receives kidney transplant. This sets the stage for potential graft rejection.
- **HLA Mismatch:** Donor is A+, HLA-A2+, HLA-B27+, HLA-DR4+. Recipient is O+, HLA-A2+, HLA-B7+, HLA-DR3+. Key mismatches are HLA-B (B27 vs B7) and HLA-DR (DR4 vs DR3). HLA-A is matched. ABO compatibility is achieved (O recipient can receive A donor, though it's not ideal, it's possible with anti-A antibodies managed).
- **Timeline:** Rejection occurs 3 weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is typical for acute cellular rejection (T cell-mediated) or acute antibody-mediated rejection.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Fever, flank pain, elevated creatinine. These are classic signs of acute graft dysfunction, suggestive of rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Acute tubular necrosis (ATN) and interstitial inflammation with mononuclear cell infiltration. This points towards rejection, specifically cellular rejection. C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries indicates complement activation, often seen in antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), but can also be present in cellular rejection (especially if there's T cell help for antibody production or endothelial injury).
- **Immunological Findings:** Increased CD8+ T cells expressing CD69 in peripheral blood. CD69 is an early activation marker for T cells, strongly suggesting T cell activation is occurring. The increase in CD8+ cells points towards a cytotoxic T lymphocyte (CTL) response.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely mechanism of acute rejection.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Graft Rejection Types:**
    - **Hyperacute Rejection:** Occurs within minutes to hours, mediated by pre-formed antibodies (e.g., ABO incompatibility, anti-HLA antibodies from prior sensitization). Characterized by endothelial damage, thrombosis, and rapid graft loss. Not consistent with the 3-week timeframe.
    - **Acute Rejection:** Occurs days to weeks post-transplant. Can be T cell-mediated (cellular rejection) or antibody-mediated (AMR).
        - **T Cell-Mediated Rejection (TCMR):** Primarily involves T lymphocytes recognizing foreign MHC molecules on the graft.
            - **CD4+ T cells (Helper T cells):** Recognize foreign MHC class II molecules presented by recipient antigen-presenting cells (APCs) like dendritic cells, macrophages, and B cells. They release cytokines (IFN-γ, TNF-α) that activate macrophages and cytotoxic T cells, and can directly damage graft cells.
            - **CD8+ T cells (Cytotoxic T lymphocytes - CTLs):** Recognize foreign MHC class I molecules presented by graft cells (virtually all nucleated cells). They directly kill graft cells expressing the foreign MHC I.
        - **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** Mediated by antibodies (donor-specific antibodies - DSAs) binding to donor HLA antigens on endothelial cells. This activates complement (leading to C4d deposition) and recruits inflammatory cells (neutrophils, macrophages), causing endothelial damage, microvascular inflammation (MVI), and thrombosis.
    - **Chronic Rejection:** Occurs months to years post-transplant. Characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes (graft arteriosclerosis). Involves both cellular and humoral immunity.
- **MHC Presentation Pathways:**
    - **MHC Class I:** Presents endogenous antigens (e.g., viral proteins, self-proteins) to CD8+ T cells. Found on virtually all nucleated cells.
    - **MHC Class II:** Presents exogenous antigens (e.g., bacterial proteins taken up by APCs) to CD4+ T cells. Found primarily on professional APCs (dendritic cells, macrophages, B cells).
- **Applying to the Vignette:**
    - The 3-week timeframe points to acute rejection.
    - The biopsy shows mononuclear cell infiltration (typical of cellular rejection) and C4d deposition (can be seen in AMR, but also in TCMR).
    - The key finding is the increase in activated CD8+ T cells (CD69+). This strongly implicates a CD8+ T cell response.
    - The HLA mismatches are HLA-B (B27 vs B7) and HLA-DR (DR4 vs DR3).
    - CD8+ T cells recognize MHC class I. Therefore, the rejection is likely mediated by CD8+ T cells recognizing a foreign MHC class I molecule.
    - The mismatched HLA-B locus (B27 vs B7) represents a difference in MHC class I molecules.
    - The mismatched HLA-DR locus (DR4 vs DR3) represents a difference in MHC class II molecules, which would primarily activate CD4+ T cells.
    - Since the dominant finding is activated CD8+ T cells, the rejection is most likely driven by CD8+ T cells recognizing the mismatched donor HLA-B27 (MHC class I) presented by graft cells. Recipient APCs can also present donor MHC I peptides, but direct recognition by CTLs is the primary mechanism.

##### 3. Option Analysis
- **(A) Antibody-mediated rejection targeting donor HLA-DR molecules on endothelial cells.** While C4d deposition is present, the primary finding is activated CD8+ T cells. AMR typically involves DSAs against HLA antigens (like DR), leading to endothelial damage and complement activation. The dominant T cell response points away from this being the *primary* mechanism, although AMR and TCMR can coexist.
- **(B) T cell-mediated rejection primarily involving CD4+ T cells recognizing donor HLA-B27 presented by recipient antigen-presenting cells.** CD4+ T cells recognize MHC class II molecules. HLA-B27 is an MHC class I molecule. Therefore, CD4+ T cells would not directly recognize HLA-B27. While CD4+ T cells play a role in rejection (e.g., helping CD8+ T cells), the dominant finding of activated CD8+ cells makes this less likely as the *primary* mechanism.
- **(C) T cell-mediated rejection primarily involving CD8+ T cells recognizing donor HLA-A2 presented by recipient antigen-presenting cells.** HLA-A2 is matched between donor and recipient. Therefore, CD8+ T cells would not recognize donor HLA-A2 as foreign.
- **(D) T cell-mediated rejection primarily involving CD8+ T cells recognizing donor HLA-B27 presented by recipient antigen-presenting cells.** This aligns perfectly with the findings: acute rejection timeframe, biopsy findings consistent with cellular rejection, presence of activated CD8+ T cells, and a mismatch in MHC class I (HLA-B27 vs B7). CD8+ T cells recognize MHC class I, and HLA-B27 is the mismatched MHC class I allele.
- **(E) Antibody-mediated rejection targeting donor HLA-A2 molecules on podocytes.** HLA-A2 is matched. Podocytes are not the primary target in AMR (endothelial cells are). This option is incorrect on multiple counts.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- **(A):** This option describes antibody-mediated rejection (AMR). While C4d deposition can be seen in AMR, the prominent finding of activated CD8+ T cells points more strongly towards T cell-mediated rejection (TCMR). AMR is characterized by DSAs targeting HLA antigens on endothelial cells, leading to endothelial injury and complement activation. The vignette emphasizes the T cell response.
- **(B):** This option describes TCMR mediated by CD4+ T cells recognizing MHC class II. CD4+ T cells recognize MHC class II molecules. The mismatched HLA-DR (DR4 vs DR3) represents a difference in MHC class II, but the key immunological finding is the increase in activated CD8+ T cells, not CD4+ T cells. Therefore, this is not the *primary* mechanism described.
- **(C):** This option describes TCMR mediated by CD8+ T cells recognizing a *matched* HLA allele (HLA-A2). Since HLA-A2 is matched, the recipient's CD8+ T cells would not recognize it as foreign. The rejection must be due to the *mismatched* HLA alleles.
- **(D):** This option correctly identifies TCMR mediated by CD8+ T cells recognizing a *mismatched* MHC class I allele (HLA-B27). CD8+ T cells recognize MHC class I, and HLA-B27 is the mismatched MHC class I allele in this case. This aligns with the clinical presentation, biopsy findings (mononuclear infiltrate), and the key immunological finding of activated CD8+ T cells.
- **(E):** This option describes AMR targeting a *matched* HLA allele (HLA-A2) on podocytes. Podocytes are not the primary target cells in AMR (endothelial cells are). Furthermore, HLA-A2 is matched, so antibodies against it would not cause rejection in this context.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Correct Answer (D) - TCMR (CD8+) | Option A (AMR)                     | Option B (TCMR - CD4+) | Option C (TCMR - CD8+ Matched) | Option E (AMR - Matched) |
|----------------------|-----------------------------------|------------------------------------|------------------------|---------------------------------|--------------------------|
| **Rejection Type**   | T Cell-Mediated (Cellular)        | Antibody-Mediated                  | T Cell-Mediated        | T Cell-Mediated                 | Antibody-Mediated        |
| **Primary Cell Type**| CD8+ T cells (CTLs)               | Antibodies, Complement, Neutrophils | CD4+ T cells (Helpers) | CD8+ T cells                    | Antibodies               |
| **MHC Class**        | Class I (HLA-B27)                 | Class I/II (HLA-DR, -B, -A)        | Class II (HLA-DR)      | Class I (HLA-A2)                | Class I (HLA-A2)         |
| **Target Cell**      | Graft cells (expressing MHC I)    | Endothelial cells                 | APCs, Graft cells      | Graft cells                     | Podocytes                |
| **Key Finding**      | Activated CD8+ T cells (CD69+)    | C4d deposition, MVI                | CD4+ T cell activation | CD8+ T cell activation           | C4d deposition           |
| **HLA Match**        | HLA-B Mismatch (B27 vs B7)        | HLA Mismatch (e.g., DR)            | HLA-DR Mismatch        | HLA-A Match (A2 vs A2)           | HLA-A Match (A2 vs A2)    |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute graft rejection can be T cell-mediated (TCMR) or antibody-mediated (AMR). TCMR involves CD4+ and/or CD8+ T cells recognizing foreign MHC molecules. CD8+ T cells recognize MHC class I, while CD4+ T cells recognize MHC class II. The clinical presentation (fever, flank pain, elevated creatinine), biopsy findings (mononuclear infiltrate, ATN), and immunological findings (activated CD8+ T cells) strongly suggest TCMR mediated by CD8+ T cells recognizing mismatched MHC class I molecules on the graft. Key differentials include AMR (characterized by DSAs, C4d deposition, endothelial injury) and hyperacute rejection (pre-formed antibodies, immediate graft loss).

<a id='question-103'></a>

### Question 103: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 03 (differ)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Differentiating between acute cellular rejection, acute antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), and chronic rejection in organ transplantation based on clinical presentation, histopathology, and immunological markers. (区分器官移植中的急性细胞性排斥、急性抗体介导性排斥和慢性排斥的临床表现、组织病理学和免疫学标志物。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male received a kidney transplant 6 months ago for end-stage renal disease secondary to diabetic nephropathy. He is currently on immunosuppressive therapy consisting of tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Over the past 2 weeks, he has experienced a gradual decline in urine output, increasing serum creatinine from 1.2 mg/dL to 3.5 mg/dL, and new onset hypertension (160/100 mmHg). Physical examination reveals mild lower extremity edema. A renal biopsy is performed. Histopathology shows diffuse interstitial edema, mild tubular atrophy, and focal glomerular ischemia. Immunofluorescence microscopy reveals C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a decrease in CD4+ T cells and an increase in CD19+ B cells compared to baseline pre-transplant levels. Serum complement levels (C3, C4) are normal. Which of the following is the most likely underlying immunological mechanism contributing to this patient's graft dysfunction?

(A) T-cell mediated attack on tubular epithelial cells, characterized by lymphocytic infiltration and tubular basement membrane disruption.
(B) Pre-formed donor-specific antibodies (DSA) binding to HLA antigens on endothelial cells, activating complement and leading to endothelial injury and microvascular inflammation.
(C) Chronic inflammation and fibrosis resulting from long-term low-grade immune responses and recurrent episodes of acute rejection.
(D) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells targeting graft cells coated with donor-specific antibodies.
(E) Complement-independent antibody-mediated injury involving non-complement-activating antibodies binding to non-HLA antigens on graft cells.

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, 6 months post-kidney transplant for diabetic nephropathy, on standard immunosuppression (tacrolimus, mycophenolate, prednisone). This sets the stage for potential rejection or complications.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Gradual decline in urine output, rising creatinine (acute graft dysfunction), new hypertension, mild edema. These symptoms point towards acute kidney injury affecting the transplanted kidney. The timeframe (6 months post-transplant) is typical for acute rejection episodes.
- **Biopsy Findings:**
    - **Histopathology:** Diffuse interstitial edema, mild tubular atrophy, focal glomerular ischemia. These findings are non-specific but suggest acute injury.
    - **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. This is a key finding strongly suggestive of antibody-mediated rejection (AMR). C4d is a stable breakdown product of the classical complement pathway activation. Its presence indicates complement activation within the graft microvasculature.
    - **Flow Cytometry:** Decrease in CD4+ T cells (expected due to immunosuppression) and increase in CD19+ B cells (can be seen in AMR, as B cells produce antibodies).
    - **Serum Complement:** Normal C3 and C4 levels. This indicates that systemic complement consumption is not occurring, which is typical for localized complement activation within the graft in AMR, rather than a systemic process like lupus nephritis.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the most likely *underlying immunological mechanism*.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Acute Rejection Types:**
    - **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** Primarily mediated by T cells (CD4+ and CD8+). Histology shows lymphocytic infiltrates, often targeting tubules (tubulitis) or glomeruli. T cells directly kill graft cells or release cytokines causing damage. C4d is typically negative. Option (A) describes features consistent with ACR (T-cell attack on tubules).
    - **Acute Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** Mediated by donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) directed against HLA or non-HLA antigens on the graft endothelium. DSAs activate complement (classical pathway) and/or bind to Fc receptors on effector cells (like NK cells via ADCC). Complement activation leads to endothelial injury, inflammation, and microvascular damage (glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis). Histology shows microvascular inflammation (neutrophils, monocytes), endothelialitis, and sometimes C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Option (B) describes the mechanism of AMR involving DSAs, complement activation, and endothelial injury. Option (D) describes ADCC, which is *one* mechanism of AMR, but the presence of C4d strongly points towards complement-mediated AMR as the primary driver.
    - **Chronic Rejection:** Occurs months to years post-transplant. Characterized by progressive fibrosis, vascular thickening (intimal arteritis), and tubular atrophy. It's a complex process involving both cellular and humoral immunity, as well as non-immune factors. Option (C) describes chronic rejection.
    - **Complement-Independent AMR:** Involves DSAs that do not activate complement but still cause injury, possibly through Fc receptor binding or direct signaling. Option (E) describes this mechanism. While possible, the presence of C4d deposition makes complement-dependent AMR (Option B) much more likely.

- **C4d Deposition:** C4d is a highly specific marker for classical complement pathway activation within the graft microvasculature. Its presence in peritubular capillaries is a hallmark diagnostic feature of acute antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) according to the Banff criteria.

- **Linking Clues:** The patient's acute graft dysfunction (rising creatinine, decreased urine output), hypertension, and especially the renal biopsy finding of C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries strongly point towards acute antibody-mediated rejection (AMR). The mechanism involves DSAs activating complement, leading to endothelial injury and microvascular inflammation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Describes acute cellular rejection (ACR). While ACR is common, the key finding of C4d deposition rules it out as the primary mechanism. ACR involves T-cell mediated damage, typically without C4d.
- (C): Describes chronic rejection. This occurs over a longer timeframe (months to years) and is characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes, not the acute presentation and C4d deposition seen here.
- (D): Describes antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC), a mechanism of AMR. While ADCC can occur in AMR, the presence of C4d deposition specifically indicates complement activation, making complement-mediated AMR (Option B) the more direct and likely mechanism highlighted by the biopsy findings. ADCC often occurs alongside complement activation in AMR.
- (E): Describes complement-independent AMR. This is less common than complement-dependent AMR, and the presence of C4d deposition directly contradicts this mechanism.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR) | Acute Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) | Chronic Rejection | ADCC in AMR | Complement-Independent AMR |
|----------------------|---------------------------------|------------------------------------------|-------------------|-------------|-----------------------------|
| **Primary Mediator** | T cells                         | Donor-Specific Antibodies (DSAs)          | Both T & B cells | NK cells   | DSAs                        |
| **Key Histology**   | Tubulitis, lymphocytic infiltrate | Microvascular inflammation, Endothelialitis | Fibrosis, Arteritis | N/A         | N/A                         |
| **Key Immunofluorescence** | Negative C4d                   | **Positive C4d in peritubular capillaries** | Variable         | N/A         | Negative C4d                |
| **Mechanism**       | Direct cell killing, Cytokines   | Complement activation, ADCC, Endothelial injury | Chronic inflammation, Fibrosis | NK cell lysis | Endothelial injury via Fc receptors |
| **Timeframe**       | Days to weeks                   | Days to weeks                            | Months to years   | Days-weeks  | Days-weeks                  |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The presence of C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries on renal biopsy is a hallmark finding of acute antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) in kidney transplantation, indicating complement activation by donor-specific antibodies. Differentiating between acute cellular rejection (no C4d, T-cell infiltrates) and acute AMR (C4d+, microvascular inflammation) is crucial for appropriate management. Chronic rejection presents later with fibrosis. ADCC and complement-independent AMR are less common mechanisms compared to complement-dependent AMR indicated by C4d.

<a id='question-104'></a>

### Question 104: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 04 (ences.)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying acute cellular rejection in solid organ transplantation, specifically the role of T lymphocytes (CD8+ cytotoxic T cells) and their recognition of alloantigens presented by MHC class I molecules on graft cells. (理解实体器官移植中急性细胞性排斥的免疫机制，特别是T淋巴细胞（CD8+细胞毒性T细胞）的作用以及它们对移植细胞表面MHC I类分子呈递的异体抗原的识别。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with one HLA mismatch at the A locus. Post-transplant, he is initiated on standard immunosuppression with tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks after transplantation, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals interstitial edema, inflammatory cell infiltrates primarily composed of lymphocytes, and evidence of tubular injury. Immunofluorescence staining shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the biopsy sample identifies a significant increase in CD8+ T cells infiltrating the graft tubules and interstitium, many of which are expressing Fas ligand (FasL). The patient's serum creatinine continues to rise despite dose adjustments of immunosuppressive medications.

Which of the following cellular mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for the observed graft injury in this patient?

(A) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells recognizing graft cells coated with donor-specific antibodies.
(B) Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) mediated by antibodies binding to donor MHC class II molecules on graft endothelial cells.
(C) Direct cytotoxicity mediated by recipient CD8+ T cells recognizing donor MHC class I molecules presenting alloantigens on graft cells.
(D) Antibody-mediated rejection involving the formation of immune complexes that deposit in the glomeruli, leading to glomerulonephritis.
(E) Macrophage-mediated inflammation driven by recipient CD4+ T helper cells secreting IFN-γ, leading to fibrosis.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for potential transplant rejection.
- **Immunosuppression:** Standard triple therapy (tacrolimus, mycophenolate, prednisone) is used, but rejection still occurs, suggesting either inadequate suppression or a particularly strong immune response.
- **Timeline:** Rejection occurs 3 weeks post-transplant, typical for acute rejection.
- **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine are classic signs of acute graft dysfunction, often due to rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Interstitial edema, lymphocyte infiltrates (especially CD8+ T cells), tubular injury, and C4d deposition are hallmarks of acute rejection. C4d deposition indicates complement activation, often associated with antibody-mediated rejection, but can also be seen in T-cell mediated rejection involving antibody production against donor HLA.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD8+ T cells infiltrating the graft, expressing FasL, is a key finding. CD8+ T cells are cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs). FasL is a death receptor ligand expressed by CTLs that binds to Fas (CD95) on target cells, inducing apoptosis.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely cellular mechanism responsible for graft injury.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Acute Cellular Rejection:** This is the most common type of acute rejection in solid organ transplants, primarily mediated by recipient T lymphocytes recognizing donor alloantigens.
- **T Cell Recognition:**
    - **Direct Pathway:** Recipient T cells directly recognize intact donor MHC molecules (MHC class I and MHC class II) on donor antigen-presenting cells (APCs) within the graft. This is the predominant pathway for acute cellular rejection. CD8+ T cells recognize donor MHC class I presenting alloantigens, leading to direct killing of graft cells. CD4+ T cells recognize donor MHC class II, leading to cytokine production (like IFN-γ) that activates macrophages and recruits other inflammatory cells.
    - **Indirect Pathway:** Recipient APCs process donor MHC molecules (shed from graft cells) and present peptides derived from them on recipient MHC class II molecules to recipient CD4+ T cells. This pathway is more important in chronic rejection and sensitization.
- **CD8+ T Cell Cytotoxicity:** In this case, the biopsy shows a significant increase in CD8+ T cells infiltrating the graft tubules and interstitium. These CD8+ T cells recognize donor MHC class I molecules presenting alloantigens on the surface of graft cells (tubular epithelial cells, endothelial cells). Upon recognition, CD8+ T cells release cytotoxic granules (containing perforin and granzymes) or express FasL, both leading to apoptosis of the target graft cells. This direct killing by CD8+ T cells is a major mechanism of acute cellular rejection.
- **FasL:** The expression of FasL by the infiltrating CD8+ T cells confirms their cytotoxic potential and mechanism of action via the extrinsic apoptosis pathway.
- **C4d Deposition:** While often associated with antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), C4d deposition can also occur in acute cellular rejection (ACR), particularly when T cells produce antibodies against donor HLA or when complement is activated secondarily to inflammation. Its presence doesn't exclude ACR, especially given the strong evidence for CD8+ T cell involvement.
- **Option Analysis:**
    - (A) ADCC involves NK cells or other cytotoxic cells binding to antibody-coated target cells via Fc receptors. While antibodies might be present, the primary mechanism highlighted by the CD8+ T cell infiltrate and FasL expression is direct T cell cytotoxicity, not ADCC.
    - (B) CDC involves antibodies binding to target cells (like endothelial cells) and activating complement, leading to cell lysis. This is a mechanism of AMR, not the primary mechanism described here.
    - (C) This option accurately describes the direct cytotoxicity mediated by recipient CD8+ T cells recognizing donor MHC class I molecules on graft cells, consistent with the biopsy findings (CD8+ infiltrate, FasL expression) and the typical mechanism of acute cellular rejection.
    - (D) This describes a form of AMR, specifically glomerulonephritis caused by immune complex deposition. The biopsy findings (tubular injury, interstitial infiltrate) are more typical of ACR, although glomerular involvement can occur in AMR.
    - (E) While CD4+ T cells (not the focus here) and macrophages play a role in ACR, the primary cytotoxic mechanism highlighted by the CD8+ T cell infiltrate and FasL expression is direct killing, not primarily macrophage-mediated inflammation leading to fibrosis (which is more characteristic of chronic rejection).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) is mediated by effector cells (like NK cells) binding to target cells coated with antibodies via Fc receptors. While antibodies can be involved in rejection, the vignette emphasizes the CD8+ T cell infiltrate and FasL expression, pointing towards direct T cell cytotoxicity, not ADCC as the *primary* mechanism. ADCC is more characteristic of antibody-mediated rejection.
- (B): Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) is a mechanism of antibody-mediated rejection where antibodies bind to target cells (often endothelial cells expressing MHC class II) and activate the classical complement pathway, leading to cell lysis. The vignette highlights CD8+ T cell infiltration and FasL, characteristic of cellular rejection, not primarily CDC.
- (D): Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) can manifest as glomerulonephritis due to immune complex deposition or as endothelial injury (e.g., C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries). While C4d was present, the dominant finding is the CD8+ T cell infiltrate and FasL expression, strongly suggesting cellular rejection as the primary driver of injury. Glomerulonephritis is a specific manifestation of AMR, not the general mechanism described.
- (E): Macrophage-mediated inflammation is part of the inflammatory response in ACR, often driven by CD4+ T cells secreting IFN-γ. However, the direct cytotoxic killing by CD8+ T cells expressing FasL is a more specific and potent mechanism causing the observed tubular injury, as highlighted by the biopsy findings. Fibrosis is more characteristic of chronic rejection.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR)                                  | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR)                                      |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Mediator**     | T lymphocytes (CD8+ cytotoxic T cells, CD4+ helper T cells)     | Antibodies (donor-specific antibodies - DSA)                             |
| **Target Cells**         | Graft parenchymal cells (tubules, endothelium), APCs             | Endothelial cells (vasculitis), Parenchymal cells (less common)         |
| **Key Histopathology**  | Infiltrate of lymphocytes (CD8+ > CD4+), tubular injury, edema | C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries, endothelialitis, vasculitis |
| **Mechanism of Injury** | Direct cytotoxicity (FasL, perforin/granzymes), cytokine release | CDC, ADCC, complement activation, inflammation                          |
| **Typical Timing**       | Days to weeks post-transplant                                  | Can occur early or late post-transplant                                 |
| **Biopsy Markers**       | CD8+ T cell infiltrate, FasL expression                       | C4d deposition, DSA presence                                           |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute cellular rejection is primarily mediated by recipient T cells recognizing donor alloantigens. CD8+ cytotoxic T cells directly kill graft cells via FasL/Fas interaction or perforin/granzyme release. Differentiating ACR from AMR is crucial, as treatments differ. Key features of ACR include lymphocytic infiltrate (especially CD8+) and tubular injury, while AMR features include C4d deposition and endothelialitis.

<a id='question-105'></a>

### Question 105: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 05 (effector)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the cellular and molecular mechanisms of acute cellular rejection in organ transplantation, specifically the role of cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) and their effector functions, including perforin/granzyme release and Fas/FasL interaction. (理解器官移植中急性细胞性排斥的细胞和分子机制，特别是细胞毒性 T 淋巴细胞 (CTL) 的作用及其效应功能，包括穿孔素/颗粒酶释放和 Fas/FasL 相互作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an unknown HLA type. He is started on standard immunosuppression with tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals diffuse interstitial inflammation with lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular epithelial cell damage, and evidence of endothelialitis. Immunofluorescence staining shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the peripheral blood shows a significant increase in CD8+ T cells expressing granzyme B. Further investigation reveals that these CD8+ T cells also express high levels of Fas ligand (FasL). The patient's immunosuppression is intensified, and his renal function gradually improves.

Which of the following mechanisms is MOST directly responsible for the tubular epithelial cell damage observed in this patient's kidney biopsy?

(A) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells recognizing antibody-coated tubular cells.
(B) Complement-mediated lysis of tubular cells due to deposition of C3b and formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC).
(C) Perforin and granzyme B release by CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) inducing apoptosis in target tubular cells.
(D) Fas ligand (FasL) expressed on activated CD8+ T cells binding to Fas receptors on tubular epithelial cells, triggering apoptosis.
(E) Release of cytokines like TNF-α and IFN-γ by CD4+ helper T cells, directly causing tubular cell necrosis.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for potential transplant rejection.
- **Timeline:** 3 weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is characteristic of acute rejection, particularly acute cellular rejection (ACR).
- **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine. These are classic signs of acute kidney graft dysfunction, often due to rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Diffuse interstitial inflammation, lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular epithelial cell damage, endothelialitis. These findings are highly suggestive of acute cellular rejection.
- **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. While C4d deposition is classically associated with antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), it can sometimes be seen in ACR, particularly in cases with mixed rejection patterns or underlying antibody sensitization. However, the predominant histological picture points towards ACR.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD8+ T cells expressing granzyme B. This confirms the presence of activated cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs), the primary effector cells in ACR.
- **FasL Expression:** CD8+ T cells also express high levels of FasL. This indicates another potential mechanism for CTL-mediated cell killing.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism MOST directly responsible for tubular epithelial cell damage. This requires identifying the primary effector mechanism of CTLs in ACR.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** Primarily mediated by T lymphocytes (especially CD8+ CTLs and CD4+ helper T cells) recognizing donor MHC molecules as foreign.
- **CD8+ CTL Effector Functions:** CTLs kill target cells (like graft tubular epithelial cells) through two main pathways:
    1.  **Granule Exocytosis Pathway:** CTLs release pre-formed cytotoxic granules containing perforin and granzymes (e.g., granzyme B). Perforin polymerizes to form pores in the target cell membrane, allowing granzymes to enter. Granzymes are serine proteases that activate caspases within the target cell, initiating the apoptotic cascade. This is a major mechanism of CTL-mediated killing.
    2.  **Fas/FasL Pathway:** CTLs express Fas ligand (FasL) on their surface. FasL binds to the Fas receptor (also known as CD95 or APO-1) on target cells. This interaction triggers the formation of the death-inducing signaling complex (DISC) within the target cell, leading to caspase activation and apoptosis.
- **Role of CD4+ T cells:** CD4+ helper T cells (Th1 type) secrete cytokines like IFN-γ and TNF-α, which activate macrophages and contribute to inflammation and tissue damage. They can also provide help to CD8+ T cells.
- **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** Mediated by donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) binding to donor HLA antigens on endothelial cells. This leads to complement activation (C4d deposition), endothelial injury, and inflammation. ADCC can occur, but it's typically mediated by NK cells or macrophages recognizing antibody-coated cells, not primarily tubular cells in ACR.
- **The vignette specifically highlights increased CD8+ T cells expressing granzyme B and FasL.** Both mechanisms (granzyme B/perforin and FasL/Fas) are employed by CTLs to kill target cells. However, the granule exocytosis pathway involving perforin and granzymes is considered the predominant and most direct mechanism for CTL-mediated killing of parenchymal cells (like tubular epithelial cells) in ACR. While FasL/Fas interaction also occurs, the granzyme B pathway is often emphasized as the primary cytotoxic effector mechanism in this context.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells recognizing antibody-coated tubular cells.** This mechanism is characteristic of antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), where antibodies bind to endothelial cells (primarily), activating NK cells or macrophages. While C4d deposition is present (suggesting some antibody involvement), the primary histological findings (lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular damage) and the prominent role of activated CD8+ T cells point towards ACR, not AMR. ADCC typically targets endothelial cells in AMR, not primarily tubular epithelial cells in ACR.
- (B) **Complement-mediated lysis of tubular cells due to deposition of C3b and formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC).** Complement activation is the hallmark of AMR, leading to endothelial damage. While C4d deposition is seen, direct complement-mediated lysis of tubular epithelial cells is not the primary mechanism of damage in typical ACR. The main target of complement in AMR is the endothelium.
- (D) **Fas ligand (FasL) expressed on activated CD8+ T cells binding to Fas receptors on tubular epithelial cells, triggering apoptosis.** This is a valid mechanism by which CTLs can kill target cells. The vignette mentions high FasL expression on the CD8+ T cells. However, the granule exocytosis pathway (perforin/granzyme) is generally considered the more prominent and direct mechanism for CTL-mediated killing of parenchymal cells in ACR. Option (C) describes the more classically emphasized primary pathway.
- (E) **Release of cytokines like TNF-α and IFN-γ by CD4+ helper T cells, directly causing tubular cell necrosis.** CD4+ T cells contribute to ACR by secreting cytokines that promote inflammation and activate other immune cells (including CTLs). However, they do not directly kill tubular cells via cytokine release; their role is primarily immunomodulatory and inflammatory. Direct killing of tubular cells is mainly mediated by CD8+ CTLs.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR)                                  | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR)                               |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Mediators**   | T lymphocytes (CD8+ CTLs, CD4+ Th1)                             | Donor-Specific Antibodies (DSAs)                               |
| **Target Cells**        | Parenchymal cells (e.g., tubular epithelial cells), endothelium | Endothelial cells                                              |
| **Key Histology**       | Lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular damage, endothelialitis         | Endothelialitis, C4d deposition, thrombosis, glomerululitis     |
| **CTL Effector Mech.**  | Perforin/Granzyme B release (primary), FasL/Fas interaction     | N/A (CTLs involved in ACR, not AMR)                             |
| **Immune Markers**      | Increased CD8+ T cells, Granzyme B expression                  | DSAs, C4d deposition                                           |
| **ADCC Role**           | Minimal/Absent                                                  | Possible, but primarily targets endothelium                     |
| **Complement Role**     | Minimal/Absent (unless mixed rejection)                        | Central (C4d deposition, MAC formation)                         |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute cellular rejection is primarily mediated by cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) killing graft parenchymal cells. The main effector mechanisms are perforin/granzyme B release inducing apoptosis and FasL/Fas interaction. Differentiate ACR from antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), which involves DSAs, complement activation (C4d), and targets endothelial cells. Remember that CD8+ T cells are the key effector cells in ACR, utilizing granule exocytosis as a primary killing mechanism.

<a id='question-106'></a>

### Question 106: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 06 (rejection)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Differentiate between acute cellular rejection (T-cell mediated) and acute antibody-mediated rejection (humoral) in organ transplantation based on histological findings and immunological mechanisms. (区分器官移植中的急性细胞性排斥反应（T细胞介导）和急性抗体介导的排斥反应（体液性）的组织学发现和免疫学机制。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor. His pre-transplant panel reactive antibody (PRA) score was 10%. He is started on standard immunosuppression consisting of tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops acute onset graft dysfunction with rising serum creatinine (from a baseline of 1.2 mg/dL to 3.5 mg/dL) and decreased urine output. A renal biopsy is performed. Light microscopy reveals diffuse interstitial edema, moderate tubular injury, and infiltration of the interstitium and tubules by mononuclear cells, predominantly lymphocytes and macrophages. Immunofluorescence microscopy shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries, but no evidence of donor-specific antibodies (DSA) binding to the endothelium. Electron microscopy shows subendothelial electron-dense deposits. The patient's tacrolimus level is found to be within the therapeutic range.

Which of the following is the most likely predominant immunological mechanism responsible for this patient's graft dysfunction?

(A) Complement-dependent cytotoxicity mediated by pre-formed donor-specific antibodies (DSA) binding to HLA class I antigens on podocytes.
(B) T-cell mediated cytotoxicity against tubular epithelial cells expressing MHC class I antigens, leading to direct cell lysis.
(C) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells recognizing antibody-coated endothelial cells.
(D) Neutrophil-mediated injury triggered by deposition of immune complexes containing C5a in the glomeruli.
(E) Macrophage activation and cytokine release induced by T-cell recognition of alloantigens presented on MHC class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells in the graft.

#### Correct Answer: E

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, ESRD due to diabetic nephropathy, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for potential graft rejection.
- **Immunosuppression:** Standard regimen (tacrolimus, MMF, prednisone). This indicates an attempt to prevent rejection, but rejection can still occur.
- **Timing:** 3 weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is typical for acute rejection episodes.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Acute graft dysfunction (rising creatinine, decreased urine output). This is a hallmark sign of rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings (Light Microscopy):** Interstitial edema, tubular injury, mononuclear cell infiltration (lymphocytes, macrophages). These findings are characteristic of acute rejection, but need further differentiation.
- **Biopsy Findings (Immunofluorescence):** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. C4d is a marker of complement activation, often seen in antibody-mediated rejection (AMR). However, the absence of DSA binding to endothelium makes classic AMR less likely.
- **Biopsy Findings (Electron Microscopy):** Subendothelial electron-dense deposits. These deposits can be seen in both AMR and some forms of cellular rejection, often associated with immune complex deposition or complement activation.
- **Tacrolimus Level:** Therapeutic range. This rules out subtherapeutic immunosuppression as the primary cause of rejection.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *predominant* immunological mechanism.

The combination of mononuclear cell infiltration (lymphocytes, macrophages), interstitial edema, tubular injury, C4d deposition, and subendothelial deposits occurring 3 weeks post-transplant, despite therapeutic tacrolimus levels, points towards a mixed picture but strongly suggests a significant component of acute cellular rejection (ACR). The C4d deposition and subendothelial deposits might indicate some degree of antibody-mediated injury or complement activation, but the prominent mononuclear cell infiltrate is the classic feature of ACR. The absence of DSA binding to endothelium argues against classic AMR as the *predominant* mechanism.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** Primarily mediated by T lymphocytes (CD4+ helper T cells and CD8+ cytotoxic T cells) recognizing alloantigens (MHC class I and II molecules) presented by graft cells or antigen-presenting cells (APCs) within the graft.
    - **Mechanism:**
        1.  **Sensitization:** Recipient T cells recognize donor alloantigens presented by APCs (e.g., dendritic cells) in the graft or draining lymph nodes.
        2.  **Activation:** T cells become activated, proliferate, and differentiate into effector cells.
        3.  **Effector Phase:**
            -   **CD8+ T cells:** Directly kill graft cells (e.g., tubular epithelial cells) expressing donor MHC class I via perforin/granzyme pathways or Fas/FasL interactions.
            -   **CD4+ T cells:** Secrete cytokines (e.g., IFN-γ, TNF-α) that activate macrophages, promote inflammation, and recruit more immune cells. Activated macrophages contribute to tissue damage.
    - **Histology:** Infiltration of lymphocytes and macrophages into the interstitium and tubules, tubular injury, endothelialitis (inflammation of blood vessels).
- **Acute Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** Mediated by antibodies (primarily DSA) directed against donor HLA antigens, especially HLA class I and II on endothelial cells.
    - **Mechanism:**
        1.  **Antibody Binding:** DSA bind to HLA antigens on graft endothelial cells.
        2.  **Complement Activation:** Binding triggers the classical complement pathway, leading to C3 and C5 convertase formation, C4d deposition, and potentially membrane attack complex (MAC) formation.
        3.  **Cellular Effector Mechanisms:**
            -   **Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC):** MAC formation leads to cell lysis.
            -   **Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC):** NK cells or other effector cells bind to the Fc portion of antibodies coating endothelial cells, leading to target cell lysis.
            -   **Endothelial Activation:** Antibodies and complement activation cause endothelial cell activation, leading to inflammation, thrombosis, and ischemia.
    - **Histology:** Endothelialitis (inflammation of blood vessels), C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries, thrombosis, glomerular injury.
- **Mixed Rejection:** Often, rejection episodes involve both cellular and humoral components.

In this case, the prominent mononuclear cell infiltrate (lymphocytes, macrophages) is the hallmark of ACR. While C4d deposition and subendothelial deposits are present (suggesting some complement activation or antibody involvement), the absence of DSA binding to endothelium makes classic AMR less likely as the *predominant* mechanism. The overall picture is most consistent with ACR, where T cells recognize alloantigens presented on MHC class II molecules on APCs (like macrophages and dendritic cells) within the graft, leading to macrophage activation and cytokine release, contributing to the interstitial inflammation and tubular injury.

##### 3. Option Analysis (选项分析)
- **(A) Complement-dependent cytotoxicity mediated by pre-formed donor-specific antibodies (DSA) binding to HLA class I antigens on podocytes.** Incorrect. While AMR involves DSA and complement, the vignette states *no evidence of DSA binding to the endothelium* on immunofluorescence. Podocytes are less commonly the primary target in AMR compared to endothelial cells. The predominant finding is mononuclear cell infiltration, characteristic of ACR.
- **(B) T-cell mediated cytotoxicity against tubular epithelial cells expressing MHC class I antigens, leading to direct cell lysis.** Incorrect. This describes a component of ACR (CD8+ T cell cytotoxicity), but it doesn't fully encompass the inflammatory picture with significant macrophage infiltration and cytokine release, which is also a major part of ACR mediated by CD4+ T cells activating macrophages. Option E provides a more comprehensive description of the overall process.
- **(C) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells recognizing antibody-coated endothelial cells.** Incorrect. This is a mechanism of AMR. The vignette lacks strong evidence for classic AMR (no DSA binding to endothelium).
- **(D) Neutrophil-mediated injury triggered by deposition of immune complexes containing C5a in the glomeruli.** Incorrect. This describes a mechanism more typical of certain types of glomerulonephritis or hyperacute rejection (though hyperacute rejection is usually mediated by pre-existing antibodies against ABO or HLA antigens and occurs within minutes to hours). The biopsy findings (mononuclear infiltrate, interstitial edema, tubular injury) are not typical for neutrophil-mediated glomerular injury.
- **(E) Macrophage activation and cytokine release induced by T-cell recognition of alloantigens presented on MHC class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells in the graft.** Correct. This accurately describes a key component of acute cellular rejection. CD4+ T cells recognize donor MHC class II molecules on APCs (like macrophages and dendritic cells) within the graft. This interaction leads to T cell activation, cytokine production (e.g., IFN-γ), and activation of macrophages. Activated macrophages release inflammatory mediators and contribute significantly to the tissue damage seen in ACR (interstitial inflammation, tubular injury). This aligns well with the histological findings of mononuclear cell infiltration.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- **(A):** This describes a mechanism of antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), specifically CDC targeting endothelial cells. The key differentiator is the lack of DSA binding to endothelium in the biopsy, making classic AMR less likely as the *predominant* mechanism. While C4d deposition is present, it can occur in ACR as well, often due to complement activation by immune complexes or cellular debris.
- **(B):** This describes direct CD8+ T cell cytotoxicity, which is *part* of ACR. However, ACR is a broader process involving CD4+ T cells activating macrophages and releasing cytokines, which also contribute significantly to the observed pathology (mononuclear cell infiltrate, interstitial edema, tubular injury). Option E provides a more complete picture of the cellular rejection process.
- **(C):** This describes ADCC, another mechanism of AMR. Similar to option A, the lack of evidence for DSA binding to endothelium makes this mechanism less likely to be the *predominant* cause.
- **(D):** This mechanism is associated with immune complex deposition and complement activation, often in the glomeruli, leading to neutrophil recruitment. The biopsy findings in the vignette (predominantly interstitial and tubular changes with mononuclear cell infiltrate) are not characteristic of this process.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR)                                  | Acute Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR)                               |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Mediators**   | T lymphocytes (CD4+, CD8+)                                     | Antibodies (DSA), Complement                                         |
| **Target Cells**        | Tubular epithelial cells, endothelial cells, interstitial cells | Primarily endothelial cells                                          |
| **Key Mechanisms**      | T-cell cytotoxicity, cytokine release, macrophage activation     | Complement activation (CDC), ADCC, endothelial activation/injury     |
| **Histology**           | Mononuclear cell infiltrate (lymphocytes, macrophages), interstitial edema, tubular injury, endothelialitis | Endothelialitis, C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries, thrombosis |
| **Immunofluorescence**  | May show C4d, but DSA binding usually absent                   | DSA binding to endothelium, C4d deposition                          |
| **Electron Microscopy** | May show electron-dense deposits, but less specific            | Subendothelial electron-dense deposits                               |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute rejection in transplantation can be broadly classified as cellular (T-cell mediated) or antibody-mediated. Cellular rejection is characterized by T-cell infiltration and cytokine-mediated inflammation, while antibody-mediated rejection involves DSA binding to endothelial cells, activating complement and effector cells. Histological features like mononuclear cell infiltrate favor ACR, while endothelialitis and C4d deposition strongly suggest AMR. Mixed rejection episodes are common. Differentiating between these types is crucial for guiding treatment.

<a id='question-107'></a>

### Question 107: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 07 (cytokines)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of specific cytokines (particularly IL-2) in T cell proliferation and function, and how their dysregulation or deficiency can lead to impaired cellular immunity, especially in the context of transplantation and opportunistic infections. (理解特定细胞因子（特别是IL-2）在T细胞增殖和功能中的作用，以及其失调或缺乏如何导致细胞免疫受损，特别是在移植和机会性感染的背景下。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male, 6 months post-liver transplantation for primary biliary cholangitis, presents with persistent, diffuse, non-productive cough, exertional dyspnea, and fatigue for 3 weeks. He is on standard immunosuppression with tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. His immunosuppression regimen has been stable. Physical examination reveals bibasilar crackles. Chest X-ray shows diffuse bilateral interstitial infiltrates. Bronchoalveolar lavage (BAL) fluid analysis reveals numerous Pneumocystis jirovecii cysts (Wright-Giemsa stain) and a markedly decreased CD4+ T cell count (150 cells/µL) and CD8+ T cell count (200 cells/µL) in the BAL fluid. Flow cytometry on peripheral blood lymphocytes shows normal B cell and NK cell counts, but a significantly reduced percentage of CD4+ T cells expressing the high-affinity IL-2 receptor (CD25). Serum IL-2 levels are also found to be markedly decreased. The patient's tacrolimus level is within the therapeutic range.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying immunological defect contributing to this patient's susceptibility to Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia (PJP)?

(A) Defective production of interferon-gamma (IFN-γ) by NK cells.
(B) Impaired antigen presentation by dendritic cells due to defective MHC class II expression.
(C) Defective IL-2 production by activated T cells.
(D) Defective IL-2 receptor signaling pathway due to mutations in JAK kinases.
(E) Defective complement activation leading to impaired opsonization of Pneumocystis jirovecii.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old male, 6 months post-liver transplant, on standard immunosuppression (tacrolimus, mycophenolate, prednisone). This context immediately points towards transplant-related complications, including opportunistic infections due to immunosuppression.
- **Symptoms:** Persistent cough, dyspnea, fatigue, bibasilar crackles, diffuse interstitial infiltrates on CXR. These are classic signs and symptoms of Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia (PJP), a common opportunistic infection in immunocompromised individuals, especially transplant recipients.
- **Diagnostic Findings:** BAL fluid shows P. jirovecii cysts, confirming PJP. BAL fluid shows markedly decreased CD4+ and CD8+ T cell counts. This indicates a severe defect in cellular immunity, which is crucial for controlling PJP.
- **Immunological Findings:** Peripheral blood flow cytometry shows a reduced percentage of CD4+ T cells expressing CD25 (high-affinity IL-2 receptor). Serum IL-2 levels are markedly decreased. This is the most critical clue. CD25 is the alpha chain of the IL-2 receptor. Its expression on activated T cells is essential for high-affinity binding of IL-2, which is a potent T cell growth factor. Decreased CD25 expression and low serum IL-2 levels strongly suggest a defect in the IL-2 pathway, specifically related to IL-2 production or signaling.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *underlying immunological defect* contributing to susceptibility to PJP. Given the findings of low CD4+ T cells, low IL-2, and reduced CD25 expression, the defect likely involves the IL-2 pathway.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Pneumocystis jirovecii Pneumonia (PJP):** Caused by an atypical fungus. Primarily affects immunocompromised individuals, especially those with impaired T cell immunity (low CD4+ count). The host immune response relies heavily on CD4+ T helper cells (Th1 subset) producing IFN-γ, which activates macrophages to kill the organism. CD8+ T cells also contribute.
- **IL-2 Pathway:** IL-2 is a crucial cytokine for T cell proliferation, differentiation, and survival. It is primarily produced by activated CD4+ T cells (Th1 cells) and binds to its receptor (IL-2R), which consists of alpha (CD25), beta (CD122), and gamma (CD132) chains. The high-affinity receptor (CD25βγ) is expressed on activated T cells and is essential for robust IL-2 signaling. IL-2 binding activates the JAK-STAT signaling pathway (specifically JAK1 and TYK2), leading to STAT5 phosphorylation and transcription of genes involved in T cell growth and function.
- **Clinical Context:** The patient is post-transplant and immunosuppressed. While immunosuppressants contribute to the overall immune deficiency, the specific findings (low CD4+, low IL-2, low CD25 expression) point to a more specific defect within the T cell activation and proliferation pathway.
- **Connecting the Clues:** The decreased CD4+ T cell count in BAL fluid indicates impaired cellular immunity against P. jirovecii. The low serum IL-2 levels and reduced CD25 expression on CD4+ T cells suggest a problem with IL-2 production or signaling. Since IL-2 is primarily produced by activated T cells, the most likely defect is impaired IL-2 production by these cells, leading to reduced T cell proliferation and survival, and thus increased susceptibility to PJP.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Defective production of interferon-gamma (IFN-γ) by NK cells. IFN-γ is primarily produced by Th1 cells and NK cells and is crucial for macrophage activation against intracellular pathogens like P. jirovecii. While IFN-γ deficiency can cause PJP susceptibility, the vignette specifically points to defects in the IL-2 pathway (low IL-2, low CD25 expression), not IFN-γ production by NK cells. NK cells are generally less affected by the immunosuppression regimen described, and the primary defect highlighted is T cell-related.
- (B) Impaired antigen presentation by dendritic cells due to defective MHC class II expression. Defective MHC class II expression would impair CD4+ T cell activation, leading to reduced T cell numbers and function. While this could contribute to PJP susceptibility, the specific findings of low IL-2 and low CD25 expression strongly implicate a defect downstream of initial T cell activation, within the IL-2 pathway itself. This option doesn't directly explain the low IL-2 and CD25 findings.
- (D) Defective IL-2 receptor signaling pathway due to mutations in JAK kinases. This would lead to a lack of response to IL-2, even if IL-2 levels were normal. Patients with JAK kinase deficiencies (e.g., JAK3 deficiency in X-linked severe combined immunodeficiency - X-SCID) typically present earlier in life with severe infections. While it affects the IL-2 pathway, the vignette specifically shows *low IL-2 levels* and *reduced CD25 expression*, suggesting the problem is upstream (production/expression) rather than downstream (signaling).
- (E) Defective complement activation leading to impaired opsonization of Pneumocystis jirovecii. Complement plays a role in innate immunity, but PJP susceptibility is primarily linked to defects in cell-mediated immunity (T cells). While complement deficiencies can increase susceptibility to certain encapsulated bacteria, they are not the primary cause of PJP in the context of T cell deficiency. The vignette's findings strongly point towards a T cell defect, specifically involving IL-2.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Correct Answer (C) - Defective IL-2 Production | (A) Defective IFN-γ Production (NK cells) | (B) Defective MHC Class II (DCs) | (D) Defective JAK Kinases (IL-2R Signaling) | (E) Defective Complement |
| :---------------------- | :--------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------- | :---------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | IL-2 production by T cells                     | IFN-γ production by NK/Th1 cells          | Antigen presentation by DCs       | IL-2 receptor signaling                       | Complement activation  |
| **Key Finding in Vignette** | Low serum IL-2, Low CD25 expression            | N/A                                       | N/A                               | N/A                                            | N/A                     |
| **Affected Immune Cells** | CD4+ T cells (primarily)                       | NK cells, Th1 cells                       | CD4+ T cells                      | All cells responding to IL-2                  | N/A                     |
| **Typical Presentation** | Opportunistic infections (PJP, CMV, etc.)       | Opportunistic infections (PJP, mycobacteria) | Opportunistic infections (PJP, CMV) | Severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID)       | Pyogenic infections    |
| **Relevance to PJP**    | High (T cell response crucial)                  | High (Macrophage activation crucial)       | High (T cell activation crucial)   | High (T cell proliferation crucial)             | Low                     |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The vignette highlights the critical role of IL-2 in T cell proliferation and function, particularly in controlling opportunistic infections like PJP. Low IL-2 levels and reduced expression of the high-affinity IL-2 receptor (CD25) on T cells indicate a defect in the IL-2 pathway, most likely impaired IL-2 production by activated T cells. This leads to reduced T cell expansion and effector function, increasing susceptibility to PJP. Differentiate this from defects in IL-2 receptor signaling (JAK kinases), IFN-γ production, MHC presentation, or complement.
