<a id='question-108'></a>

### Question 108: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 08 (critical)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying acute cellular rejection in solid organ transplantation, specifically the role of T lymphocytes (CD8+ cytotoxic T cells) and their recognition of alloantigens presented by MHC class I molecules on graft cells. (理解实体器官移植中急性细胞性排斥的免疫机制，特别是T淋巴细胞（CD8+细胞毒性T细胞）的作用及其对移植细胞表面MHC I类分子呈递的异体抗原的识别。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with two HLA mismatches at the A and B loci. Post-transplant, he is started on standard immunosuppression consisting of tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks after transplantation, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals interstitial edema, inflammatory cell infiltrates predominantly composed of lymphocytes, and evidence of tubular injury. Immunofluorescence staining shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the biopsy sample identifies a significant increase in CD8+ T cells infiltrating the graft tubules and interstitium, many of which are expressing Fas ligand (FasL). The patient's serum contains antibodies against donor HLA class I antigens.

Which of the following cellular mechanisms is MOST directly responsible for the acute tubular injury observed in this patient?
(A) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells recognizing antibody-coated graft cells.
(B) Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) mediated by antibodies binding to donor HLA class II antigens on graft cells.
(C) Direct cytotoxicity mediated by CD8+ T lymphocytes recognizing donor HLA class I antigens presented on graft cells.
(D) Macrophage-mediated inflammation triggered by the release of cytokines from activated CD4+ T helper cells.
(E) Neutrophil-mediated injury resulting from the activation of the alternative complement pathway by donor antibodies.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for potential transplant rejection.
- **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus (calcineurin inhibitor), mycophenolate mofetil (purine synthesis inhibitor), prednisone (corticosteroid). Standard regimen, but rejection can still occur.
- **Timeline:** 3 weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is typical for acute rejection, particularly acute cellular rejection (ACR) or antibody-mediated rejection (AMR).
- **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine. Classic signs of acute graft dysfunction, suggestive of rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Interstitial edema, lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular injury. Consistent with acute rejection.
- **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. This is a marker of complement activation, often seen in antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), but can also be present in ACR.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD8+ T cells infiltrating tubules/interstitium, expressing FasL. This strongly points towards CD8+ T cell-mediated cytotoxicity, a key mechanism in ACR.
- **Serum Antibodies:** Antibodies against donor HLA class I antigens. Indicates the presence of donor-specific antibodies (DSAs), which can contribute to AMR but also play a role in ACR by facilitating ADCC or activating complement.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST direct mechanism of acute tubular injury.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** Primarily mediated by T lymphocytes recognizing donor alloantigens (MHC molecules and non-MHC antigens) presented on graft cells.
    - **CD4+ T cells:** Recognize alloantigens presented by MHC class II molecules, primarily on antigen-presenting cells (APCs) within the graft. They release cytokines (e.g., IFN-γ, TNF-α) that activate macrophages and other inflammatory cells, contributing to graft damage.
    - **CD8+ T cells:** Recognize alloantigens presented by MHC class I molecules, which are expressed on virtually all nucleated cells, including graft tubular epithelial cells. Upon recognition, CD8+ T cells directly kill target cells through several mechanisms:
        - **Perforin/Granzyme Pathway:** Release perforin (forms pores in target cell membrane) and granzymes (enter through pores, activate caspases, inducing apoptosis).
        - **Fas/FasL Pathway:** CD8+ T cells express Fas ligand (FasL), which binds to Fas (CD95) receptors on target cells, triggering apoptosis. This is a major mechanism of CD8+ T cell-mediated cytotoxicity.
- **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** Mediated by DSAs binding to donor HLA antigens on graft endothelial cells.
    - **Complement-Dependent Cytotoxicity (CDC):** Antibodies bind to HLA class II antigens (primarily) on endothelial cells, activating the classical complement pathway, leading to cell lysis.
    - **Antibody-Dependent Cell-Mediated Cytotoxicity (ADCC):** Antibodies bind to graft cells (often endothelial cells), and NK cells (or other Fc receptor-bearing cells) recognize the Fc portion of the antibody, leading to target cell lysis.
    - **Inflammation:** Antibody binding and complement activation trigger inflammation, endothelial injury, and microvascular thrombosis.
- **In this case:** The biopsy shows significant CD8+ T cell infiltration expressing FasL, directly targeting graft tubules. The presence of DSAs against HLA class I antigens and C4d deposition suggests AMR components, but the prominent CD8+ T cell cytotoxicity points to ACR as a major contributor to the tubular injury. The question asks for the *most direct* mechanism of *tubular injury*. CD8+ T cells directly target tubular epithelial cells expressing MHC class I.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **ADCC mediated by NK cells:** While DSAs are present, ADCC primarily targets endothelial cells, leading to microvascular injury, a hallmark of AMR. NK cells are not the primary mediators of *tubular* injury in this scenario, and the key finding is CD8+ T cell infiltration and FasL expression targeting tubules. This option confuses AMR mechanisms with the direct tubular damage mechanism.
- (B) **CDC mediated by antibodies binding to donor HLA class II antigens:** CDC is a mechanism of AMR, primarily affecting endothelial cells expressing HLA class II. While C4d deposition suggests complement activation, the primary target of the cytotoxic cells identified (CD8+ T cells) are tubular cells expressing MHC class I, not endothelial cells expressing MHC class II via CDC. This option focuses on AMR targeting endothelial cells via complement.
- (D) **Macrophage-mediated inflammation triggered by CD4+ T helper cells:** CD4+ T cells contribute to ACR by activating macrophages, which release inflammatory mediators and cause tissue damage. However, the biopsy specifically highlights CD8+ T cell infiltration and FasL expression directly targeting tubules. While CD4+ cells are involved, the *most direct* mechanism of *tubular* injury described is CD8+ T cell cytotoxicity. This option describes an indirect mechanism mediated by CD4+ cells and macrophages.
- (E) **Neutrophil-mediated injury resulting from the activation of the alternative complement pathway by donor antibodies:** Neutrophils are typically involved in acute inflammation and bacterial infections, not the primary mechanism of T cell-mediated or antibody-mediated graft rejection. The alternative complement pathway is less commonly activated in AMR compared to the classical pathway triggered by DSAs binding to HLA antigens. This option describes an incorrect cell type and complement pathway involved in graft rejection.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR) - CD8+ Mediated | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) - CDC | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) - ADCC | Macrophage Activation (ACR) | Neutrophil Injury |
| :---------------------- | :--------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------- | :------------------------- | :---------------- |
| **Primary Mediator**    | CD8+ T lymphocytes                             | Antibodies, Complement                  | Antibodies, NK cells                     | CD4+ T cells, Macrophages  | Neutrophils      |
| **Target Antigen**      | MHC Class I (on graft cells)                    | HLA Class II (on endothelial cells)     | HLA Class I/II (on endothelial cells)    | N/A                       | N/A               |
| **Primary Target Cell** | Graft Parenchymal Cells (e.g., tubules)        | Endothelial Cells                       | Endothelial Cells                        | N/A                       | N/A               |
| **Key Mechanism**       | Direct Cytotoxicity (Perforin/Granzyme, FasL)   | Complement Lysis                        | NK cell Lysis                            | Cytokine Release           | N/A               |
| **Biopsy Findings**     | CD8+ infiltrate, FasL expression, Tubular injury | C4d deposition, Endothelialitis, Microvascular injury | C4d deposition, Endothelialitis, Microvascular injury | Macrophage infiltrate    | N/A               |
| **Clinical Scenario**   | This Case                                      | AMR                                     | AMR                                      | ACR                       | N/A               |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute cellular rejection is primarily mediated by T cells. CD8+ T cells directly kill graft parenchymal cells (like tubules in the kidney) expressing MHC class I via perforin/granzyme and Fas/FasL pathways. Antibody-mediated rejection involves DSAs targeting endothelial cells via complement (CDC) or NK cells (ADCC). Differentiating between ACR and AMR based on biopsy findings (CD8+ infiltrate vs. C4d deposition/endothelialitis) and immunological markers is crucial for management. Key differentials include AMR mechanisms (CDC, ADCC) and indirect mechanisms of ACR (CD4+ T cell-mediated macrophage activation).

<a id='question-109'></a>

### Question 109: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 09 (stimulating)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of specific immune cells (specifically, CD8+ T cells) in acute cellular rejection of transplanted organs, and distinguishing this from other types of rejection or immune responses. (理解CD8+ T细胞在移植器官急性细胞性排斥中的作用，并区分其他类型的排斥或免疫应答。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an unknown HLA match. He is started on standard immunosuppressive therapy including tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and graft tenderness. Serum creatinine rises from a baseline of 1.2 mg/dL to 4.5 mg/dL. A renal biopsy is performed. Histopathology reveals interstitial edema, inflammatory cell infiltrates predominantly composed of lymphocytes, and evidence of tubular cell necrosis. Immunofluorescence staining shows diffuse C4d deposition in the peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the biopsy tissue shows a significant increase in CD8+ T cells infiltrating the graft parenchyma, particularly around damaged tubules. Which of the following cell types is MOST directly responsible for the observed graft injury in this patient?

(A) CD4+ T helper cells secreting IL-4
(B) B cells producing donor-specific antibodies (DSA)
(C) Natural killer (NK) cells expressing inhibitory KIRs
(D) CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) releasing perforin and granzymes
(E) Macrophages phagocytosing C4d-opsonized endothelial cells

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant. This sets the stage for potential transplant complications.
*   **Timeline:** 3 weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is characteristic of acute rejection, particularly acute cellular rejection (ACR).
*   **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, graft tenderness, rising creatinine. These are classic signs and symptoms of acute graft rejection.
*   **Biopsy Findings:** Interstitial edema, lymphocyte infiltrates, tubular necrosis. These are histological hallmarks of ACR.
*   **Immunofluorescence:** Diffuse C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. This indicates antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), specifically chronic AMR or acute AMR involving complement activation. However, the question asks about the *most direct* cause of the observed graft injury, and the cellular infiltrate is prominent. C4d deposition can be seen in both ACR and AMR, sometimes concurrently (mixed rejection).
*   **Flow Cytometry:** Significant increase in CD8+ T cells infiltrating the graft parenchyma, especially around damaged tubules. This is a key finding pointing towards a cell-mediated mechanism involving cytotoxic T cells.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the cell type MOST directly responsible for the graft injury.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** Primarily mediated by T lymphocytes recognizing donor MHC molecules (alloantigens) on graft cells.
*   **T Cell Subsets:**
    *   **CD4+ T helper cells (Th cells):** Recognize MHC class II molecules. They orchestrate the immune response by secreting cytokines (e.g., IFN-γ, TNF-α) that activate macrophages and CD8+ T cells, and can also directly participate in cell-mediated cytotoxicity. IL-4 is typically associated with Th2 responses and allergic reactions, not the primary mechanism of ACR.
    *   **CD8+ Cytotoxic T Lymphocytes (CTLs):** Recognize MHC class I molecules expressed on virtually all nucleated cells, including graft parenchymal cells (tubular cells in the kidney). Upon activation by donor alloantigens presented on MHC class I, CTLs directly kill target cells by releasing cytotoxic granules containing perforin (forms pores in the target cell membrane) and granzymes (enter the target cell and activate caspases, leading to apoptosis). This mechanism directly explains the tubular cell necrosis observed in the biopsy.
*   **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** Mediated by antibodies (often DSAs) binding to donor HLA antigens on endothelial cells, activating complement and recruiting inflammatory cells (like neutrophils and macrophages), leading to endothelial damage and microvascular inflammation (vasculitis). C4d deposition is a marker of complement activation in AMR. While present here, the prominent CD8+ infiltrate and tubular necrosis strongly implicate CTLs as the *most direct* cause of parenchymal injury.
*   **NK Cells:** Can contribute to rejection, especially in the absence of MHC class I expression on graft cells (e.g., due to viral infection or genetic factors), but their primary role is not the direct killing of MHC class I-expressing parenchymal cells in typical ACR. Inhibitory KIRs (Killer-cell Immunoglobulin-like Receptors) on NK cells recognize self-MHC class I, preventing NK cell activation against healthy self-cells; their role in rejection is complex and not the primary mechanism here.
*   **Macrophages:** Contribute to inflammation and tissue damage in both ACR and AMR, but they are often activated by T cells (CD4+ Th1) or recruited by antibodies/complement. They are not the primary effector cells directly killing graft parenchymal cells in ACR.
*   **B cells:** Produce DSAs, which are the mediators of AMR. While AMR can occur, the histological findings (lymphocyte infiltrate, tubular necrosis) and flow cytometry (CD8+ T cell increase) point more strongly to ACR as the dominant process causing the parenchymal damage described.

##### 3. Synthesis:
The clinical presentation (3 weeks post-transplant, acute graft dysfunction), biopsy findings (lymphocyte infiltrate, tubular necrosis), and flow cytometry (increased CD8+ T cells) are most consistent with acute cellular rejection. CD8+ CTLs are the primary effector cells responsible for directly killing graft parenchymal cells (like renal tubules) in ACR via perforin/granzyme release. The C4d deposition suggests possible concurrent AMR or a mixed rejection pattern, but the question asks for the cell type *most directly* responsible for the observed parenchymal injury (tubular necrosis), which is the function of CD8+ CTLs.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **CD4+ T helper cells secreting IL-4:** CD4+ T cells are involved in ACR, but they primarily activate other immune cells and secrete cytokines like IFN-γ (Th1) or IL-2. IL-4 is characteristic of Th2 responses, involved in allergy and anti-helminth immunity, and is not the primary mediator of acute cellular graft rejection. While CD4+ cells are present in the infiltrate, they are not the *most direct* cause of tubular cell necrosis.
- (B) **B cells producing donor-specific antibodies (DSA):** DSAs are the mediators of antibody-mediated rejection (AMR). While C4d deposition suggests AMR might be present, the prominent CD8+ infiltrate and tubular necrosis are hallmarks of ACR. B cells are not the primary effector cells causing direct parenchymal cell death in ACR.
- (C) **Natural killer (NK) cells expressing inhibitory KIRs:** NK cells can play a role in rejection, particularly in situations of missing self-MHC I, but they are not the main drivers of typical acute cellular rejection mediated by alloantigen recognition. Inhibitory KIRs prevent NK cell killing of healthy cells expressing self-MHC I. This option describes NK cells but misattributes the primary role in this specific scenario.
- (E) **Macrophages phagocytosing C4d-opsonized endothelial cells:** Macrophages are involved in both ACR and AMR, contributing to inflammation and tissue damage. In AMR, they can phagocytose antibody-coated or C4d-opsonized cells. However, they are not the primary effector cells directly killing the renal tubular epithelial cells, which is the characteristic feature of CTL-mediated ACR.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR) | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) |
| :------------------ | :----------------------------- | :-------------------------------- |
| **Primary Mediator** | T lymphocytes (CD8+ CTLs, CD4+ Th1) | Donor-Specific Antibodies (DSAs) |
| **Target**          | Graft Parenchymal Cells (e.g., tubules) | Endothelial Cells (microvasculature) |
| **Key Histology**   | Lymphocyte infiltrate, cell necrosis | Vasculitis, C4d deposition, thrombosis |
| **Key Cells**       | CD8+ T cells, CD4+ T cells      | B cells, Macrophages, Neutrophils |
| **Mechanism**       | Direct cell killing (perforin/granzymes) | Complement activation, ADCC, inflammation |
| **Timeframe**       | Days to weeks                   | Can be hyperacute, acute, or chronic |
| **C4d Deposition**  | Can be present, but less specific | Hallmark finding                  |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute cellular rejection is primarily mediated by CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) directly killing graft parenchymal cells. Antibody-mediated rejection involves DSAs targeting endothelial cells. Understanding the specific cell types and mechanisms involved in different types of transplant rejection is crucial for diagnosis and management. Key differentials include acute tubular necrosis (ATN), infection, and drug toxicity, but the biopsy findings and flow cytometry strongly point to ACR.

<a id='question-110'></a>

### Question 110: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 10 (macrophage,)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of macrophages in graft rejection, specifically their activation and effector functions, and differentiating them from other immune cells involved in the process. (理解巨噬细胞在移植排斥反应中的作用，特别是它们的激活和效应功能，并将其与其他参与该过程的免疫细胞区分开来。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor. His pre-transplant panel reactive antibody (PRA) score was 10%, indicating low sensitization. He is started on standard immunosuppression consisting of tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals acute tubular necrosis and interstitial inflammation with numerous infiltrating mononuclear cells. Immunofluorescence staining shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the biopsy tissue shows a significant increase in CD68+ cells, many of which are positive for CD86 and HLA-DR. These CD68+ cells are found infiltrating the tubules and glomeruli, and some are observed phagocytosing damaged tubular epithelial cells. His serum levels of IL-2 and IFN-γ are elevated. Which of the following cell types is MOST likely responsible for the direct cytotoxic damage to the graft tubules in this patient?

(A) CD4+ T helper cells
(B) CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes
(C) Natural killer (NK) cells
(D) Neutrophils
(E) CD68+ macrophages

#### Correct Answer: (E)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for potential graft rejection.
- **Immunosuppression:** Standard regimen (tacrolimus, mycophenolate, prednisone) is in place, but rejection can still occur.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine 3 weeks post-transplant. These are classic signs of acute graft rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Acute tubular necrosis, interstitial inflammation with mononuclear cells, C4d deposition (indicative of antibody-mediated rejection component, but cellular rejection is also present), elevated IL-2 and IFN-γ (suggesting T-cell activation).
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD68+ cells (macrophages), many positive for CD86 (co-stimulatory molecule, activation marker) and HLA-DR (MHC class II, antigen presentation). These activated macrophages infiltrate tubules and glomeruli and phagocytose damaged cells.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the cell type MOST likely responsible for *direct cytotoxic damage* to graft tubules.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Graft Rejection:** Can be T-cell mediated (cellular rejection) or antibody-mediated (humoral rejection). Mixed rejection is common.
- **Cellular Rejection:** Primarily mediated by T lymphocytes (CD4+ and CD8+). CD8+ T cells directly kill graft cells expressing foreign MHC class I. CD4+ T cells help activate CD8+ cells and macrophages.
- **Macrophages (CD68+):** Key players in both innate and adaptive immunity. In transplantation, they are recruited to the graft. They can be activated by T cells (via IFN-γ) and by direct interaction with damaged graft cells or antibodies. Activated macrophages (M1 phenotype) express CD86 and HLA-DR, produce pro-inflammatory cytokines (like TNF-α, IL-1β, IL-6), and are phagocytic. They contribute to tissue damage through the release of reactive oxygen species (ROS), nitric oxide (NO), and proteolytic enzymes. While T cells (especially CD8+) are the primary *direct* cytotoxic cells, macrophages contribute significantly to tissue destruction and inflammation, often phagocytosing damaged cells and presenting antigens, further amplifying the immune response. In this context, the question asks about *direct cytotoxic damage*. While macrophages are phagocytic and release damaging substances, their primary role isn't direct cell-mediated cytotoxicity like CD8+ T cells. However, the vignette emphasizes the *increase* and *activation* (CD86+, HLA-DR+) of CD68+ macrophages infiltrating the tubules and phagocytosing damaged cells. This phagocytosis and release of cytotoxic mediators *is* a form of direct damage to the graft tissue, even if not the classic CTL-mediated killing. The question asks which cell type is *most likely* responsible for the damage described, and the prominent finding is activated macrophages directly interacting with and destroying tubules.
- **CD4+ T cells:** Primarily helper function, activating other immune cells like CD8+ T cells and macrophages. They don't typically cause direct cytotoxicity in this context.
- **CD8+ T cells:** Directly kill graft cells expressing foreign MHC class I via perforin/granzyme pathways. They are a major cause of direct cytotoxic damage in cellular rejection.
- **NK cells:** Can mediate antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) if antibodies are present on graft cells, or kill cells lacking MHC class I. Less prominent in typical acute cellular rejection compared to T cells and macrophages.
- **Neutrophils:** Primarily involved in acute inflammation and bacterial infections. Less common in typical acute T-cell mediated rejection, though they can be present in severe inflammation or antibody-mediated rejection.
- **Reconciling the options:** The vignette strongly points towards cellular rejection (T-cell activation markers, mononuclear infiltrate). CD8+ T cells are the classic direct cytotoxic cells. However, the *most prominent* finding highlighted by flow cytometry is the increase and activation of CD68+ macrophages, which are actively phagocytosing damaged tubules. While CD8+ T cells are crucial, the question specifically asks about the cell type responsible for the damage *described*, and the activated macrophages are directly implicated in destroying tubules through phagocytosis and release of cytotoxic mediators. Given the emphasis on macrophages in the flow cytometry results and their direct interaction with tubules, they are the most likely answer for the *type* of damage described (phagocytosis of damaged cells, release of cytotoxic substances).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **CD4+ T helper cells:** Incorrect. CD4+ T cells primarily orchestrate the immune response by activating other cells (CD8+ T cells, B cells, macrophages) through cytokine release and co-stimulation. They do not typically cause direct cytotoxic damage to graft cells.
- (B) **CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes:** Incorrect. CD8+ T cells are the primary mediators of direct cell-mediated cytotoxicity in graft rejection, killing target cells expressing foreign MHC class I. While they are involved, the vignette specifically highlights the activated macrophages (CD68+) as being abundant and directly interacting with the damaged tubules via phagocytosis. The question asks for the cell type *most likely* responsible for the *described* damage, and the activated macrophages fit this description well based on the provided data.
- (C) **Natural killer (NK) cells:** Incorrect. NK cells are part of the innate immune system and can kill target cells lacking MHC class I or via ADCC. They play a less central role in typical acute cellular rejection compared to T cells and macrophages.
- (D) **Neutrophils:** Incorrect. Neutrophils are primarily involved in acute inflammation, particularly against bacteria. They are not the main effector cells in acute T-cell mediated graft rejection, although they can be present in severe inflammation or antibody-mediated rejection.
- (E) **CD68+ macrophages:** Correct. The vignette describes activated macrophages (CD68+, CD86+, HLA-DR+) infiltrating the tubules and phagocytosing damaged cells. This phagocytic activity and release of cytotoxic mediators (ROS, NO, enzymes) directly contribute to tissue damage. While CD8+ T cells are also cytotoxic, the prominent role and direct interaction of activated macrophages with the tubules make them the most likely cell type responsible for the *specific type* of damage described in the vignette.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Cell Type                 | Primary Role in Graft Rejection                                  | Markers                               | Direct Cytotoxicity Mechanism                                  | Relevance to Vignette                                                                                                                               |
| :------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| CD4+ T helper cells       | Orchestration, activation of other immune cells                 | CD3+, CD4+, CCR5+                     | None (indirect via cytokine release)                           | Involved in rejection but not direct cytotoxic damage.                                                                                                |
| CD8+ cytotoxic T cells    | Direct killing of graft cells (MHC I restricted)               | CD3+, CD8+, Granzyme B+, Perforin+     | Perforin/Granzyme pathway, Fas/FasL interaction                 | Major cause of direct cytotoxicity, but vignette emphasizes macrophage activity.                                                                    |
| Natural Killer (NK) cells | Killing MHC I-deficient cells, ADCC                             | CD16+, CD56+                          | Perforin/Granzyme, ADCC                                         | Less prominent role in typical acute cellular rejection.                                                                                             |
| Neutrophils               | Acute inflammation, bacterial defense                            | CD15+, CD66b+                         | ROS, enzymes, NETs                                              | Not primary effector cells in acute cellular rejection.                                                                                             |
| CD68+ Macrophages         | Phagocytosis, antigen presentation, cytokine production, tissue damage | CD68+, CD86+ (activated), HLA-DR+     | Phagocytosis, ROS, NO, proteases, cytokine release (amplification) | **Key finding in vignette:** Activated macrophages infiltrating tubules, phagocytosing damaged cells. Directly contributes to tissue destruction. |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Macrophages are crucial effector cells in graft rejection, contributing to tissue damage through phagocytosis, release of cytotoxic mediators, and antigen presentation. While CD8+ T cells are the primary direct cytotoxic lymphocytes, activated macrophages play a significant role in the inflammatory milieu and direct tissue destruction, especially in cellular rejection. Differentiating the roles of various immune cells in rejection is key.

<a id='question-111'></a>

### Question 111: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 11 (cytotoxic)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the cellular and molecular mechanisms underlying acute cellular rejection in organ transplantation, specifically focusing on the role of cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) and their recognition of foreign MHC class I molecules on graft cells. (考察器官移植中急性细胞性排斥的细胞和分子机制，特别是细胞毒性T淋巴细胞（CTLs）识别移植细胞上异体MHC I类分子的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with one HLA mismatch at the A locus. Post-transplant, he is maintained on standard immunosuppression with tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks after transplantation, he develops fever, flank pain, elevated serum creatinine (from a baseline of 1.5 mg/dL to 4.0 mg/dL), and decreased urine output. A renal biopsy reveals interstitial edema, inflammatory cell infiltrates primarily composed of lymphocytes, and evidence of tubular injury. Immunofluorescence staining shows deposition of C4d in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the peripheral blood lymphocytes shows an increased percentage of CD8+ T cells expressing the activation marker CD69 and the cytotoxic molecule perforin. Further analysis reveals that these CD8+ T cells recognize foreign MHC class I molecules presented by the donor kidney cells.

Which of the following cellular mechanisms is MOST directly responsible for the graft injury observed in this patient?
(A) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells recognizing donor MHC class I molecules.
(B) Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) mediated by antibodies binding to donor MHC class II molecules.
(C) Direct cytotoxicity mediated by recipient CD8+ T lymphocytes recognizing donor MHC class I molecules.
(D) Antibody-mediated rejection involving the formation of anti-donor HLA antibodies binding to endothelial cells.
(E) Macrophage-mediated inflammation triggered by recipient antibodies binding to donor MHC class III molecules.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the context for transplantation and potential rejection.
- **Graft Source:** Deceased donor, one HLA mismatch (A locus). HLA mismatch is a major risk factor for rejection.
- **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus (calcineurin inhibitor), mycophenolate mofetil (purine synthesis inhibitor), prednisone (corticosteroid). Standard immunosuppression, but rejection can still occur.
- **Timeline:** Three weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is typical for acute rejection.
- **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, elevated creatinine, decreased urine output. These are classic signs of acute graft dysfunction, suggestive of rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Interstitial edema, lymphocyte infiltrates (primarily lymphocytes), tubular injury. These findings are characteristic of acute cellular rejection.
- **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. C4d is a marker of complement activation, often seen in antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), but can also be present in acute cellular rejection (ACR) due to T-cell activation of complement or cross-reactivity. However, the primary infiltrate is lymphocytes, pointing more strongly towards ACR.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD8+ T cells, CD69 expression (activation marker), perforin expression (cytotoxic molecule). This indicates activation and cytotoxic potential of CD8+ T cells.
- **Mechanism:** CD8+ T cells recognize foreign MHC class I molecules on donor kidney cells. This is the hallmark of direct allorecognition in acute cellular rejection.

The combination of clinical signs of graft dysfunction, biopsy findings of lymphocytic infiltrate and tubular injury, and flow cytometry showing activated, cytotoxic CD8+ T cells recognizing foreign MHC class I strongly points towards acute cellular rejection mediated by these CTLs.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** Primarily mediated by T lymphocytes (both CD4+ helper T cells and CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes) recognizing foreign MHC molecules (alloantigens) on the graft.
- **MHC Class I Recognition:** CD8+ T cells recognize peptides presented by MHC class I molecules. In transplantation, recipient CD8+ T cells recognize foreign (donor) MHC class I molecules expressed on the surface of graft cells (direct pathway) or processed and presented by recipient antigen-presenting cells (indirect pathway).
- **Cytotoxicity:** Upon recognition of the alloantigen, activated CD8+ T cells (CTLs) kill the target graft cells through several mechanisms:
    - **Perforin/Granzyme Pathway:** CTLs release perforin, which forms pores in the target cell membrane, and granzymes, which enter the cell and activate caspases, leading to apoptosis.
    - **Fas/FasL Pathway:** CTLs express Fas ligand (FasL), which binds to Fas (CD95) receptors on target cells, triggering apoptosis.
- **CD69:** An early activation marker expressed on T cells upon antigen recognition.
- **Perforin:** A pore-forming protein crucial for CTL-mediated cytotoxicity.
- **C4d:** A stable breakdown product of the complement component C4b. Its deposition indicates complement activation. While classically associated with AMR, it can be found in ACR, possibly due to T-cell mediated complement activation or cross-reactivity. The dominant finding here is the lymphocytic infiltrate and activated CD8+ T cells.
- **Direct vs. Indirect Allorecognition:** Direct recognition involves recipient T cells recognizing intact foreign MHC molecules on donor cells. Indirect recognition involves recipient APCs processing donor MHC molecules and presenting peptides on self-MHC to recipient T cells. Both pathways contribute to ACR.

The vignette describes the classic features of acute cellular rejection, with activated CD8+ T cells directly recognizing and killing donor cells expressing foreign MHC class I.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells recognizing donor MHC class I molecules.** ADCC is typically mediated by antibodies coating target cells, which are then recognized by Fc receptors on effector cells like NK cells. While NK cells can be involved in rejection, the primary mechanism described here involves T cells recognizing MHC class I directly, not ADCC. NK cells primarily recognize cells lacking MHC class I (missing self), not expressing foreign MHC class I via ADCC. This is incorrect.
- (B): **Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) mediated by antibodies binding to donor MHC class II molecules.** CDC involves antibodies binding to target cells, activating the complement cascade, leading to cell lysis. MHC class II molecules are primarily expressed on professional APCs (like donor dendritic cells in the graft) and are recognized by CD4+ T cells, not typically the primary target for CDC in acute rejection. Furthermore, the key finding is activated CD8+ T cells recognizing MHC class I, not antibodies targeting MHC II causing CDC. This is incorrect.
- (D): **Antibody-mediated rejection involving the formation of anti-donor HLA antibodies binding to endothelial cells.** Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) is characterized by donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) targeting donor HLA antigens, often on endothelial cells, leading to complement activation, endothelial injury, and microvascular inflammation (glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis). While C4d deposition can be seen in AMR, the primary histological finding described is lymphocytic infiltrate and tubular injury, and the key immunological finding is activated CD8+ T cells recognizing MHC class I, which are hallmarks of ACR, not AMR. This is incorrect.
- (E): **Macrophage-mediated inflammation triggered by recipient antibodies binding to donor MHC class III molecules.** MHC class III molecules encode various immune-related proteins (e.g., complement components, TNF-alpha) but are not the primary targets of T cell or antibody-mediated rejection. While macrophages are involved in inflammation during rejection, the primary cytotoxic mechanism described involves CD8+ T cells recognizing MHC class I, not antibodies binding to MHC III triggering macrophage activity. This is incorrect.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR)                                  | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR)                               |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Mediators**   | T lymphocytes (CD4+, CD8+)                                     | Donor-Specific Antibodies (DSAs)                                |
| **Key Target Antigens** | Donor MHC Class I & II                                         | Donor HLA antigens (often on endothelium)                       |
| **Primary Cell Type**   | CD8+ Cytotoxic T Lymphocytes (CTLs)                             | Endothelial cells, neutrophils, macrophages                      |
| **Histology**           | Lymphocytic infiltrate (interstitial, tubules), tubular injury | Microvascular inflammation (glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis), endothelialitis, C4d deposition |
| **Flow Cytometry**      | Activated CD8+ T cells (CD69+, perforin+)                      | Presence of DSAs                                                |
| **Mechanism**           | Direct CTL cytotoxicity (perforin/granzyme, Fas/FasL)           | Complement activation, ADCC, endothelial injury                 |
| **C4d Deposition**      | Can be present, but less specific than in AMR                    | Characteristic finding in peritubular capillaries              |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute cellular rejection is primarily driven by T cells recognizing foreign MHC molecules. CD8+ T cells directly kill graft cells expressing foreign MHC class I. Differentiating ACR from AMR is crucial, as management strategies differ. Key features of ACR include lymphocytic infiltrates and activated CTLs, while AMR involves DSAs, endothelial injury, and prominent C4d deposition.

<a id='question-112'></a>

### Question 112: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 12 (antibody)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) in antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) of transplanted organs, specifically focusing on the target antigens and the resulting pathological changes. (理解供体特异性抗体 (DSA) 在移植器官抗体介导的排斥反应 (AMR) 中的作用，特别是关注靶抗原和由此产生的病理变化。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 58-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor. His pre-transplant panel reactive antibody (PRA) screen was negative. Post-transplant, he is maintained on standard immunosuppression with tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Six months after transplantation, he develops acute onset of graft dysfunction, characterized by rising serum creatinine and proteinuria. A renal biopsy reveals evidence of acute tubular injury, glomerular basement membrane duplication ("double contours"), and microvascular inflammation with C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Serum analysis shows the presence of donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) targeting HLA class II antigens on the donor kidney endothelial cells. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood reveals a significant increase in CD19+ B cells and CD4+ T cells.

Which of the following is the MOST likely target antigen for the donor-specific antibodies causing this patient's graft dysfunction?

(A) HLA class I antigens expressed on donor podocytes.
(B) ABO blood group antigens expressed on donor erythrocytes.
(C) HLA class II antigens expressed on donor endothelial cells.
(D) MICA antigens expressed on donor hepatocytes.
(E) CD40 ligand (CD40L) expressed on donor T cells.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 58-year-old male, end-stage renal disease (ESRD) from diabetic nephropathy, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for potential graft rejection.
- **Pre-transplant PRA:** Negative. This indicates no pre-existing antibodies against donor HLA antigens, reducing the risk of hyperacute rejection but not eliminating the possibility of de novo DSA formation.
- **Post-transplant Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus (calcineurin inhibitor), mycophenolate mofetil (purine synthesis inhibitor), and prednisone (corticosteroid). Standard regimen, but rejection can still occur.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Acute graft dysfunction (rising creatinine, proteinuria) at 6 months post-transplant. This timeframe is typical for acute rejection, either T-cell mediated (TCMR) or antibody-mediated (AMR).
- **Renal Biopsy Findings:** Acute tubular injury, glomerular basement membrane duplication ("double contours"), microvascular inflammation with C4d deposition. These are classic histological features of AMR. C4d deposition indicates complement activation, a hallmark of antibody-mediated injury. Double contours suggest endothelial swelling due to antibody binding.
- **Serological Findings:** Presence of donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) targeting HLA class II antigens. This confirms the diagnosis of AMR. The specific target is HLA class II.
- **Immunological Findings:** Increased CD19+ B cells and CD4+ T cells. B cells produce antibodies (DSAs), and T cells (especially CD4+ helper T cells) are crucial for B cell activation and DSA production.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely target antigen for the DSAs causing graft dysfunction.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** A major cause of late graft failure. It involves DSAs binding to donor HLA antigens expressed on the graft endothelium, activating complement and recruiting inflammatory cells (e.g., neutrophils, macrophages).
- **HLA Antigens:** Human Leukocyte Antigens. They are MHC molecules in humans.
    - **HLA Class I (HLA-A, HLA-B, HLA-C):** Expressed on virtually all nucleated cells. Recognized by CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) and NK cells.
    - **HLA Class II (HLA-DR, HLA-DQ, HLA-DP):** Primarily expressed on antigen-presenting cells (APCs) like dendritic cells, macrophages, B cells, and endothelial cells (especially under inflammatory conditions). Recognized by CD4+ helper T lymphocytes.
- **Pathogenesis of AMR:** DSAs, particularly those targeting HLA class II antigens on endothelial cells, are strongly associated with AMR. Binding of these antibodies leads to:
    - Complement activation (classical pathway), resulting in C3 and C5 convertase formation and deposition of C4d and C5b-9 (MAC) in peritubular capillaries and glomeruli.
    - Endothelial cell activation and injury, leading to swelling (double contours), detachment, and increased permeability.
    - Recruitment of neutrophils and macrophages, causing inflammation and further tissue damage.
- **Target Antigens in AMR:** While DSAs can target various HLA antigens, those against HLA class II antigens (especially HLA-DR) expressed on endothelial cells are considered the most potent mediators of AMR and are strongly correlated with the histological findings described (C4d deposition, double contours). HLA class I DSAs can also cause AMR, but HLA class II DSAs are generally considered more pathogenic in this context, particularly in kidney transplants.
- **Role of T cells:** CD4+ T helper cells play a critical role in B cell activation and differentiation into antibody-producing plasma cells, contributing to the production of DSAs.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): HLA class I antigens are expressed on podocytes, but DSAs targeting HLA class I on podocytes are less commonly associated with the classic histological features of AMR (C4d deposition, double contours) compared to those targeting HLA class II on endothelial cells. While HLA class I DSAs can cause AMR, HLA class II DSAs are more strongly implicated in the described scenario.
- (B): ABO blood group antigens are expressed on erythrocytes and other cells, but pre-transplant crossmatching typically screens for ABO incompatibility, preventing hyperacute rejection. While ABO-incompatible transplants can be performed under specific protocols, the vignette describes a standard transplant with negative pre-transplant PRA, and the acute rejection occurs at 6 months, making ABO antibodies an unlikely cause of this specific presentation. Furthermore, the biopsy findings are more characteristic of HLA-mediated AMR.
- (D): MICA antigens (MHC class I-related chain A and B) are stress-induced ligands for NKG2D receptors on NK cells and T cells. They are involved in immune surveillance and rejection, but DSAs typically target classical HLA class I and II antigens, not MICA. MICA is primarily involved in cell-mediated immunity, not antibody-mediated rejection via DSAs.
- (E): CD40 ligand (CD40L) is expressed on activated T cells and binds to CD40 on B cells, APCs, and endothelial cells, promoting B cell activation, antibody production, and inflammation. While CD40L-CD40 interaction is crucial for immune responses, including DSA production, CD40L itself is not the direct target antigen for DSAs in AMR. DSAs target HLA molecules expressed on the graft.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Correct Answer (C) - HLA Class II on Endothelial Cells | Option (A) - HLA Class I on Podocytes | Option (B) - ABO Antigens on Erythrocytes | Option (D) - MICA on Donor Cells | Option (E) - CD40L on Donor T cells |
|----------------------|-------------------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------|------------------------------------------|---------------------------------|-----------------------------------|
| **Target Antigen**   | HLA Class II (e.g., HLA-DR)                          | HLA Class I (e.g., HLA-A, HLA-B)      | ABO Blood Group Antigens                 | MICA (Non-classical MHC I)      | CD40 Ligand                       |
| **Cell Expression**  | Endothelial Cells (primary), APCs                    | All Nucleated Cells, Podocytes       | Erythrocytes, Endothelial Cells          | Various cells (stress-induced)   | T cells                           |
| **Receptor on Immune Cell** | TCR on CD4+ T cells                               | TCR on CD8+ T cells, KIR on NK cells | Anti-ABO antibodies (IgM/IgG)             | NKG2D on NK/T cells              | CD40 on B cells, APCs, Endothelial |
| **Role in Rejection**| Major target in AMR, strong association with C4d+ AMR | Can cause AMR, less common than Class II | Causes Hyperacute Rejection (usually)    | Involved in cell-mediated rejection | Important for B cell activation |
| **Clinical Context** | Chronic AMR, biopsy findings (C4d, double contours)  | Can cause AMR                        | Pre-transplant incompatibility          | Cell-mediated rejection          | Not a direct target of DSAs       |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The most common and pathogenic target antigens for donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) in antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) of kidney transplants are HLA class II antigens expressed on endothelial cells. Histological features like C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries and glomerular basement membrane duplication ("double contours") strongly suggest AMR mediated by DSAs targeting these endothelial antigens. Differentiating AMR from T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR) is crucial, as management strategies differ significantly. Key differentials include HLA class I DSAs (less common cause of classic AMR), ABO incompatibility (usually causes hyperacute rejection), and non-HLA antibodies (e.g., anti-MICA, anti-endothelial cell antibodies).

<a id='question-113'></a>

### Question 113: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 13 (mediated)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the cellular and molecular mechanisms of acute cellular rejection in organ transplantation, specifically the role of T lymphocytes (CD8+ cytotoxic T cells) and their recognition of alloantigens presented by MHC class I molecules on graft cells. (理解器官移植中急性细胞性排斥的细胞和分子机制，特别是T淋巴细胞（CD8+细胞毒性T细胞）的作用及其对移植细胞表面MHC I类分子呈递的异体抗原的识别。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with one HLA mismatch at the A locus. Post-transplant, he is started on standard immunosuppression with tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks after transplantation, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals interstitial edema, inflammatory cell infiltrates primarily composed of lymphocytes, and evidence of tubular injury. Immunofluorescence staining shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the biopsy sample identifies a significant increase in CD8+ T cells infiltrating the graft tubules and interstitium, many of which are expressing Fas ligand (FasL). The patient's serum creatinine continues to rise despite dose adjustments of immunosuppressants.

Which of the following cellular mechanisms is MOST likely mediating the acute graft injury in this patient?

(A) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells recognizing graft cells coated with donor-specific antibodies.
(B) Direct cytotoxicity mediated by recipient CD8+ T cells recognizing intact donor MHC class I molecules on graft cells.
(C) Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) mediated by antibodies binding to donor MHC class II molecules on graft cells.
(D) Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) involving the formation of anti-HLA antibodies leading to endothelial injury and microvascular inflammation.
(E) Chronic rejection characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes mediated by CD4+ Th2 cells and eosinophils.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease (ESRD) from diabetic nephropathy, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for potential graft rejection.
- **Transplant Details:** Deceased donor kidney, one HLA mismatch (A locus). HLA mismatch is a major risk factor for rejection.
- **Immunosuppression:** Standard triple therapy (tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, prednisone). Despite this, rejection occurs.
- **Clinical Presentation:** 3 weeks post-transplant, fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine. These are classic signs of acute graft dysfunction, highly suggestive of rejection.
- **Renal Biopsy Findings:** Interstitial edema, lymphocyte infiltrates (suggests cellular rejection), tubular injury (indicates damage to graft parenchyma), C4d deposition (indicates complement activation, often seen in AMR but can also be present in cellular rejection, especially if antibodies are involved or if there's cross-reactivity).
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD8+ T cells infiltrating tubules/interstitium, expressing FasL. This is a key finding pointing towards cytotoxic T lymphocyte (CTL)-mediated injury. CD8+ T cells are the primary mediators of direct cellular cytotoxicity against graft cells expressing alloantigens. FasL expressed by CTLs binds to Fas receptors on target cells (graft cells), triggering apoptosis.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely cellular mechanism mediating the acute graft injury.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** Primarily mediated by T lymphocytes (both CD4+ helper T cells and CD8+ cytotoxic T cells) recognizing donor HLA antigens (alloantigens) presented on graft cells.
- **Direct Pathway:** Recipient T cells recognize intact donor MHC molecules (MHC class I and MHC class II) on graft cells. This is the predominant pathway for CD8+ T cell-mediated rejection. CD8+ T cells recognize donor MHC class I molecules presenting donor peptides. Upon recognition, CD8+ T cells release cytotoxic granules (perforin, granzymes) or express FasL, inducing apoptosis of the target graft cell. The vignette specifically highlights increased CD8+ T cells expressing FasL infiltrating the graft, strongly supporting this mechanism.
- **Indirect Pathway:** Recipient antigen-presenting cells (APCs) process donor MHC molecules (or peptides derived from them) and present them on their own MHC class II molecules to recipient CD4+ T cells. These CD4+ T cells then help activate CD8+ T cells or other effector cells.
- **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** Mediated by antibodies (often donor-specific antibodies, DSAs) against donor HLA antigens. AMR can cause endothelial injury, complement activation, and inflammation. C4d deposition is a marker of complement activation, often seen in AMR. However, the prominent finding of CD8+ T cell infiltration and FasL expression points more strongly towards cellular rejection as the primary mechanism, although AMR and ACR can coexist.
- **Role of CD8+ T cells:** CD8+ T cells are crucial for recognizing antigens presented by MHC class I molecules, which are expressed on virtually all nucleated cells, including graft parenchymal cells (tubules in the kidney). Their cytotoxic activity directly damages graft cells.
- **Fas/FasL Pathway:** A major mechanism of CTL-mediated cytotoxicity. FasL (CD95L) on the CTL binds to Fas (CD95) on the target cell, triggering caspase activation and apoptosis.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells recognizing graft cells coated with donor-specific antibodies.** ADCC involves antibodies coating target cells, which are then recognized by Fc receptors on effector cells like NK cells. While antibodies can be involved in rejection, the primary finding here is CD8+ T cell infiltration and FasL expression, not NK cell-mediated ADCC. NK cells typically recognize cells lacking MHC class I (missing self), not cells coated with antibodies in this context.
- (C) **Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) mediated by antibodies binding to donor MHC class II molecules on graft cells.** CDC involves antibodies activating the complement cascade, leading to cell lysis. While antibodies can bind to MHC class II, the primary cytotoxic mechanism highlighted by the biopsy (CD8+ T cells, FasL) is direct cell-mediated cytotoxicity, not CDC. Furthermore, MHC class II is primarily expressed on APCs, not the parenchymal cells (tubules) that are likely being targeted by CD8+ T cells.
- (D) **Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) involving the formation of anti-HLA antibodies leading to endothelial injury and microvascular inflammation.** AMR is characterized by DSAs, endothelial damage, C4d deposition, and microvascular inflammation (capillaritis). While C4d is present, the dominant finding is CD8+ T cell infiltration and FasL expression, pointing towards cellular rejection as the primary driver. AMR typically involves CD4+ T cells and B cells more prominently than CD8+ T cells expressing FasL.
- (E) **Chronic rejection characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes mediated by CD4+ Th2 cells and eosinophils.** Chronic rejection occurs months to years after transplantation and is characterized by fibrosis, graft arteriosclerosis, and loss of graft function. It involves both cellular and humoral immunity, often with a Th2-skewed response and fibrosis. The presentation here is acute (3 weeks post-transplant) with cellular infiltrates and tubular injury, not the chronic changes of fibrosis and vascular sclerosis.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Acute Cellular Rejection (Correct Answer) | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) | Chronic Rejection | ADCC (NK Cells) | CDC (Antibodies) |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :---------------- | :-------------- | :--------------- |
| **Primary Mediators**   | CD8+ T cells (CTLs), CD4+ T cells         | Antibodies (DSAs), CD4+ T cells, B cells | CD4+ T cells, Fibroblasts | NK cells        | Antibodies       |
| **Target Cells**        | Graft parenchymal cells (MHC I), APCs (MHC II) | Endothelial cells                 | Endothelial cells, Parenchyma | Antibody-coated cells | Antibody-coated cells |
| **Key Mechanisms**      | Direct cytotoxicity (FasL, perforin/granzymes), IFN-γ | Complement activation, ADCC, Endothelial injury | Fibrosis, Vascular changes | FcR engagement   | Complement cascade |
| **Typical Timing**      | Days to months post-transplant              | Days to months post-transplant      | Months to years  | Variable        | Variable         |
| **Biopsy Findings**     | Lymphocyte infiltrates (tubules/interstitium), Tubular injury | Microvascular inflammation (capillaritis), C4d, Endothelialitis | Fibrosis, Vascular sclerosis | Variable        | Variable         |
| **Key Clue in Vignette** | CD8+ T cells, FasL expression              | DSAs, C4d, Endothelialitis        | Fibrosis          | NK cells        | Complement activation |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute cellular rejection is primarily mediated by T cells, particularly CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs), recognizing alloantigens presented by MHC class I molecules on graft cells. CTLs induce apoptosis via FasL/Fas interaction or release of cytotoxic granules. Differentiating acute cellular rejection from antibody-mediated rejection is crucial, often relying on biopsy findings (lymphocyte infiltrates vs. microvascular inflammation/endothelialitis) and serology (presence of DSAs). C4d deposition can be seen in both but is more characteristic of AMR. Chronic rejection involves fibrosis and vascular changes occurring over a longer period.

<a id='question-114'></a>

### Question 114: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 14 (killing.)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the mechanisms of graft rejection, specifically the role of cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) and their recognition of foreign MHC class I molecules on graft cells, leading to graft cell killing. (理解移植物排斥机制，特别是细胞毒性T淋巴细胞（CTLs）的作用及其识别移植物细胞上外源性MHC I类分子，导致移植物细胞杀伤。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor who was HLA-A*02:01 positive and HLA-B*07:02 positive. The recipient is HLA-A*03:01 positive and HLA-B*08:01 positive. He is started on standard immunosuppressive therapy including cyclosporine, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals diffuse interstitial inflammation with infiltration of mononuclear cells, tubular epithelial cell necrosis, and evidence of endothelial injury in peritubular capillaries. Immunofluorescence staining shows deposition of C4d in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the peripheral blood shows a significant increase in CD8+ T cells expressing granzyme B and perforin. Further analysis reveals that these CD8+ T cells recognize peptides presented by HLA-B*07:02 molecules.

Which of the following mechanisms is MOST directly responsible for the observed graft injury in this patient?

(A) Antibody-mediated complement-dependent cytotoxicity (ADCC) against donor endothelial cells.
(B) Direct cell-mediated cytotoxicity by recipient CD8+ T cells recognizing donor MHC class I molecules.
(C) Antibody-mediated complement-dependent injury (AMCDI) targeting donor MHC class I molecules.
(D) Antibody-mediated cellular dysfunction (AMCF) leading to tubular epithelial cell apoptosis.
(E) Macrophage-mediated phagocytosis of donor renal tubular cells coated with IgG.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient.
- **HLA Mismatch:** Donor HLA-A*02:01/B*07:02, Recipient HLA-A*03:01/B*08:01. This indicates a mismatch at the HLA-B locus (B*07:02 vs B*08:01).
- **Clinical Presentation:** 3 weeks post-transplant, fever, flank pain, elevated creatinine – suggestive of acute rejection.
- **Renal Biopsy Findings:** Diffuse interstitial inflammation, mononuclear cell infiltration, tubular epithelial cell necrosis, endothelial injury in peritubular capillaries. These are classic signs of acute cellular rejection (T-cell mediated) and possibly acute antibody-mediated rejection (AMR).
- **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. C4d is a marker of complement activation, strongly suggesting an antibody-mediated component (AMR).
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD8+ T cells expressing granzyme B and perforin. Granzyme B and perforin are key effector molecules used by cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) to kill target cells.
- **T Cell Specificity:** CD8+ T cells recognize peptides presented by HLA-B*07:02 molecules. Since the donor expresses HLA-B*07:02 and the recipient does not, this indicates that the recipient's CD8+ T cells are recognizing foreign MHC class I molecules on the donor graft cells.

**Synthesis:** The patient presents with signs of acute rejection (clinical symptoms, biopsy findings). The biopsy shows features consistent with both cellular rejection (T-cell infiltrate, tubular necrosis) and antibody-mediated rejection (C4d deposition). However, the flow cytometry data specifically points to the activation and effector function (granzyme B, perforin) of recipient CD8+ T cells that recognize donor HLA-B*07:02 molecules. This directly implicates cell-mediated cytotoxicity as a major mechanism of graft injury. The C4d deposition suggests a concurrent or preceding antibody-mediated process, but the question asks for the mechanism *most directly* responsible for the observed graft injury, and the activated CTLs are a clear and direct cause of cell killing.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Graft Rejection Types:**
    - **Hyperacute Rejection:** Pre-formed antibodies against donor ABO or HLA antigens, occurs within minutes to hours.
    - **Acute Rejection:** Occurs days to weeks post-transplant. Can be T-cell mediated (cellular rejection) or antibody-mediated (AMR).
        - **Cellular Rejection:** Primarily mediated by recipient T cells (CD4+ helper T cells and CD8+ cytotoxic T cells) recognizing donor MHC molecules (alloantigens). CD8+ T cells directly kill graft cells expressing foreign MHC class I. CD4+ T cells help activate CD8+ T cells and macrophages.
        - **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** Mediated by recipient antibodies (pre-formed or de novo) against donor HLA or non-HLA antigens. Mechanisms include:
            - **AMCDI (Antibody-Mediated Complement-Dependent Injury):** Antibodies bind to donor endothelial cells, activate complement, leading to endothelial injury, inflammation, and thrombosis. C4d deposition is a key marker.
            - **ADCC (Antibody-Dependent Cell-Mediated Cytotoxicity):** Antibodies bind to donor cells, which are then killed by NK cells or other effector cells expressing Fc receptors.
            - **AMCF (Antibody-Mediated Cellular Dysfunction):** Antibodies bind to receptors on graft cells (e.g., ACE2 in kidneys), blocking their function or causing internalization, leading to cellular dysfunction rather than direct cell death.
    - **Chronic Rejection:** Occurs months to years post-transplant, characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes (graft arteriosclerosis). Involves both cellular and humoral immunity.
- **Cell-Mediated Cytotoxicity:** CD8+ T cells (CTLs) recognize foreign MHC class I molecules presenting peptides on the surface of target cells (e.g., graft cells). Upon recognition, CTLs form an immunological synapse, release cytotoxic granules containing perforin (forms pores in the target cell membrane) and granzyme B (enters the target cell via pores and activates caspases, leading to apoptosis).
- **MHC Class I Presentation:** MHC class I molecules present endogenous peptides (e.g., viral peptides, self-peptides, or in this case, foreign peptides from the donor graft) to CD8+ T cells.
- **Granzyme B and Perforin:** Key effector molecules of CTLs responsible for inducing apoptosis in target cells.

##### 3. Role of C4d Deposition
- C4d is a stable breakdown product of the complement C4 protein. Its deposition in peritubular capillaries is a strong histological marker for antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), specifically indicating complement activation. While present in this case, the question asks for the mechanism *most directly* responsible for the graft injury, and the activated CTLs provide a direct mechanism for cell killing. AMR often coexists with cellular rejection.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Antibody-mediated complement-dependent cytotoxicity (ADCC) against donor endothelial cells.** ADCC involves antibodies binding to target cells and NK cells (or other Fc receptor-bearing cells) killing the target. While AMR is present (indicated by C4d), the primary mechanism highlighted by the flow cytometry data is T-cell mediated killing. ADCC is a specific mechanism of AMR, but not the most direct explanation for the observed CTL activity.
- (C) **Antibody-mediated complement-dependent injury (AMCDI) targeting donor MHC class I molecules.** AMCDI involves antibodies binding to donor endothelial cells, activating complement, and causing endothelial damage. C4d deposition supports this mechanism. However, the question emphasizes the activated CD8+ T cells recognizing donor MHC class I and expressing cytotoxic molecules. While AMR is present, the direct killing mechanism described is T-cell mediated. AMCDI primarily affects endothelial cells, whereas the biopsy shows tubular epithelial cell necrosis, which is more directly targeted by CTLs.
- (D) **Antibody-mediated cellular dysfunction (AMCF) leading to tubular epithelial cell apoptosis.** AMCF involves antibodies binding to functional receptors on graft cells, impairing their function. This typically leads to dysfunction rather than direct cell death via apoptosis, although severe dysfunction can lead to cell death. The primary mechanism described is direct cytotoxic killing by T cells.
- (E) **Macrophage-mediated phagocytosis of donor renal tubular cells coated with IgG.** This describes a form of antibody-dependent phagocytosis. While macrophages can contribute to graft rejection, the key finding is the activation of CD8+ T cells expressing cytotoxic molecules, indicating direct cell-mediated killing as the primary mechanism highlighted in the vignette.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Correct Answer (B) - Cell-Mediated Cytotoxicity | Distractor (A) - ADCC | Distractor (C) - AMCDI | Distractor (D) - AMCF | Distractor (E) - Phagocytosis |
|-------------------------|---------------------------------------------------|-----------------------|-----------------------|-----------------------|--------------------------------|
| **Primary Mediator**     | CD8+ T cells (CTLs)                               | Antibodies, NK cells  | Antibodies, Complement| Antibodies            | Antibodies, Macrophages        |
| **Target Recognition**   | Donor MHC Class I peptides                       | Donor cells coated with Ab | Donor endothelial cells coated with Ab | Functional receptors on donor cells | Donor cells coated with Ab |
| **Mechanism of Injury**  | Perforin/Granzyme-mediated apoptosis              | NK cell cytotoxicity  | Complement-mediated endothelial damage | Receptor blockade/internalization | Phagocytosis by macrophages |
| **Key Markers**          | CD8+ T cells, Granzyme B, Perforin, MHC mismatch  | IgG, NK cells         | C4d, IgG              | IgG                   | IgG, Macrophages               |
| **Biopsy Findings**      | Tubular necrosis, T cell infiltrate              | Endothelial injury    | Endothelial injury    | Cellular dysfunction  | Cellular debris                 |
| **Vignette Support**     | Strong (Flow cytometry data)                     | Weak (C4d present)     | Moderate (C4d present) | Weak                  | Weak                            |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The key takeaway is differentiating the mechanisms of acute graft rejection. While antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) is suggested by C4d deposition, the direct evidence of activated CD8+ T cells expressing cytotoxic molecules (granzyme B, perforin) recognizing donor MHC class I points to cell-mediated cytotoxicity as the most direct mechanism of graft cell killing in this scenario. Remember that acute rejection can involve both cellular and humoral components. Differentiating between T-cell mediated killing (CTLs) and antibody-mediated mechanisms (ADCC, AMCDI, AMCF) is crucial.
