<a id='question-115'></a>

### Question 115: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 15 (Interferons)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of interferons (specifically IFN-γ) in cell-mediated immunity, particularly in the context of macrophage activation and defense against intracellular pathogens, and how their dysregulation can contribute to graft rejection or susceptibility to infection. (理解干扰素（特别是IFN-γ）在细胞介导免疫中的作用，特别是在巨噬细胞激活和防御细胞内病原体方面的作用，以及其失调如何导致移植物排斥或感染易感性。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with a history of chronic granulomatous disease (CGD) undergoes an allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (HSCT) from an HLA-matched unrelated donor for severe, recurrent bacterial and fungal infections. The conditioning regimen includes fludarabine and cyclophosphamide. Post-transplant, he develops a severe, disseminated infection characterized by multiple abscesses in the liver, lungs, and spleen. Cultures from these abscesses grow *Aspergillus fumigatus*. Despite treatment with voriconazole, the patient's condition deteriorates rapidly. Serum levels of interferon-gamma (IFN-γ) are measured and found to be significantly elevated. Further investigation reveals that the donor's macrophages exhibit impaired phagocytosis and killing of *Aspergillus fumigatus* despite normal expression of Fc receptors and complement receptors.

Which of the following mechanisms BEST explains the impaired macrophage function observed in this patient?

(A) Defective production of reactive oxygen species (ROS) due to mutations in NADPH oxidase subunits.
(B) Impaired activation of macrophages by IFN-γ due to a defect in the IFN-γ receptor signaling pathway.
(C) Reduced expression of mannose receptors on macrophages, leading to decreased opsonization of *Aspergillus*.
(D) Inhibition of macrophage phagocytosis by immunosuppressive drugs used in the conditioning regimen.
(E) Increased production of IL-10 by regulatory T cells (Tregs), suppressing macrophage activation.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male with Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD) undergoing HSCT. CGD is characterized by defective NADPH oxidase, leading to impaired ROS production by phagocytes.
- **Procedure:** Allogeneic HSCT. This introduces immune cells from a donor, potentially leading to Graft-versus-Host Disease (GVHD) or graft rejection, but also providing functional immune cells.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Severe, disseminated *Aspergillus fumigatus* infection post-HSCT, unresponsive to voriconazole. *Aspergillus* is an opportunistic fungus often causing invasive disease in immunocompromised hosts.
- **Key Lab Finding:** Elevated serum IFN-γ levels. IFN-γ is a crucial cytokine for activating macrophages, especially against intracellular pathogens like *Aspergillus*.
- **Key Functional Finding:** Donor macrophages exhibit impaired phagocytosis and killing of *Aspergillus* despite normal expression of Fc and complement receptors. This points to a defect in the *activation* pathway of macrophages, rather than the initial recognition/opsonization steps.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism explaining the impaired macrophage function.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **CGD:** The patient's underlying CGD involves mutations in genes encoding subunits of the NADPH oxidase complex (e.g., *CYBB*, *CYBA*, *NCF1*, *NCF2*, *NCF4*). This prevents the generation of superoxide radicals (O2-), which are essential for killing phagocytosed microbes. However, the HSCT provides donor cells with functional NADPH oxidase.
- **IFN-γ and Macrophage Activation:** IFN-γ is a key cytokine produced primarily by Th1 cells and NK cells. It binds to the IFN-γ receptor (IFNGR) on macrophages, initiating a signaling cascade (JAK-STAT pathway). This leads to the upregulation of genes involved in macrophage activation, including those encoding enzymes for ROS production (like NADPH oxidase), nitric oxide synthase (iNOS), and lysosomal enzymes.
- **The Paradox:** The patient has CGD but receives donor cells with functional NADPH oxidase. The *Aspergillus* infection persists despite voriconazole, and donor macrophages fail to kill the fungus effectively. Serum IFN-γ is high, suggesting an attempt to activate macrophages. The defect lies in the *response* of the donor macrophages to IFN-γ.
- **Mechanism:** The most likely explanation is that the donor's macrophages have a defect in the IFN-γ receptor signaling pathway. This prevents IFN-γ from effectively activating the macrophages, even though IFN-γ levels are high. This defect would impair the upregulation of NADPH oxidase (even in donor cells, potentially due to subtle genetic variations or epigenetic modifications) and other microbicidal mechanisms needed to kill *Aspergillus*. The normal expression of Fc and complement receptors indicates that the initial recognition and opsonization steps are intact.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Defective production of reactive oxygen species (ROS) due to mutations in NADPH oxidase subunits. This describes the patient's *underlying* condition (CGD). However, the HSCT provides donor cells with functional NADPH oxidase. The problem is not the *production* of ROS by the donor cells, but their *activation* to produce ROS effectively against *Aspergillus*. The vignette explicitly states the donor macrophages have impaired killing despite normal Fc/complement receptors, pointing away from a primary defect in recognition/opsonization and towards a defect in activation.
- (C): Reduced expression of mannose receptors on macrophages, leading to decreased opsonization of *Aspergillus*. Mannose receptors are involved in recognizing carbohydrate patterns on microbes. Reduced expression would impair opsonization and phagocytosis. However, the vignette states that Fc and complement receptors are normally expressed, suggesting that initial recognition/opsonization pathways are functional. The primary defect is in the *killing* mechanism *after* phagocytosis, which is typically IFN-γ dependent.
- (D): Inhibition of macrophage phagocytosis by immunosuppressive drugs used in the conditioning regimen. While immunosuppressants can affect macrophage function, the vignette highlights a specific defect in *killing* after phagocytosis, coupled with high IFN-γ levels, suggesting a targeted failure in IFN-γ signaling rather than a general immunosuppressive effect. Furthermore, the normal expression of Fc/complement receptors argues against a general inhibition of phagocytosis.
- (E): Increased production of IL-10 by regulatory T cells (Tregs), suppressing macrophage activation. IL-10 is an immunosuppressive cytokine that can inhibit macrophage activation. While Tregs are present post-HSCT and can contribute to GVHD or graft failure, the vignette specifically points to impaired IFN-γ *signaling* within the macrophages themselves, evidenced by the failure to activate despite high IFN-γ levels and normal receptor expression (implying receptor binding occurs but downstream signaling fails). This makes a defect in the IFN-γ receptor pathway a more direct explanation for the observed phenotype.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                     | Correct Answer (B) - IFN-γ Receptor Defect | (A) CGD (Patient's Baseline) | (C) Mannose Receptor Defect | (D) Immunosuppressant Effect | (E) IL-10 Production |
| :-------------------------- | :----------------------------------------- | :-------------------------- | :-------------------------- | :-------------------------- | :------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**          | IFN-γ signaling pathway                    | NADPH oxidase               | Mannose receptor expression | General immune suppression | IL-10 production     |
| **Affected Cell**           | Donor Macrophage                            | Patient's Phagocytes        | Macrophage                  | Multiple immune cells       | Tregs                |
| **IFN-γ Levels**            | High (attempting activation)                | N/A                         | N/A                         | N/A                         | N/A                  |
| **Macrophage Function**      | Impaired activation/killing                | Impaired ROS production     | Impaired opsonization/phagocytosis | General suppression        | Suppressed activation |
| **Receptor Expression (Fc/CR)** | Normal                                     | Normal                      | Normal                      | Normal                      | Normal               |
| **Pathogen Susceptibility** | Intracellular (e.g., *Aspergillus*, Mycobacteria) | Catalase-positive organisms | Opsonization-dependent pathogens | Broadened susceptibility | Broadened suppression |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
IFN-γ is critical for macrophage activation, particularly against intracellular pathogens. Defects in the IFN-γ receptor or downstream signaling (JAK-STAT pathway) lead to impaired macrophage function and increased susceptibility to infections like *Aspergillus* and mycobacteria, even with normal phagocytosis. This contrasts with CGD (impaired ROS production) and mannosidosis (impaired mannose receptor function). Remember that high IFN-γ levels in the context of ineffective macrophage killing strongly suggest a defect in IFN-γ signaling.

---

<a id='question-116'></a>

### Question 116: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 16 (increase)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying graft rejection, specifically the role of T cells (CD4+ and CD8+) and antibodies in acute cellular and antibody-mediated rejection, and differentiating them from other causes of graft dysfunction. (理解移植物排斥的免疫机制，特别是T细胞（CD4+和CD8+）和抗体在急性细胞介导和抗体介导的排斥中的作用，并将其与其他移植物功能障碍的原因区分开来。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an unknown HLA match. He is started on standard immunosuppression including tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and graft tenderness. Serum creatinine rises from a baseline of 1.2 mg/dL to 4.5 mg/dL. Urinalysis shows proteinuria and hematuria. A renal biopsy is performed. Light microscopy reveals interstitial edema, inflammatory cell infiltrates, and tubular injury. Immunofluorescence microscopy shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the biopsy tissue shows a significant increase in CD3+ and CD8+ T cells infiltrating the graft interstitium, particularly around tubules and small vessels. Serum levels of donor-specific antibodies (DSA) are negative. Which of the following is the MOST likely mechanism responsible for this patient's graft dysfunction?

(A) Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) due to pre-formed DSAs.
(B) Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) due to de novo DSAs.
(C) Acute cellular rejection (ACR) mediated primarily by CD4+ T helper cells.
(D) Acute cellular rejection (ACR) mediated primarily by CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs).
(E) Chronic rejection characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, ESRD due to diabetic nephropathy, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for potential graft rejection.
*   **Immunosuppression:** Standard triple therapy (tacrolimus, mycophenolate, prednisone) is used, but rejection can still occur.
*   **Timeline:** 3 weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is typical for acute rejection episodes (both cellular and antibody-mediated).
*   **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, graft tenderness. These are classic signs of acute graft dysfunction, often associated with rejection.
*   **Laboratory Findings:** Rising creatinine (4.5 mg/dL), proteinuria, hematuria. Indicate acute kidney injury and graft damage.
*   **Biopsy Findings (Light Microscopy):** Interstitial edema, inflammatory cell infiltrates, tubular injury. Suggests inflammation and damage within the graft parenchyma.
*   **Biopsy Findings (Immunofluorescence):** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. C4d is a marker of complement activation, often seen in antibody-mediated rejection, but can also be present in ACR. Its presence here is somewhat ambiguous but doesn't rule out ACR.
*   **Biopsy Findings (Flow Cytometry):** Increased CD3+ and CD8+ T cells infiltrating the graft interstitium, particularly around tubules and small vessels. This is a hallmark of acute cellular rejection. The prominence of CD8+ cells points towards direct cytotoxicity.
*   **Serology:** Negative donor-specific antibodies (DSA). This finding makes antibody-mediated rejection due to pre-formed or de novo DSAs less likely, although DSA testing can have limitations.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely mechanism.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** This is a T-cell mediated process.
    *   **Mechanism:** Recipient T cells (both CD4+ helper T cells and CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes - CTLs) recognize donor HLA antigens presented on graft cells as foreign.
    *   **CD4+ T cells:** Recognize HLA class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells (APCs) within the graft and release cytokines (e.g., IFN-γ, TNF-α) that activate macrophages and recruit other inflammatory cells, contributing to inflammation and tissue damage. They also help activate CD8+ T cells.
    *   **CD8+ T cells:** Recognize HLA class I molecules on graft cells (including tubular epithelial cells and endothelial cells) and directly kill these cells via cytotoxic mechanisms (e.g., perforin/granzyme release, Fas/FasL interaction).
    *   **Histopathology:** Characterized by interstitial infiltrates of lymphocytes (predominantly T cells), endothelialitis (inflammation of blood vessel endothelium), and tubulitis (inflammation of tubules).
    *   **Flow Cytometry:** Shows increased numbers of CD3+ (pan-T cell marker) and CD8+ T cells within the graft tissue.
*   **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** This is mediated by antibodies (DSAs) directed against donor HLA antigens.
    *   **Mechanism:** DSAs bind to donor HLA antigens on graft endothelial cells, activating complement and recruiting inflammatory cells (e.g., neutrophils, macrophages). This leads to endothelial injury, microvascular inflammation (glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis), thrombosis, and ultimately graft ischemia and damage.
    *   **Histopathology:** Characterized by microvascular inflammation (glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis), C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries, and sometimes thrombosis.
    *   **Serology:** Presence of DSAs is a key diagnostic feature.
*   **Chronic Rejection:** Occurs months to years after transplantation. Characterized by progressive fibrosis, vascular changes (e.g., transplant glomerulopathy, intimal arteritis), and gradual loss of graft function. Not consistent with the acute presentation (3 weeks) and biopsy findings described.

In this case, the biopsy shows significant infiltration of CD8+ T cells, a hallmark of ACR. While C4d deposition is present (often associated with AMR), the negative DSA levels and the prominent CD8+ T cell infiltrate strongly point towards ACR as the primary mechanism. CD8+ CTLs are particularly important in causing direct damage to graft parenchymal cells (tubules and vessels).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) due to pre-formed DSAs:** Incorrect. Pre-formed DSAs are antibodies present *before* transplantation, often due to prior sensitization (e.g., previous transplant, pregnancy, blood transfusion). While they can cause hyperacute or acute AMR, the patient received a kidney from a deceased donor with an *unknown* HLA match, and the vignette doesn't mention prior sensitization. More importantly, the serum DSA levels are explicitly stated as *negative*. AMR requires the presence of DSAs.
- (B) **Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) due to de novo DSAs:** Incorrect. De novo DSAs develop *after* transplantation. While possible, the negative DSA levels make this unlikely. Furthermore, the biopsy findings, particularly the prominent CD8+ T cell infiltrate, are more characteristic of ACR than AMR. C4d deposition can be seen in both ACR and AMR, but it's considered more specific for AMR. However, in the context of negative DSAs and strong evidence of T-cell infiltration, ACR is favored.
- (C) **Acute cellular rejection (ACR) mediated primarily by CD4+ T helper cells:** Incorrect. While CD4+ T cells play a role in ACR by releasing cytokines and activating other immune cells, the flow cytometry data specifically highlights a significant increase in *CD8+* T cells. CD8+ CTLs are the primary mediators of direct cytotoxicity against graft cells in ACR, making them the dominant cell type in this scenario. The question asks for the *most likely* mechanism, and the CD8+ predominance points to CD8+ T cell-mediated ACR.
- (E) **Chronic rejection characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes:** Incorrect. Chronic rejection is a late complication occurring months to years post-transplant. The patient is only 3 weeks post-transplant, and the biopsy findings (acute inflammation, tubular injury, interstitial infiltrates) are characteristic of acute rejection, not the chronic changes of fibrosis and vascular sclerosis.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR) | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) | Chronic Rejection |
| :------------------ | :----------------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :---------------- |
| **Timeline**        | Days to months                 | Days to months                     | Months to years   |
| **Mediator**        | T cells (CD4+, CD8+)           | Antibodies (DSAs)                  | T cells, Abs, Fibrosis |
| **Key Cells**       | CD3+, CD8+ T cells             | B cells, Plasma cells, Neutrophils | Macrophages, Fibroblasts |
| **Histology**       | Tubulitis, Endothelitis, Interstitial Infiltrates | Microvascular Inflammation (glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis), C4d deposition, Thrombosis | Fibrosis, Vascular changes (transplant glomerulopathy, intimal arteritis) |
| **DSA Status**      | Negative                       | Positive                           | Variable          |
| **Flow Cytometry**  | Increased CD3+, CD8+ T cells   | Not diagnostic                     | Not diagnostic    |
| **C4d Deposition**  | Possible, less specific        | Characteristic                     | Possible          |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute graft rejection is broadly classified into ACR and AMR. ACR is T-cell mediated, characterized by T-cell infiltrates (especially CD8+ CTLs) and tubulitis/endothelitis. AMR is antibody-mediated, characterized by microvascular inflammation, C4d deposition, and the presence of DSAs. Differentiating between ACR and AMR is crucial for management. Key differentiating features include the presence/absence of DSAs, C4d deposition, and the predominant cell type infiltrating the graft (T cells vs. neutrophils/macrophages in microvasculature). This case highlights the importance of flow cytometry in identifying the dominant cell type (CD8+ T cells) in ACR, even when C4d is present.

<a id='question-117'></a>

### Question 117: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 17 (expression)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of specific cell surface molecules (CD40L/CD154) in T-cell dependent B-cell activation and antibody production, particularly in the context of graft rejection and the impact of genetic deficiencies in these molecules. (理解特定细胞表面分子 (CD40L/CD154) 在 T 细胞依赖性 B 细胞活化和抗体生成中的作用，特别是在移植物排斥和这些分子遗传缺陷的影响方面。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy, previously healthy, underwent a life-saving liver transplant for biliary atresia 6 months ago. He has been on standard immunosuppressive therapy (tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, prednisone). Despite this, he presents with recurrent, severe bacterial infections, including *Pneumocystis jirovecii* pneumonia, *Staphylococcus aureus* sepsis, and multiple episodes of otitis media caused by encapsulated bacteria like *Streptococcus pneumoniae* and *Haemophilus influenzae*. His immunoglobulin levels are markedly reduced: IgG 150 mg/dL (normal >400 mg/dL), IgA <10 mg/dL (normal >50 mg/dL), and IgM 20 mg/dL (normal >50 mg/dL). T-cell counts (CD3+) are normal, but B-cell counts (CD19+) are normal. Flow cytometry reveals normal expression of CD20 and CD21 on B cells, but B cells fail to proliferate in response to mitogenic stimuli (e.g., LPS, anti-IgM) *in vitro*. His mother reports a history of similar severe infections in her firstborn son, who died in infancy. Genetic testing reveals a homozygous mutation in the *CD40LG* gene. Which of the following cellular interactions is most likely impaired in this patient, leading to his clinical presentation?

(A) Interaction between CD4+ T cells and antigen-presenting cells (APCs) via MHC class II molecules.
(B) Interaction between CD8+ T cells and infected host cells via MHC class I molecules.
(C) Interaction between activated CD4+ T cells and B cells via CD40L and CD40.
(D) Interaction between NK cells and target cells via KIRs and MHC class I molecules.
(E) Interaction between macrophages and T cells via CD28 and B7 molecules.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 3-year-old boy with a history of liver transplant 6 months ago. This immediately flags potential complications related to transplantation, including immunosuppression and rejection, but also potential underlying immune deficiencies exacerbated by the transplant or immunosuppression.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Recurrent, severe bacterial infections, including opportunistic infections (*Pneumocystis jirovecii*) and infections with encapsulated bacteria (*S. pneumoniae*, *H. influenzae*). This pattern strongly suggests a defect in humoral immunity (antibody production), as antibodies are crucial for clearing encapsulated bacteria and preventing opportunistic infections.
- **Immunological Findings:** Markedly reduced IgG, IgA, and IgM levels (panhypogammaglobulinemia). Normal T-cell counts (CD3+) but normal B-cell counts (CD19+). This indicates that B cells are present but are not producing antibodies effectively. Normal CD20 and CD21 expression on B cells rules out defects in B cell development or maturation markers. Failure of B cells to proliferate *in vitro* in response to mitogens suggests a defect in T-cell help required for B-cell activation.
- **Family History:** Similar severe infections and early death in a previous sibling points towards a genetic immunodeficiency.
- **Genetic Testing:** Homozygous mutation in the *CD40LG* gene. This gene encodes CD40 Ligand (CD40L, also known as CD154), a protein expressed on activated T cells.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the impaired cellular interaction responsible for the clinical picture.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **CD40L/CD40 Pathway:** CD40L is expressed primarily on activated CD4+ T helper cells. CD40 is expressed on various cells, including B cells, macrophages, dendritic cells, and endothelial cells. The interaction between CD40L on activated T cells and CD40 on B cells is crucial for T-cell dependent B-cell activation.
- **T-cell Dependent B-cell Activation:** When a B cell encounters its specific antigen, it internalizes and processes it, presenting peptides on MHC class II molecules to CD4+ T helper cells. If the T cell recognizes the peptide-MHC complex via its T-cell receptor (TCR) and receives co-stimulatory signals (e.g., CD28-B7), the T cell becomes activated. The activated T cell then expresses CD40L. The CD40L on the T cell binds to CD40 on the B cell. This CD40-CD40L interaction, along with cytokines secreted by the T cell (e.g., IL-4, IL-5, IL-21), provides the necessary "help" for the B cell to undergo isotype switching (e.g., from IgM to IgG, IgA, IgE), affinity maturation, and differentiation into plasma cells and memory B cells.
- **X-linked Hyper-IgM Syndrome (HIGM1):** Mutations in the *CD40LG* gene cause X-linked Hyper-IgM syndrome (HIGM1). Patients with HIGM1 have normal or elevated levels of IgM but severely reduced levels of IgG, IgA, and IgE. This is because B cells can undergo initial activation and produce IgM (T-independent process), but they cannot receive the necessary T-cell help via the CD40-CD40L interaction to switch isotypes or differentiate effectively into plasma cells. The clinical presentation includes recurrent pyogenic bacterial infections (especially encapsulated organisms), opportunistic infections (like *Pneumocystis jirovecii*), and autoimmune manifestations. The patient in the vignette has panhypogammaglobulinemia (low IgG, IgA, IgM), which is atypical for classic HIGM1 (usually high IgM, low IgG/IgA/IgE). However, the *CD40LG* mutation is the definitive clue. While classic HIGM1 presents with high IgM, some variants or atypical presentations can show low IgM along with low IgG/IgA, especially if B cell development or survival is also affected secondarily. The core defect remains the inability of T cells to provide CD40L-mediated help to B cells. The question asks for the *impaired interaction* caused by the CD40LG mutation.
- **Relevance to Transplant:** Patients with underlying immunodeficiencies like HIGM1 are at increased risk of infections post-transplant, especially when on immunosuppression. The immunosuppression further impairs immune function, exacerbating the underlying defect.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Interaction between CD4+ T cells and antigen-presenting cells (APCs) via MHC class II molecules. This interaction is crucial for T-cell activation itself. While impaired T-cell activation could indirectly affect B-cell help, the primary defect in CD40LG deficiency is the *delivery* of help to B cells, not the initial activation of the T cell. T cells in HIGM1 patients *can* be activated by APCs, but they cannot effectively help B cells due to the lack of CD40L.
- (B) Interaction between CD8+ T cells and infected host cells via MHC class I molecules. This interaction is central to cytotoxic T lymphocyte (CTL) responses against intracellular pathogens (viruses, intracellular bacteria). It is not directly involved in T-cell dependent B-cell activation or antibody production. Defects here typically lead to susceptibility to viral infections.
- (D) Interaction between NK cells and target cells via KIRs and MHC class I molecules. This interaction regulates NK cell activity. NK cells are part of the innate immune system and are not directly involved in the CD40-CD40L dependent pathway for B-cell activation. Defects here typically lead to increased susceptibility to viral infections or autoimmune diseases.
- (E) Interaction between macrophages and T cells via CD28 and B7 molecules. This interaction (CD28 on T cells binding B7 on APCs/macrophages) is a crucial co-stimulatory signal required for T-cell activation. While important for T-cell function, it is not the specific defect caused by a *CD40LG* mutation. The primary problem in HIGM1 is the failure of activated T cells to express functional CD40L to interact with B cells.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | CD40LG Deficiency (HIGM1)                                  | MHC Class II Deficiency (Bare Lymphocyte Syndrome) | CD40 Deficiency (HIGM3)                                  | DiGeorge Syndrome (CD4+ T cell defect)                  |
| :---------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | Lack of CD40L on activated T cells                          | Lack of MHC Class II on APCs                         | Lack of CD40 on B cells, macrophages, dendritic cells       | Defective T cell development (Thymic hypoplasia)          |
| **Cell Type Affected**  | Activated CD4+ T cells                                      | APCs (B cells, macrophages, dendritic cells)         | B cells, macrophages, dendritic cells                      | CD4+ T cells                                             |
| **Key Interaction Impaired** | CD40L (T cell) - CD40 (B cell)                             | TCR-MHC Class II (T cell)                             | CD40L (T cell) - CD40 (B cell)                             | TCR-MHC Class II (T cell)                               |
| **Immunoglobulin Levels** | Typically High IgM, Low IgG/IgA/IgE (Classic HIGM1)         | Low all Ig classes                                   | Typically High IgM, Low IgG/IgA/IgE (Classic HIGM3)         | Variable, often low IgG, IgA, IgE due to lack of T help |
| **Clinical Features**   | Recurrent bacterial infections, *Pneumocystis*, autoimmunity | Severe infections early in life, failure to thrive   | Recurrent bacterial infections, *Pneumocystis*, autoimmunity | Recurrent infections, hypocalcemia, cardiac defects      |
| **Genetic Basis**       | X-linked (*CD40LG*)                                         | Autosomal recessive (*HLA-DRA, -DRB, -DQ*)           | Autosomal recessive (*TNFSF5*)                             | Autosomal dominant/recessive (*TBX1*)                    |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The *CD40LG* gene encodes CD40L (CD154), expressed on activated T cells, which binds to CD40 on B cells, providing essential help for isotype switching and antibody production. Mutations cause X-linked Hyper-IgM syndrome (HIGM1), characterized by impaired T-cell dependent B-cell activation, leading to low IgG, IgA, IgE, and often high IgM, with susceptibility to pyogenic and opportunistic infections. Differentiate this from other primary immunodeficiencies affecting T cells (e.g., DiGeorge), B cells (e.g., XLA), or specific interactions (e.g., MHC Class II deficiency, CD40 deficiency).

<a id='question-118'></a>

### Question 118: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 18 (ecules)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of complement activation, specifically the membrane attack complex (MAC) formation via C5b-9 (ecules), in graft rejection, particularly hyperacute rejection, and the mechanisms by which pathogens can evade complement-mediated killing. (理解补体激活，特别是通过C5b-9 (ecules)形成的膜攻击复合物 (MAC) 在移植物排斥中的作用，特别是超急性排斥，以及病原体逃避补体介导杀伤的机制。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 58-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor. The patient's pre-transplant panel reactive antibody (PRA) screen was negative. The surgery proceeds without complications. However, within minutes of initiating the reperfusion of the donor kidney, the surgical team observes immediate, diffuse thrombosis of the graft vasculature, leading to rapid graft failure and severe hypertension in the recipient. A biopsy of the graft shows fibrin deposition within the glomerular capillaries and peritubular capillaries, along with extensive endothelial cell damage and infiltration by neutrophils. Immunofluorescence staining reveals strong deposition of C3d along the endothelium and within the thrombi. The patient's serum is tested, and it is found to contain pre-formed antibodies against donor-specific HLA class I antigens.

Which of the following mechanisms is most likely responsible for the rapid graft failure observed in this patient?

(A) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells targeting donor endothelial cells.
(B) Formation of immune complexes that deposit in the graft vasculature, activating the classical complement pathway and leading to inflammation.
(C) Activation of the alternative complement pathway by donor microbial products, resulting in C3 convertase formation and opsonization.
(D) Direct binding of pre-formed donor-specific antibodies to donor endothelial cells, activating the classical complement pathway and leading to membrane attack complex (MAC) formation.
(E) T-cell mediated cytotoxicity targeting donor MHC class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells within the graft.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 58-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, receives kidney transplant. This sets the stage for a transplantation scenario.
- **Rapid Graft Failure:** "Within minutes of initiating reperfusion," "immediate, diffuse thrombosis," "rapid graft failure." This points towards a very rapid rejection mechanism, specifically hyperacute rejection.
- **Clinical Signs:** "Severe hypertension." This is a classic sign of hyperacute rejection due to widespread vascular thrombosis and release of vasoactive mediators.
- **Pathology:** "Fibrin deposition within the glomerular capillaries and peritubular capillaries," "extensive endothelial cell damage," "infiltration by neutrophils." These findings indicate widespread vascular injury and inflammation.
- **Immunofluorescence:** "Strong deposition of C3d along the endothelium and within the thrombi." C3d is a breakdown product of C3b, indicating complement activation on the endothelial surface.
- **Serology:** "Pre-formed antibodies against donor-specific HLA class I antigens." This is the crucial finding. Pre-existing antibodies against the donor's HLA antigens are the hallmark of hyperacute rejection.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism responsible for the rapid graft failure.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Hyperacute Rejection:** This type of rejection occurs within minutes to hours after transplantation. It is mediated by pre-existing antibodies in the recipient's serum that recognize donor antigens, primarily HLA class I and class II molecules expressed on endothelial cells.
- **Mechanism:**
    1.  **Antibody Binding:** Pre-formed donor-specific antibodies (DSAs), typically IgG, bind to HLA antigens on the donor endothelial cells.
    2.  **Classical Complement Activation:** The binding of IgG antibodies to the endothelial surface activates the classical complement pathway. The C1 complex binds to the Fc portion of the bound IgG.
    3.  **C3 Convertase Formation:** C1 activates C4 and C2, leading to the formation of the classical C3 convertase (C4b2a).
    4.  **C3b Deposition:** The C3 convertase cleaves C3 into C3a and C3b. C3b covalently binds to the endothelial surface, opsonizing the cells and amplifying the complement cascade.
    5.  **C5 Convertase Formation:** C3b binds to the C3 convertase to form the C5 convertase (C4b2a3b).
    6.  **MAC Formation:** The C5 convertase cleaves C5 into C5a and C5b. C5b initiates the formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC), also known as the terminal complement complex (TCC) or ecules (C5b-9). The MAC inserts into the endothelial cell membrane, creating pores that disrupt cell integrity, leading to endothelial cell lysis, platelet activation, coagulation cascade activation (thrombosis), and neutrophil recruitment.
    7.  **Endothelial Damage & Thrombosis:** The combined effects of complement activation, endothelial cell lysis, platelet activation, and coagulation lead to diffuse microvascular thrombosis, ischemia, and rapid graft failure.
- **C3d Deposition:** C3d is a stable fragment of C3b that remains bound to the surface after C3b is cleaved. Its presence confirms complement activation on the endothelium.
- **Key Takeaway:** Hyperacute rejection is driven by pre-existing antibodies binding to donor endothelium, triggering the classical complement pathway and leading to MAC formation and thrombosis.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells targeting donor endothelial cells.** While antibodies can mediate ADCC, NK cells primarily target cells lacking MHC class I expression. Donor endothelial cells express MHC class I. Furthermore, ADCC is typically involved in slower rejection processes or responses to viral infections, not the immediate thrombosis seen in hyperacute rejection. The primary mechanism here is complement activation leading to MAC formation and thrombosis, not direct NK cell lysis.
- (B) **Formation of immune complexes that deposit in the graft vasculature, activating the classical complement pathway and leading to inflammation.** Immune complex deposition typically occurs in tissues distant from the site of antigen exposure (e.g., glomeruli in systemic lupus erythematosus) or in response to soluble antigens. In hyperacute rejection, the antibodies bind directly to antigens (HLA) expressed on the *surface* of the endothelial cells within the graft, not forming circulating immune complexes that deposit. While complement is activated, the initial trigger is direct antibody binding to cell surface antigens, not immune complex deposition.
- (C) **Activation of the alternative complement pathway by donor microbial products, resulting in C3 convertase formation and opsonization.** The alternative pathway is typically activated by microbial surfaces or abnormal host cells. While microbial contamination can occur, the vignette strongly points to an antibody-mediated process due to the presence of pre-formed DSAs and the rapid onset. The classical pathway is activated by antibody binding.
- (E) **T-cell mediated cytotoxicity targeting donor MHC class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells within the graft.** T-cell mediated rejection (acute or chronic) involves T cells recognizing donor MHC molecules (either directly or indirectly) on antigen-presenting cells or graft cells. This process takes days to weeks to develop, not minutes. The rapid onset and the presence of pre-formed antibodies strongly argue against T-cell mediated rejection as the primary cause of *hyperacute* rejection.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Hyperacute Rejection (Correct Answer) | Acute Rejection (T-cell mediated) | Chronic Rejection (T/B cell mediated) | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (Subacute) | Immune Complex Disease |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------ | :-------------------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------- | :------------------- |
| **Timing**              | Minutes to hours                     | Days to weeks                     | Months to years                      | Days to weeks                           | Variable            |
| **Mediator**            | Pre-formed Antibodies (IgG)           | T cells (CD8+, CD4+)              | T cells, B cells, Fibroblasts        | Donor-Specific Antibodies (DSA)         | Immune Complexes    |
| **Target Antigen**      | HLA Class I/II on endothelium         | Donor MHC I/II on APCs/cells      | Donor MHC I/II, Alloantigens          | HLA Class I/II on endothelium           | Soluble Antigens    |
| **Primary Mechanism**   | Complement Activation (MAC), Thrombosis | Cytotoxicity, Inflammation        | Fibrosis, Vascular changes            | Complement Activation (MAC), ADCC, Inflammation | Complement, FcR     |
| **Key Pathology**       | Vascular Thrombosis, Endothelial Damage | Infiltrates, Cell Damage          | Fibrosis, Vascular Thickening         | Endothelial Damage, Thrombosis           | Tissue Deposition    |
| **Complement Role**     | Central (Classical Pathway)            | Minor                             | Minor                                | Central (Classical Pathway)              | Central             |
| **Antibody Type**       | IgG                                   | N/A                               | N/A                                  | IgG                                      | IgG, IgM            |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Hyperacute rejection is a rapid, antibody-mediated process triggered by pre-existing recipient antibodies against donor HLA antigens, primarily activating the classical complement pathway leading to MAC formation, endothelial damage, and thrombosis. Differentiate this from acute (T-cell mediated) and chronic (T/B cell mediated) rejection, which occur over longer time frames, and subacute antibody-mediated rejection, which occurs days to weeks post-transplant. Remember the key role of pre-formed antibodies and complement in hyperacute rejection.

<a id='question-119'></a>

### Question 119: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 19 (graft,)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying acute cellular rejection in solid organ transplantation, specifically the role of T lymphocytes (CD8+ cytotoxic T cells) in directly damaging graft cells, and differentiating it from other types of rejection or infections. (理解实体器官移植中急性细胞性排斥的免疫机制，特别是CD8+细胞毒性T淋巴细胞直接损伤移植细胞的作用，并将其与其他类型的排斥反应或感染区分开来。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease secondary to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an unknown HLA match. He is started on standard immunosuppression with tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals diffuse interstitial edema, inflammatory cell infiltrates primarily composed of lymphocytes, and evidence of tubular epithelial cell necrosis. Immunofluorescence staining shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the biopsy tissue shows a significant increase in CD8+ T cells infiltrating the tubules and interstitium, along with evidence of tubular cell apoptosis. Serum levels of donor-specific antibodies (DSA) are negative.

Which of the following cellular mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for the observed graft injury?

(A) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells recognizing antibody-coated graft cells.
(B) Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) mediated by antibodies binding to graft cell surface antigens and activating the classical complement pathway.
(C) Direct cytotoxicity mediated by CD8+ T lymphocytes recognizing donor MHC class I molecules on graft cells.
(D) Macrophage-mediated inflammation driven by Th2 cytokines (IL-4, IL-5, IL-13) promoting fibrosis.
(E) Neutrophil-mediated acute tubular necrosis (ATN) triggered by ischemia-reperfusion injury during transplantation.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease (ESRD) due to diabetes, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for potential complications post-transplant, including rejection.
- **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus (calcineurin inhibitor), mycophenolate mofetil (purine synthesis inhibitor), and prednisone (corticosteroid). Standard triple therapy, but rejection can still occur.
- **Timeline:** 3 weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is typical for acute rejection episodes.
- **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine. Classic signs of acute kidney graft dysfunction, suggestive of rejection or infection.
- **Biopsy Findings:**
    - Diffuse interstitial edema and inflammatory cell infiltrates (lymphocytes): Indicates inflammation and immune response within the graft.
    - Tubular epithelial cell necrosis: Suggests direct cell damage.
    - C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries: Indicates complement activation, often associated with antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), but can also be seen in cellular rejection.
    - Increased CD8+ T cells infiltrating tubules and interstitium: Strong evidence of cellular immune response targeting graft cells.
    - Tubular cell apoptosis: Consistent with cytotoxic T cell activity.
- **DSA Negative:** Rules out significant antibody-mediated rejection as the primary driver, although C4d deposition can occur in cellular rejection too.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely cellular mechanism responsible for the graft injury.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** This is the most common type of acute rejection in solid organ transplantation. It is primarily mediated by T lymphocytes, specifically CD4+ helper T cells (Th1 phenotype) and CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs).
- **Mechanism of CD8+ T Cell Mediated Rejection:**
    - **Antigen Presentation:** Donor MHC class I molecules (present on all nucleated cells, including graft tubular epithelial cells) are recognized as foreign by the recipient's CD8+ T cells.
    - **T Cell Receptor (TCR) Recognition:** The TCR on the recipient's CD8+ T cell specifically binds to the non-self donor MHC class I-peptide complex presented on the surface of the graft cell.
    - **Cytotoxic Granule Release:** Upon recognition, the CD8+ T cell forms an immunological synapse with the target graft cell and releases cytotoxic granules containing perforin and granzymes.
    - **Perforin:** Forms pores in the target cell membrane.
    - **Granzymes:** Serine proteases that enter the target cell through the perforin pores and activate caspases, initiating the apoptotic pathway.
    - **Fas/FasL Interaction:** CD8+ T cells can also express Fas ligand (FasL), which binds to Fas (CD95) receptors on the target graft cell, triggering apoptosis.
    - **Cytokine Release:** CD8+ T cells also release cytokines like IFN-γ and TNF-α, which further promote inflammation and cell death.
- **Role of CD4+ T cells:** CD4+ T cells (Th1) recognize donor MHC class II molecules (present on antigen-presenting cells like dendritic cells within the graft) and release cytokines (IFN-γ, TNF-α) that activate macrophages and enhance the cytotoxic activity of CD8+ T cells.
- **Biopsy Interpretation:** The biopsy findings (lymphocytic infiltrate, tubular necrosis, CD8+ T cell infiltration, apoptosis) are classic features of acute cellular rejection, particularly T cell-mediated rejection (TCMR). The C4d deposition can be seen in both AMR and TCMR, but the prominent CD8+ T cell infiltrate and negative DSA point strongly towards TCMR.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells:** ADCC involves antibodies binding to target cells, which are then recognized and killed by effector cells (like NK cells) expressing Fc receptors. While antibodies can play a role in rejection (AMR), the vignette emphasizes T cell infiltration (CD8+), negative DSA, and lacks clear evidence of significant antibody involvement as the primary mechanism. NK cells are involved in innate immunity and missing-self recognition, not typically the primary mediators of acute *cellular* rejection in this context.
- (B) **Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) mediated by antibodies:** CDC involves antibodies binding to target cells, activating the classical complement pathway, leading to the formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC) and cell lysis. This is a mechanism of antibody-mediated rejection (AMR). The negative DSA and primary finding of CD8+ T cell infiltrate make this less likely than direct T cell cytotoxicity. While C4d deposition indicates complement activation, it doesn't necessarily mean CDC is the primary cytotoxic mechanism, especially in the context of strong cellular rejection findings.
- (D) **Macrophage-mediated inflammation driven by Th2 cytokines:** Th2 cytokines (IL-4, IL-5, IL-13) are typically associated with allergic responses, parasitic infections, and fibrosis, not the acute cytotoxic rejection seen here. Acute cellular rejection is primarily driven by Th1 cytokines (IFN-γ, TNF-α) which activate macrophages and CD8+ T cells. Macrophages do contribute to inflammation in rejection, but the primary cytotoxic mechanism described is T cell-mediated.
- (E) **Neutrophil-mediated acute tubular necrosis (ATN) triggered by ischemia-reperfusion injury:** Ischemia-reperfusion injury can cause ATN, often involving neutrophils. However, this typically occurs immediately after transplantation during the reperfusion phase. The vignette describes events 3 weeks post-transplant, and the biopsy findings (lymphocytic infiltrate, CD8+ T cells, apoptosis) are characteristic of rejection, not primarily ischemia-reperfusion injury. While ischemia-reperfusion can predispose to rejection, the specific findings point to an immune-mediated process.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Acute Cellular Rejection (TCMR) | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) | Ischemia-Reperfusion Injury | Infection (e.g., CMV) |
|--------------------------|---------------------------------|-----------------------------------|------------------------------|-----------------------|
| **Primary Mediator**     | T lymphocytes (CD8+, CD4+)       | Antibodies, Complement             | Neutrophils, Reactive Oxygen Species | Pathogen, Immune Cells |
| **Timeline**             | Days to weeks post-transplant    | Days to weeks post-transplant    | Immediately post-transplant  | Variable              |
| **Biopsy Findings**      | Lymphocytic infiltrate, CD8+ cells, tubular apoptosis | C4d deposition, endothelialitis, glomerulitis, fibrosis | Neutrophil infiltrate, tubular cell swelling/necrosis | Viral inclusions, inflammation |
| **Key Cytokines**        | IFN-γ, TNF-α                     | -                                 | -                            | Variable              |
| **DSA**                  | Negative or low titer            | Positive                          | Negative                     | Negative              |
| **C4d Deposition**       | May be present                  | Typically present                 | Absent                       | Variable              |
| **Mechanism of Injury**  | Direct cytotoxicity (CD8+), cytokine-mediated inflammation | CDC, ADCC, complement activation | ROS, inflammation          | Pathogen-specific    |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute cellular rejection is primarily mediated by T lymphocytes, particularly CD8+ cytotoxic T cells, which directly kill graft cells via perforin/granzyme release and Fas/FasL interactions. Biopsy findings of lymphocytic infiltrate, CD8+ T cell predominance, and tubular cell apoptosis are characteristic. Differentiating TCMR from AMR (characterized by antibodies, C4d deposition, endothelialitis) and non-immune causes like ischemia-reperfusion injury is crucial.

<a id='question-120'></a>

### Question 120: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 20 (increases)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying acute cellular rejection in solid organ transplantation, specifically the role of T lymphocytes (CD8+ cytotoxic T cells and CD4+ helper T cells) and their recognition of alloantigens presented by MHC molecules on graft cells. (理解实体器官移植中急性细胞性排斥的免疫机制，特别是T淋巴细胞（CD8+细胞毒性T细胞和CD4+辅助T细胞）的作用及其对移植细胞上MHC分子呈递的异体抗原的识别。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with two HLA mismatches (HLA-A and HLA-B). He is started on standard immunosuppression with tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and graft tenderness. Serum creatinine rises from a baseline of 1.2 mg/dL to 4.5 mg/dL. A renal biopsy reveals diffuse interstitial inflammation with lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular epithelial cell damage, and endothelialitis. Immunofluorescence staining shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the peripheral blood shows a significant increase in CD8+ T cells expressing HLA-A and HLA-B alleles mismatched with the donor kidney.

Which of the following immunological processes is MOST likely responsible for this patient's acute graft dysfunction?

(A) Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) targeting donor HLA-DR antigens.
(B) Acute cellular rejection (ACR) mediated primarily by CD4+ T helper cells recognizing donor MHC class II molecules.
(C) Acute cellular rejection (ACR) mediated primarily by CD8+ cytotoxic T cells recognizing donor MHC class I molecules.
(D) Chronic rejection characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes due to persistent low-grade inflammation.
(E) Hyperacute rejection mediated by pre-formed anti-donor HLA antibodies causing immediate graft thrombosis.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease (ESRD) from diabetic nephropathy, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for potential graft rejection.
- **Transplant Details:** Deceased donor, two HLA mismatches (HLA-A and HLA-B). HLA mismatches are a primary trigger for T-cell mediated rejection.
- **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus (calcineurin inhibitor, inhibits T-cell activation), mycophenolate mofetil (inhibits purine synthesis, affects lymphocyte proliferation), prednisone (corticosteroid, broad immunosuppressant). Despite immunosuppression, rejection can still occur.
- **Timing:** Three weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is characteristic of acute rejection (cellular or antibody-mediated). Hyperacute rejection occurs within minutes to hours, chronic rejection develops over months to years.
- **Symptoms & Signs:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, graft tenderness, rising creatinine. These are classic signs of acute graft dysfunction, often due to rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Diffuse interstitial inflammation with lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular epithelial cell damage, endothelialitis. These findings are characteristic of acute cellular rejection (ACR). C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries suggests an antibody-mediated component (AMR), but the predominant findings point towards ACR.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD8+ T cells expressing mismatched donor HLA-A and HLA-B alleles. This is a key finding indicating that CD8+ cytotoxic T cells are recognizing donor MHC class I molecules (HLA-A and HLA-B) on the graft cells, which is the hallmark of acute cellular rejection.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** This is a T-cell mediated immune response against the transplanted organ. It typically occurs within the first few months post-transplant.
    - **Mechanism:** Recipient T cells (both CD4+ and CD8+) recognize donor MHC molecules (alloantigens) as foreign.
        - **CD4+ T cells:** Recognize donor MHC class II molecules (e.g., HLA-DR, HLA-DQ, HLA-DP) presented by antigen-presenting cells (APCs) in the graft or by recipient APCs that have processed donor antigens. They release cytokines (e.g., IFN-γ, TNF-α) that activate macrophages and CD8+ T cells, amplifying the inflammatory response.
        - **CD8+ T cells:** Recognize donor MHC class I molecules (e.g., HLA-A, HLA-B, HLA-C) presented on the surface of graft cells (parenchymal cells, endothelial cells). Upon recognition, they directly kill the graft cells via cytotoxic mechanisms (e.g., perforin/granzyme release, Fas/FasL interaction).
    - **Histopathology:** Infiltrates of lymphocytes (primarily T cells) in the interstitium, tubules, and blood vessels (endothelialitis). Tubular damage and interstitial fibrosis can occur.
    - **Flow Cytometry:** Can detect increased numbers of recipient T cells in the peripheral blood or graft infiltrate that express T-cell receptors (TCRs) specific for donor MHC molecules. The vignette specifically mentions increased CD8+ T cells expressing mismatched donor HLA-A and HLA-B, strongly implicating CD8+ T cell-mediated cytotoxicity.
- **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** Mediated by antibodies (pre-formed or de novo) against donor HLA antigens, particularly HLA class II (DR) or endothelial antigens (e.g., MICA).
    - **Mechanism:** Antibodies bind to donor endothelial cells, activating complement and recruiting inflammatory cells (neutrophils, macrophages). This leads to endothelial damage, microvascular inflammation (glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis), thrombosis, and graft ischemia.
    - **Histopathology:** Endothelialitis, microvascular inflammation, C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries (indicative of complement activation).
    - **Flow Cytometry:** Can detect donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) in the recipient's serum.
- **Hyperacute Rejection:** Occurs within minutes to hours due to pre-formed antibodies against donor ABO blood group antigens or HLA antigens. Leads to rapid graft thrombosis and necrosis.
- **Chronic Rejection:** Develops over months to years. Characterized by progressive graft fibrosis, vascular changes (e.g., transplant glomerulopathy, chronic active antibody-mediated rejection), and eventual graft failure.

In this case, the biopsy shows lymphocytic infiltrates and tubular damage (consistent with ACR), and the flow cytometry specifically points to activated CD8+ T cells recognizing mismatched donor MHC class I molecules. This strongly supports CD8+ T cell-mediated ACR. While C4d deposition is present, suggesting a possible AMR component, the predominant histological and flow cytometry findings align with ACR driven by CD8+ T cells.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) targeting donor HLA-DR antigens:** While C4d deposition is present, suggesting some AMR component, the primary histological findings (lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular damage) and the specific flow cytometry data (increased CD8+ T cells recognizing HLA-A/B) point more strongly towards ACR. AMR typically involves more prominent endothelialitis and microvascular inflammation. HLA-DR is MHC class II, primarily recognized by CD4+ T cells and targeted by antibodies in AMR, not the primary focus indicated by the CD8+ T cell findings.
- (B) **Acute cellular rejection (ACR) mediated primarily by CD4+ T helper cells recognizing donor MHC class II molecules:** CD4+ T cells are involved in ACR, but the flow cytometry specifically highlights the increase in CD8+ T cells recognizing mismatched HLA-A and HLA-B (MHC class I). While CD4+ cells contribute, the direct cytotoxic effect on graft cells is primarily mediated by CD8+ T cells recognizing MHC class I.
- (D) **Chronic rejection characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes due to persistent low-grade inflammation:** The timeframe (3 weeks) is too early for chronic rejection, which develops over months to years. The biopsy findings described (acute inflammation, tubular damage) are characteristic of acute rejection, not the chronic fibrosis and vascular changes seen in chronic rejection.
- (E) **Hyperacute rejection mediated by pre-formed anti-donor HLA antibodies causing immediate graft thrombosis:** Hyperacute rejection occurs within minutes to hours, not weeks. It is caused by pre-formed antibodies, and the clinical presentation involves immediate graft failure and thrombosis. This patient's presentation is clearly acute, not hyperacute.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR) | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) | Hyperacute Rejection | Chronic Rejection |
|----------------------|---------------------------------|-----------------------------------|----------------------|-------------------|
| **Timing**           | Days to months                  | Days to months                    | Minutes to hours     | Months to years   |
| **Mediator**         | T cells (CD4+, CD8+)            | Antibodies                        | Pre-formed Antibodies| Chronic Inflammation|
| **Key Histology**    | Lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular damage, endothelialitis | Endothelialitis, microvascular inflammation, C4d deposition | Thrombosis, necrosis | Fibrosis, vascular changes|
| **Key Flow Cytometry**| Increased donor-specific T cells | Donor-specific antibodies (DSA)   | N/A                  | N/A               |
| **MHC Target**       | MHC I (CD8+) & MHC II (CD4+)    | MHC II (esp. DR), Endothelial Ags | ABO, HLA              | N/A               |
| **Example Scenario** | Patient in vignette             | C4d+ biopsy, DSA+ serum           | Immediate graft loss | Late graft failure |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute cellular rejection is primarily T-cell mediated, with CD8+ cytotoxic T cells recognizing MHC class I alloantigens (like HLA-A and HLA-B) on graft cells being a major mechanism. Differentiating ACR from AMR is crucial, often relying on biopsy findings (lymphocytic infiltrates vs. endothelialitis/C4d) and specific immunological tests (T-cell assays vs. DSA). Remember the typical time course for each type of rejection.

<a id='question-121'></a>

### Question 121: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 21 (expression)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of specific cell surface markers (CD4, CD8, CD25, CD45RA, CD45RO, CD62L, CCR7) in T cell differentiation, activation, homing, and function, particularly in the context of transplant rejection and immune regulation. (理解 T 细胞表面标志物 (CD4, CD8, CD25, CD45RA, CD45RO, CD62L, CCR7) 在 T 细胞分化、活化、归巢和功能中的作用，特别是在移植排斥和免疫调节的背景下。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor. His pre-transplant panel reactive antibody (PRA) screen was negative. Post-transplant, he is maintained on immunosuppression with tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Six months after transplantation, he develops acute onset of fever, flank pain, and decreased urine output. Serum creatinine rises from a baseline of 1.2 mg/dL to 4.5 mg/dL. A renal biopsy reveals acute tubular necrosis and interstitial inflammation with mononuclear cell infiltrates. Flow cytometry analysis of the peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a significant increase in CD3+CD4+ T cells expressing CD25 (IL-2 receptor alpha chain) and CD45RO (an isoform of CD45 associated with memory T cells). These T cells also show decreased expression of CD62L (L-selectin) and CCR7, markers associated with homing to lymph nodes. Further analysis reveals that these activated T cells are producing high levels of IFN-gamma and TNF-alpha. Which of the following cell surface markers is MOST characteristic of the T cell subset primarily responsible for this acute cellular rejection episode?

(A) CD45RA, CD62L, CCR7
(B) CD45RO, CD62L, CCR7
(C) CD45RA, CD45RO, CD25
(D) CD45RO, CD45RO, CD25
(E) CD45RA, CD62L, CD25

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for potential transplant rejection.
- **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, prednisone. Despite immunosuppression, rejection can still occur.
- **Clinical Presentation:** 6 months post-transplant, acute onset of fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, rising creatinine. These are classic signs and symptoms of acute graft rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Acute tubular necrosis and interstitial inflammation with mononuclear cell infiltrates. This confirms acute cellular rejection.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD3+CD4+ T cells expressing CD25, CD45RO, decreased CD62L, and CCR7. This is the crucial immunological clue.
    - CD4+ T cells are involved.
    - CD25 (IL-2Rα) expression indicates activation.
    - CD45RO expression indicates memory phenotype.
    - Decreased CD62L and CCR7 indicate effector memory phenotype and reduced homing to lymph nodes, favoring migration to inflamed tissues (like the graft).
- **Cytokine Profile:** High levels of IFN-gamma and TNF-alpha. These are pro-inflammatory cytokines produced by activated T cells, contributing to tissue damage in rejection.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the cell surface markers MOST characteristic of the T cell subset responsible for this acute cellular rejection.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **T Cell Subsets:** T cells differentiate into various subsets based on their function, activation state, and homing properties. Key markers distinguish these subsets:
    - **Naive T cells:** Typically express CD45RA, CD62L (L-selectin), and CCR7. They circulate through lymph nodes, awaiting antigen presentation.
    - **Central Memory T cells (Tcm):** Express CD45RA, CD62L, CCR7, and CD44. They reside in lymph nodes and respond rapidly upon re-encountering antigen.
    - **Effector Memory T cells (Tem):** Express CD45RO, lack CD62L and CCR7, and express CD44. They circulate through peripheral tissues and mediate rapid effector functions upon antigen encounter.
    - **Effector T cells (Te):** Express CD45RO, lack CD62L and CCR7, and express CD44. They are terminally differentiated and migrate to sites of inflammation.
- **Acute Cellular Rejection:** This is primarily mediated by T lymphocytes (both CD4+ and CD8+) that recognize donor antigens as foreign.
    - **CD4+ T cells:** Recognize MHC class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells (APCs) in the graft. Activated CD4+ T cells (specifically effector memory and effector subsets) produce cytokines (IFN-γ, TNF-α) that recruit and activate other immune cells (macrophages, CD8+ T cells), leading to tissue damage.
    - **CD8+ T cells:** Recognize MHC class I molecules on graft cells. Activated CD8+ T cells directly kill graft cells.
- **Marker Profile in Rejection:** The flow cytometry findings (CD4+, CD25+, CD45RO+, CD62L-, CCR7-) are characteristic of activated effector memory T cells (Tem) or effector T cells (Te). These cells are primed to respond rapidly to donor antigens and migrate to the inflamed graft tissue. CD45RO is associated with memory and effector T cells, while CD45RA is associated with naive T cells. CD62L and CCR7 are homing molecules for lymph nodes; their downregulation allows T cells to exit lymph nodes and enter peripheral tissues, including the graft. CD25 (IL-2Rα) is an activation marker.
- **Therefore:** The T cell subset primarily responsible for this acute cellular rejection is an effector memory or effector T cell subset, characterized by the expression of CD45RO and the lack of expression of CD62L and CCR7.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) CD45RA, CD62L, CCR7: This profile describes naive T cells. Naive T cells are not the primary effector cells in acute cellular rejection; they are the precursors that differentiate into effector cells.
- (B) CD45RO, CD62L, CCR7: This profile is inconsistent. CD45RO is characteristic of memory/effector cells, while CD62L and CCR7 are characteristic of naive/central memory cells that home to lymph nodes. Effector memory/effector cells involved in rejection *lack* CD62L and CCR7. This option is incorrect because it mixes markers from different subsets.
- (C) CD45RA, CD45RO, CD25: This profile is also inconsistent. CD45RA and CD45RO are generally mutually exclusive isoforms, representing naive/central memory vs. effector/memory phenotypes, respectively. While CD25 indicates activation, the combination of CD45RA and CD45RO is not typical for a single T cell subset.
- (D) CD45RO, CD45RO, CD25: This option repeats CD45RO, which is incorrect. CD45RO is expressed, but not twice. The correct profile should include markers indicating effector/memory phenotype (CD45RO) and lack of lymph node homing (lack of CD62L/CCR7).
- (E) CD45RA, CD62L, CD25: This profile describes an activated naive T cell (CD25+ indicates activation, CD45RA/CD62L indicate naive phenotype). While naive T cells can become activated, the primary effector cells in acute rejection are memory/effector cells, not activated naive cells.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature          | Naive T cells (Tn) | Central Memory T cells (Tcm) | Effector Memory T cells (Tem) / Effector T cells (Te) |
|-------------------|--------------------|-----------------------------|------------------------------------------------------|
| CD45 Isoform      | CD45RA             | CD45RA                      | CD45RO                                               |
| CD62L (L-selectin) | +                  | +                           | -                                                      |
| CCR7              | +                  | +                           | -                                                      |
| CD25 (IL-2Rα)     | -                  | -                           | + (upon activation)                                    |
| Homing           | Lymph Nodes        | Lymph Nodes                 | Peripheral Tissues (e.g., graft)                      |
| Role in Rejection| Precursor          | Rapid response in LN         | Primary effector cells in graft tissue                |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The key takeaway is understanding the distinct surface marker profiles of different T cell subsets and their roles in immune responses, particularly in transplant rejection. Effector T cells and effector memory T cells, characterized by CD45RO expression and lack of CD62L/CCR7, are the primary mediators of acute cellular rejection. Differentiating these from naive (CD45RA+, CD62L+, CCR7+) and central memory (CD45RA+, CD62L+, CCR7+) T cells is crucial.
