<a id='question-122'></a>

### Question 122: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 22 (increasing)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying acute cellular rejection in organ transplantation, specifically the role of T lymphocytes (CD8+ cytotoxic T cells) and their recognition of alloantigens presented by MHC class I molecules on graft cells. (理解器官移植中急性细胞性排斥的免疫机制，特别是T淋巴细胞（CD8+细胞毒性T细胞）的作用及其对移植细胞表面MHC I类分子呈递的异体抗原的识别。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with one HLA mismatch at the A locus. Post-transplant, he is started on immunosuppressive therapy including tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks after transplantation, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals diffuse interstitial inflammation with infiltration of mononuclear cells, tubular epithelial cell damage, and evidence of endothelialitis in peritubular capillaries. Immunofluorescence staining shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the peripheral blood shows a significant increase in CD8+ T cells expressing HLA-DR. Further analysis reveals that these CD8+ T cells recognize peptides derived from the donor's HLA-A allele presented on MHC class I molecules. Which of the following cellular mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for the observed graft injury?

(A) Antibody-mediated complement activation leading to endothelial damage.
(B) CD4+ T helper cell activation leading to macrophage recruitment and cytokine release.
(C) CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocyte-mediated killing of graft parenchymal cells.
(D) Natural killer (NK) cell activation due to lack of inhibitory signals from MHC class I molecules.
(E) B cell-mediated production of antibodies against donor-specific HLA antigens.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for potential transplant complications.
- **Transplant Details:** Deceased donor, one HLA mismatch (A locus). HLA mismatch is a major risk factor for rejection.
- **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus (calcineurin inhibitor), mycophenolate mofetil (purine synthesis inhibitor), prednisone (corticosteroid). Despite immunosuppression, rejection can still occur.
- **Clinical Presentation:** 3 weeks post-transplant, fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine. These are classic signs and symptoms of acute kidney transplant rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Diffuse interstitial inflammation, mononuclear cell infiltration, tubular epithelial cell damage, endothelialitis in peritubular capillaries. These findings are characteristic of acute cellular rejection (specifically T-cell mediated).
- **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. While C4d deposition is classically associated with antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), it can also be seen in acute cellular rejection (ACR), particularly in cases with overlapping features or endothelial injury. However, the predominant histological features point strongly towards ACR.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD8+ T cells expressing HLA-DR. HLA-DR expression on CD8+ T cells indicates their activation.
- **Mechanism:** CD8+ T cells recognize donor HLA-A peptides presented on MHC class I. This confirms a cell-mediated response targeting graft cells expressing the mismatched HLA allele.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely cellular mechanism responsible for graft injury.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** Primarily mediated by T lymphocytes (both CD4+ and CD8+) recognizing donor alloantigens (like HLA molecules) presented by recipient antigen-presenting cells (APCs) or graft cells.
- **CD8+ T Cells:** Recognize alloantigens presented on MHC class I molecules, which are expressed on virtually all nucleated cells, including graft parenchymal cells (tubular epithelial cells in the kidney). Upon recognition, activated CD8+ T cells (cytotoxic T lymphocytes, CTLs) directly kill target cells via mechanisms like perforin/granzyme release and Fas/FasL interaction, leading to parenchymal damage.
- **CD4+ T Cells:** Recognize alloantigens presented on MHC class II molecules, primarily expressed on APCs. They orchestrate the immune response by releasing cytokines (e.g., IFN-γ, TNF-α) that activate macrophages and other immune cells, contributing to inflammation and graft damage.
- **MHC Class I Presentation:** Essential for CD8+ T cell recognition. The vignette explicitly states that CD8+ T cells recognize donor HLA-A peptides presented on MHC class I molecules.
- **HLA-DR Expression:** An activation marker for T cells. Increased HLA-DR expression on CD8+ T cells confirms their activation state in response to the alloantigen.
- **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** Mediated by donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) binding to donor HLA antigens on endothelial cells, activating complement and recruiting inflammatory cells (e.g., neutrophils, macrophages), leading to endothelialitis and microvascular injury (C4d deposition is a key marker). While C4d is present, the dominant histological features (parenchymal damage, mononuclear cell infiltrate) and the specific mechanism involving activated CD8+ T cells targeting MHC class I point more strongly towards ACR.
- **NK Cells:** Can contribute to rejection, especially in the context of missing self-MHC class I recognition (e.g., due to viral infection or genetic deficiency), but the primary mechanism described here involves specific T cell recognition of alloantigens.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Antibody-mediated complement activation leading to endothelial damage. This describes Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR). While C4d deposition is present (a marker of complement activation often seen in AMR), the primary histological findings (parenchymal damage, mononuclear infiltrate) and the specific identification of activated CD8+ T cells recognizing MHC class I-presented alloantigens strongly favor ACR over AMR as the *most likely* primary mechanism. AMR typically presents with more prominent endothelialitis and microvascular inflammation (glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis).
- (B) CD4+ T helper cell activation leading to macrophage recruitment and cytokine release. CD4+ T cells are indeed involved in ACR, orchestrating the response. However, the vignette specifically highlights the role of *CD8+ T cells* recognizing MHC class I and causing direct cell killing, which is the predominant mechanism described. While CD4+ cells contribute, the direct cytotoxic effect described points to CD8+ cells.
- (D) Natural killer (NK) cell activation due to lack of inhibitory signals from MHC class I molecules. NK cells can mediate rejection, particularly in cases where MHC class I expression is downregulated (e.g., by viruses or genetic defects). However, the vignette describes a specific adaptive immune response mediated by CD8+ T cells recognizing intact MHC class I molecules presenting alloantigens, not an innate response due to missing self.
- (E) B cell-mediated production of antibodies against donor-specific HLA antigens. This is the mechanism underlying Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR). As discussed in (A), while antibodies might be present, the clinical, histological, and immunological data (activated CD8+ T cells recognizing MHC class I) point more strongly towards ACR as the primary driver of the observed graft injury in this specific scenario.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR)                                  | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR)                                    |
|-------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Primary Mediators**   | T lymphocytes (CD4+, CD8+)                                      | Donor-Specific Antibodies (DSAs)                                      |
| **Target Cells**        | Graft parenchymal cells (CD8+), APCs, endothelial cells (CD4+) | Endothelial cells                                                     |
| **Key Histology**       | Interstitial inflammation, parenchymal damage, endothelialitis   | Endothelialitis, microvascular inflammation (glomerulitis), C4d+      |
| **Key Immune Markers**  | Activated T cells (e.g., HLA-DR+), T cell infiltrates          | DSAs, C4d deposition, complement activation markers                  |
| **MHC Involvement**     | CD8+ recognize MHC I; CD4+ recognize MHC II                     | Antibodies bind to HLA (MHC I & II) on endothelium                   |
| **Vignette Clues**      | CD8+ T cell activation, recognition of MHC I-presented alloantigen | C4d deposition (can be present in both, but more classic for AMR)      |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute cellular rejection is primarily mediated by T cells recognizing donor alloantigens. CD8+ T cells recognize MHC class I-presented antigens and directly kill graft parenchymal cells. Differentiating ACR from AMR is crucial, relying on histology, presence of DSAs, C4d deposition, and specific immune cell markers. While C4d can be seen in both, the vignette's emphasis on activated CD8+ T cells targeting MHC class I points definitively to ACR. Key differentials include AMR, infection, and drug toxicity.

<a id='question-123'></a>

### Question 123: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 23 (susceptibility)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the increased susceptibility of immunocompromised transplant recipients (specifically those on chronic immunosuppression) to opportunistic infections, particularly *Pneumocystis jirovecii* pneumonia (PJP), and the diagnostic methods used. (理解免疫功能低下移植受者（尤其是长期免疫抑制者）对机会性感染的易感性，特别是 *Pneumocystis jirovecii* 肺炎 (PJP)，以及使用的诊断方法。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 58-year-old male with a history of chronic kidney disease secondary to diabetic nephropathy received a kidney transplant 3 years ago. He has been maintained on chronic immunosuppression with tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Over the past month, he has experienced progressive dyspnea on exertion, a non-productive cough, and intermittent low-grade fever (37.8-38.3°C). His physical examination reveals bibasilar crackles and a respiratory rate of 24 breaths/min. Arterial blood gas analysis shows hypoxemia (PaO2 60 mmHg) and a widened alveolar-arterial (A-a) gradient. A chest X-ray demonstrates diffuse bilateral interstitial infiltrates. Sputum induction was performed, and the sample was stained using Gomori methenamine silver (GMS). Microscopic examination reveals numerous encysted organisms with a characteristic "crushed ping-pong ball" morphology within alveolar spaces. Which of the following is the most likely causative organism?

(A) *Aspergillus fumigatus*
(B) *Cryptococcus neoformans*
(C) *Pneumocystis jirovecii*
(D) *Mycobacterium tuberculosis*
(E) *Streptococcus pneumoniae*

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 58-year-old male, kidney transplant recipient 3 years ago. This immediately flags him as immunocompromised due to chronic immunosuppression.
- **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. This combination significantly suppresses both T-cell and B-cell mediated immunity, increasing susceptibility to opportunistic infections.
- **Symptoms:** Progressive dyspnea, non-productive cough, low-grade fever. These are classic symptoms of pneumonia, particularly atypical pneumonia.
- **Physical Exam:** Bibasilar crackles, tachypnea (RR 24). Consistent with lower respiratory tract infection.
- **ABG:** Hypoxemia (PaO2 60 mmHg) and widened A-a gradient. Indicates impaired gas exchange, typical of interstitial lung disease or pneumonia.
- **Chest X-ray:** Diffuse bilateral interstitial infiltrates. This pattern is highly suggestive of *Pneumocystis jirovecii* pneumonia (PJP) in an immunocompromised host, although other causes exist.
- **Diagnostic Test:** Sputum induction stained with Gomori methenamine silver (GMS). GMS is a specific stain used to visualize fungi and *Pneumocystis*.
- **Microscopic Findings:** Numerous encysted organisms with a "crushed ping-pong ball" morphology within alveolar spaces. This is the pathognomonic description of *Pneumocystis jirovecii* cysts.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the most likely causative organism based on the clinical presentation and diagnostic findings.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- *Pneumocystis jirovecii* (formerly *Pneumocystis carinii*) is an atypical fungus (now classified as a fungus based on molecular data) that causes PJP, a severe opportunistic pneumonia primarily affecting immunocompromised individuals, especially those with impaired T-cell immunity (e.g., HIV/AIDS patients, transplant recipients on immunosuppression).
- **Pathogenesis:** Inhalation of *P. jirovecii* cysts leads to rupture and release of sporozoites, which infect alveolar epithelial cells (type I pneumocytes). They multiply asexually (trophozoites) and sexually (cysts). The host immune response, particularly cell-mediated immunity involving CD4+ T cells, is crucial for controlling the infection. Immunosuppression impairs this response, leading to uncontrolled proliferation and lung damage.
- **Diagnosis:** Definitive diagnosis requires microscopic identification of the organism in respiratory specimens (sputum induction, bronchoalveolar lavage - BAL). GMS stain is the preferred method for visualizing the cysts. Immunofluorescence assays using monoclonal antibodies against *P. jirovecii* are also highly sensitive and specific. PCR can detect *P. jirovecii* DNA.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Typically subacute onset of fever, non-productive cough, and progressive dyspnea. Chest X-ray often shows diffuse bilateral interstitial infiltrates, although other patterns can occur. ABG typically shows hypoxemia and a widened A-a gradient.
- **Immunocompromised Host:** Transplant recipients on chronic immunosuppression are at high risk for PJP. Prophylaxis with trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole (TMP-SMX) is often used in high-risk patients.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) *Aspergillus fumigatus*: This is a mold that can cause invasive pulmonary aspergillosis in severely immunocompromised patients (e.g., neutropenic patients, hematopoietic stem cell transplant recipients). While it can cause pneumonia, the characteristic microscopic finding is septate hyphae branching at acute angles (45 degrees), not "crushed ping-pong ball" cysts. GMS stain can visualize *Aspergillus*, but the morphology described is incorrect.
- (B) *Cryptococcus neoformans*: This is an encapsulated yeast that causes cryptococcosis, often presenting as meningitis in immunocompromised hosts (especially HIV/AIDS). Pulmonary cryptococcosis can occur but is less common than PJP in transplant recipients. *Cryptococcus* appears as round, budding yeast cells, often with a characteristic capsule, when stained with India ink or GMS. The morphology described ("crushed ping-pong ball") is not characteristic of *Cryptococcus*.
- (D) *Mycobacterium tuberculosis*: This bacterium causes tuberculosis. While transplant recipients are at increased risk for reactivation TB, the typical presentation involves upper lobe infiltrates, cavitation, and often productive cough. Diagnosis relies on acid-fast bacilli (AFB) staining (Ziehl-Neelsen or Kinyoun stain) and culture, not GMS staining showing "crushed ping-pong ball" cysts.
- (E) *Streptococcus pneumoniae*: This bacterium is a common cause of community-acquired pneumonia. It typically presents acutely with fever, productive cough, pleuritic chest pain, and lobar consolidation on chest X-ray. Gram stain of sputum would show Gram-positive diplococci. GMS staining is not used for bacterial diagnosis, and the microscopic morphology described is inconsistent with *S. pneumoniae*.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | *Pneumocystis jirovecii* (PJP) | *Aspergillus fumigatus* (Aspergillosis) | *Cryptococcus neoformans* (Cryptococcosis) | *Mycobacterium tuberculosis* (TB) | *Streptococcus pneumoniae* (Pneumonia) |
|----------------------|---------------------------------|-----------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------|----------------------------------------|
| **Host Risk**        | Immunocompromised (T-cell defect) | Severely immunocompromised (Neutropenia) | Immunocompromised (HIV, Steroids)          | Immunocompromised (Transplant, TNF-α) | Immunocompetent or compromised        |
| **Typical Symptoms** | Subacute dyspnea, dry cough, fever | Fever, cough, pleuritic pain, hemoptysis | Often asymptomatic (pulmonary), meningitis | Cough (productive), fever, night sweats, weight loss | Acute fever, productive cough, pleuritic pain |
| **Chest X-ray**      | Diffuse bilateral interstitial infiltrates | Nodules, cavitation, consolidation       | Nodules, infiltrates, cavitation          | Upper lobe infiltrates, cavitation | Lobar consolidation                   |
| **Stain**            | GMS, Silver stain, DFA            | GMS, PAS                                 | India ink, GMS, Cryptococcal antigen       | AFB (Ziehl-Neelsen)                | Gram stain                             |
| **Morphology**       | "Crushed ping-pong ball" cysts   | Septate hyphae (45° branching)           | Round budding yeast, capsule               | Acid-fast bacilli                  | Gram-positive diplococci               |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Immunocompromised patients, especially transplant recipients on chronic immunosuppression, are highly susceptible to opportunistic infections like *Pneumocystis jirovecii* pneumonia (PJP). The classic presentation includes subacute onset of dyspnea, dry cough, fever, and diffuse bilateral interstitial infiltrates on chest X-ray. Diagnosis relies on identifying the characteristic "crushed ping-pong ball" cysts using GMS stain on respiratory specimens. Remember to differentiate PJP from other opportunistic infections like invasive aspergillosis (hyphae), cryptococcosis (yeast), and bacterial pneumonias based on clinical presentation, radiographic findings, and specific staining characteristics.

<a id='question-124'></a>

### Question 124: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 24 (restricted)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying graft rejection, specifically the role of T cells (CD4+ and CD8+) and antibodies in acute and chronic rejection, and differentiating between cellular and antibody-mediated rejection. (理解移植物排斥的免疫机制，特别是T细胞（CD4+和CD8+）和抗体在急性和慢性排斥中的作用，以及区分细胞介导和抗体介导的排斥。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease secondary to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an unknown HLA type. He is started on standard immunosuppression consisting of tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. Renal biopsy reveals diffuse interstitial edema, inflammatory cell infiltrates primarily composed of lymphocytes and macrophages, and evidence of tubular injury. Immunofluorescence microscopy shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the peripheral blood shows a decrease in CD4+ T cells and an increase in CD8+ T cells compared to baseline. Serum creatinine continues to rise despite dose adjustments of immunosuppressants.

Which of the following is the MOST likely mechanism contributing to this patient's graft dysfunction?

(A) Antibody-mediated rejection involving donor-specific antibodies (DSA) targeting HLA class II antigens on endothelial cells.
(B) Antibody-mediated rejection involving donor-specific antibodies (DSA) targeting HLA class I antigens on tubular epithelial cells.
(C) Acute cellular rejection mediated primarily by CD4+ T helper cells recognizing donor MHC class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells.
(D) Acute cellular rejection mediated primarily by CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) directly recognizing donor MHC class I molecules on graft cells.
(E) Chronic antibody-mediated rejection characterized by transplant glomerulopathy and C4d deposition in glomerular capillaries.

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease (ESRD) due to diabetic nephropathy, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for potential graft rejection.
- **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus (calcineurin inhibitor, inhibits T cell activation), mycophenolate mofetil (inhibits purine synthesis, affects lymphocyte proliferation), prednisone (corticosteroid, broad immunosuppressant). Standard regimen, but rejection can still occur.
- **Timeline:** 3 weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is typical for acute rejection episodes (both cellular and antibody-mediated).
- **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated serum creatinine. These are classic signs of acute graft dysfunction, suggesting rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:**
    - Diffuse interstitial edema, inflammatory cell infiltrates (lymphocytes, macrophages), tubular injury: These findings are characteristic of acute rejection, particularly acute cellular rejection (ACR).
    - C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries: C4d is a marker of complement activation, often seen in antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), but can also be present in ACR, especially in cases with significant inflammation. Its presence here doesn't definitively rule out ACR.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Decrease in CD4+ T cells, increase in CD8+ T cells. This pattern is highly suggestive of acute cellular rejection, where CD8+ CTLs infiltrate the graft and cause direct cell damage.
- **Rising Creatinine despite Immunosuppression:** Indicates ongoing graft injury and failure of current immunosuppression to control the rejection process.

The combination of clinical signs, biopsy findings (inflammation, tubular injury), and flow cytometry results (increased CD8+ T cells) strongly points towards acute cellular rejection mediated by CD8+ T cells.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** Primarily mediated by T lymphocytes.
    - **CD4+ T helper cells (Th):** Recognize donor MHC class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells (APCs) within the graft. They release cytokines (e.g., IFN-γ, TNF-α) that activate macrophages and recruit other inflammatory cells, contributing to graft damage.
    - **CD8+ Cytotoxic T Lymphocytes (CTLs):** Recognize donor MHC class I molecules directly on graft parenchymal cells (e.g., tubular epithelial cells). They kill these cells via perforin/granzyme pathways or Fas/FasL interactions, leading to direct graft injury.
- **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** Mediated by antibodies, primarily donor-specific antibodies (DSAs), targeting donor HLA antigens.
    - **HLA Class II DSAs:** Often target endothelial cells, leading to complement activation (C4d deposition), endothelial injury, microvascular inflammation (glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis), and thrombosis.
    - **HLA Class I DSAs:** Can also target endothelial cells or parenchymal cells.
- **C4d Deposition:** A marker of complement activation (specifically the classical pathway). It is a key feature of AMR but can also be seen in ACR, particularly in cases with significant inflammation or overlap syndromes. Its presence alone is not diagnostic of AMR.
- **Flow Cytometry in Rejection:** Changes in lymphocyte subsets can help differentiate rejection types. Increased CD8+ T cells are characteristic of ACR, while changes in B cells or NK cells might be seen in AMR.

In this case, the prominent histological features of interstitial inflammation and tubular injury, coupled with the flow cytometry showing an increase in CD8+ T cells, strongly implicates CD8+ CTL-mediated acute cellular rejection as the primary mechanism. While C4d deposition is present, it doesn't exclude ACR, and the overall picture fits ACR better.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Antibody-mediated rejection involving HLA class II DSAs targeting endothelial cells. While AMR is a possibility, the biopsy findings (prominent interstitial inflammation and tubular injury) and flow cytometry (increased CD8+ T cells) are more typical of ACR. C4d deposition can be seen in both, but the overall picture favors ACR. This option focuses on endothelial damage typical of AMR.
- (B): Antibody-mediated rejection involving HLA class I DSAs targeting tubular epithelial cells. Similar to (A), this describes AMR. While HLA class I DSAs can target tubular cells, the histological and flow cytometry findings are more consistent with cellular rejection.
- (C): Acute cellular rejection mediated primarily by CD4+ T helper cells. CD4+ T cells play a role in ACR by activating macrophages and other cells, but the direct cytotoxic damage to graft cells is primarily mediated by CD8+ T cells. The flow cytometry showing an increase in CD8+ T cells makes this option less likely than (D) as the *primary* mechanism.
- (E): Chronic antibody-mediated rejection characterized by transplant glomerulopathy and C4d deposition in glomerular capillaries. This describes a chronic process, not an acute one occurring at 3 weeks. Transplant glomerulopathy is a specific histological finding of chronic AMR, and C4d deposition is typically found in glomeruli in chronic AMR, not peritubular capillaries as described in the vignette.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR) | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) | Chronic Rejection (General) |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :-------------------------- |
| **Primary Mediators**   | T cells (CD4+, CD8+)            | Antibodies (DSAs)                  | T cells, Antibodies, Fibrosis |
| **Timeline**            | Days to weeks                  | Days to weeks                      | Months to years             |
| **Histology**           | Interstitial inflammation, tubular injury, edema | Endothelialitis, microvascular inflammation, thrombosis, C4d deposition | Fibrosis, glomerulosclerosis, interstitial atrophy |
| **Key Cells**           | CD8+ CTLs, CD4+ Th cells        | B cells, Plasma cells, NK cells    | Variable                   |
| **C4d Deposition**      | Can be present, often peritubular | Characteristic, often peritubular/glomerular | Can be present             |
| **Flow Cytometry**      | Increased CD8+ T cells          | Variable, may see B cell changes   | Variable                   |
| **Example Scenario**    | Fever, rising creatinine 3 weeks post-transplant, increased CD8+ T cells | Fever, rising creatinine 1 week post-transplant, positive DSA, C4d+ | Gradual decline in graft function over years |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute graft rejection can be mediated by T cells (ACR) or antibodies (AMR). ACR is characterized by T cell infiltration and direct cell killing (CD8+) or cytokine-mediated inflammation (CD4+). AMR involves DSAs targeting HLA antigens, leading to complement activation and endothelial injury. Differentiating between ACR and AMR is crucial for management. Key clues include biopsy findings (inflammation pattern, C4d location), flow cytometry (lymphocyte subset changes), and presence of DSAs. This case highlights the importance of CD8+ T cells in direct graft cell damage in ACR.

<a id='question-125'></a>

### Question 125: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 25 (killing.)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the mechanisms of graft rejection, specifically the roles of different immune cells (T cells, B cells, NK cells) and antibodies in acute and chronic rejection, and differentiating between cell-mediated and antibody-mediated rejection. (理解移植物排斥机制，特别是不同免疫细胞（T细胞、B细胞、NK细胞）和抗体在急性和慢性排斥中的作用，以及区分细胞介导性和抗体介导的排斥。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an unknown HLA type. He is started on standard immunosuppression consisting of tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and graft tenderness. Serum creatinine rises from a baseline of 1.2 mg/dL to 4.5 mg/dL. A renal biopsy reveals diffuse interstitial edema, inflammatory cell infiltrates predominantly composed of CD3+ T lymphocytes and CD68+ macrophages, and evidence of tubular epithelial cell injury. Immunofluorescence staining shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a decrease in CD4+ T cells and an increase in CD8+ T cells compared to baseline. His anti-donor HLA antibody levels are negative.

Which of the following immune cells is MOST likely directly responsible for the observed graft injury in this patient?

(A) CD4+ T helper cells secreting IL-2
(B) CD19+ B cells producing anti-HLA antibodies
(C) CD56+ Natural Killer (NK) cells mediating antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC)
(D) CD8+ Cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) releasing perforin and granzymes
(E) CD14+ Monocytes differentiating into M1 macrophages

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease (ESRD) from diabetic nephropathy, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for potential graft rejection.
- **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus (calcineurin inhibitor), mycophenolate mofetil (purine synthesis inhibitor), prednisone (corticosteroid). Standard regimen, but rejection can still occur.
- **Timeline:** 3 weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is typical for acute rejection.
- **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, graft tenderness. Classic signs of acute kidney graft rejection.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Rising serum creatinine (4.5 mg/dL from 1.2 mg/dL). Indicates worsening kidney function, consistent with graft injury.
- **Renal Biopsy Findings:**
    - Diffuse interstitial edema, inflammatory cell infiltrates (CD3+ T cells, CD68+ macrophages), tubular epithelial cell injury. Suggests inflammation and direct damage to the graft.
    - C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. This is a key finding indicating complement activation, often associated with antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), but can also be seen in T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR) with some antibody involvement or overlap.
    - Negative anti-donor HLA antibody levels. This makes pure AMR less likely, although low levels might be missed or present transiently.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Decrease in CD4+ T cells (expected effect of immunosuppression), increase in CD8+ T cells. Suggests activation or expansion of cytotoxic T cells.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the immune cell MOST likely directly responsible for graft injury.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Acute Rejection:** Can be T-cell mediated (TCMR) or antibody-mediated (AMR), or a combination.
    - **TCMR:** Primarily mediated by T lymphocytes recognizing donor HLA antigens. CD8+ CTLs directly kill graft cells (tubular epithelial cells in the kidney) by releasing perforin and granzymes, leading to apoptosis. CD4+ T helper cells (Th1 phenotype) orchestrate the response by activating macrophages and CTLs via cytokines like IL-2 and IFN-γ. The biopsy findings of CD3+ T cell infiltrates and tubular injury are characteristic of TCMR.
    - **AMR:** Mediated by donor-specific antibodies (DSAs), typically anti-HLA antibodies. These antibodies bind to endothelial cells, activating complement (leading to C4d deposition) and recruiting inflammatory cells (like NK cells via ADCC, macrophages). This causes endothelial injury, microvascular inflammation (glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis), and thrombosis, ultimately leading to graft dysfunction. The C4d deposition points towards AMR, but negative anti-HLA antibodies make this less straightforward. C4d can be present in TCMR as well, especially if there's some antibody involvement or cross-reactivity.
- **Analysis of Findings:** The biopsy shows significant T cell infiltrates (CD3+) and tubular injury. The increase in CD8+ T cells in the peripheral blood supports a role for CTLs. While C4d deposition is present (suggesting complement activation often seen in AMR), the anti-HLA antibody levels are negative. This clinical picture, with prominent T cell infiltration and tubular damage, along with rising creatinine and graft tenderness within 3 weeks, is most consistent with acute *cellular* rejection (TCMR), primarily driven by CD8+ CTLs. CD4+ T cells play a role in orchestrating the response, but CD8+ CTLs are the direct effectors of cell killing in this scenario.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) CD4+ T helper cells secreting IL-2: CD4+ T cells are crucial in initiating and amplifying the immune response, including TCMR, by secreting cytokines like IL-2 (which promotes T cell proliferation) and IFN-γ (which activates macrophages and CTLs). However, they are not the primary *direct* effector cells causing cytolysis of graft cells. The direct killing is mainly done by CD8+ CTLs. While important, they are not the *most* direct cause of the observed tubular injury compared to CTLs.
- (B) CD19+ B cells producing anti-HLA antibodies: B cells produce antibodies, which are the mediators of AMR. The vignette states that anti-donor HLA antibody levels are negative. While low levels might exist, the primary pathology described (T cell infiltrates, tubular injury) and the negative antibody test make B cells producing DSAs less likely to be the *most* direct cause in this specific presentation. The C4d deposition could be misleading without positive DSAs.
- (C) CD56+ Natural Killer (NK) cells mediating antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC): NK cells can contribute to AMR by mediating ADCC against endothelial cells coated with DSAs. However, this requires the presence of significant DSAs, which are reported as negative in this case. Furthermore, the primary pathology described involves T cell infiltrates and tubular injury, not primarily endothelial damage characteristic of AMR mediated by NK cells.
- (E) CD14+ Monocytes differentiating into M1 macrophages: Monocytes/macrophages are recruited to the site of inflammation and contribute to tissue damage through phagocytosis and release of inflammatory mediators. They are activated by T cells (especially Th1 cells) and can be involved in both TCMR and AMR. However, they are not the primary *direct* effector cells responsible for the specific cytolytic killing of tubular epithelial cells described in the biopsy. T cells (especially CD8+ CTLs) are the main drivers of this direct cell killing.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Acute Cellular Rejection (TCMR)                                  | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR)                                     |
|----------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Primary Mediators** | CD8+ Cytotoxic T Lymphocytes (CTLs), CD4+ T helper cells          | Donor-Specific Antibodies (DSAs), Complement, NK cells, Macrophages     |
| **Key Pathological Features** | T cell infiltrates (CD3+), Tubular injury, Interstitial edema | Endothelialitis, Glomerulitis, Peritubular capillaritis, C4d deposition |
| **Typical Timing**    | Days to weeks post-transplant                                     | Days to months post-transplant                                         |
| **Biopsy Findings**   | CD3+ infiltrates, Tubular cell apoptosis/necrosis                 | C4d deposition, Endothelial cell swelling/necrosis, Microvascular inflammation |
| **Antibody Status**   | Usually negative or low titer DSAs                               | Positive DSAs (usually anti-HLA)                                      |
| **Cell Type Focus**   | CD8+ CTLs directly killing graft cells                            | Antibodies targeting endothelium, activating complement/NK cells        |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute graft rejection can be cell-mediated (TCMR) or antibody-mediated (AMR). TCMR is characterized by T cell infiltrates and direct cell killing, often by CD8+ CTLs. AMR involves DSAs, complement activation (C4d), and endothelial injury. This case presents with features suggestive of TCMR (T cell infiltrates, tubular injury, rising creatinine, increased CD8+ cells) despite the presence of C4d, and negative DSA levels, highlighting that TCMR is the most likely primary mechanism. Differentiate TCMR from AMR based on biopsy findings, antibody status, and predominant cell types involved.

<a id='question-126'></a>

### Question 126: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 26 (alleles)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of HLA alleles in graft rejection, specifically focusing on the difference between direct and indirect allorecognition pathways and the cell types involved. (理解HLA等位基因在移植物排斥中的作用，特别是直接和间接同基因识别途径的区别以及所涉及的细胞类型。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor. The donor's HLA typing reveals HLA-A*02:01, HLA-B*07:02, HLA-DRB1*15:01, and HLA-DQB1*06:03. The recipient's HLA typing shows HLA-A*02:01, HLA-B*44:02, HLA-DRB1*03:01, and HLA-DQB1*02:01. The patient is started on standard immunosuppressive therapy including a calcineurin inhibitor and mycophenolate mofetil. Three weeks post-transplant, the patient develops acute onset fever, flank pain, elevated serum creatinine (from baseline 1.5 mg/dL to 4.0 mg/dL), and proteinuria. A renal biopsy reveals acute tubular necrosis and interstitial inflammation with infiltration of mononuclear cells. Flow cytometry analysis of the biopsy sample shows a predominance of CD8+ T cells infiltrating the graft tubules. Further investigation reveals that the recipient's CD8+ T cells recognize intact, donor-derived HLA class I molecules presented on the surface of graft cells.

Which of the following cell types is primarily responsible for the allorecognition process described in this patient?

(A) Recipient B cells recognizing donor MHC class II molecules presented by recipient antigen-presenting cells (APCs).
(B) Recipient γδ T cells recognizing non-classical MHC molecules on graft endothelial cells.
(C) Recipient CD4+ T cells recognizing donor MHC class II molecules presented by recipient APCs.
(D) Recipient CD8+ T cells recognizing donor MHC class I molecules presented directly on graft cells.
(E) Recipient natural killer (NK) cells recognizing graft cells lacking recipient HLA class I molecules.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for potential graft rejection.
- **HLA Mismatch:** Donor HLA-A*02:01, HLA-B*07:02, HLA-DRB1*15:01, HLA-DQB1*06:03. Recipient HLA-A*02:01, HLA-B*44:02, HLA-DRB1*03:01, HLA-DQB1*02:01. There is a mismatch at HLA-B and HLA-DR loci. HLA-A and HLA-DQ are matched. This mismatch is a major risk factor for rejection.
- **Clinical Signs of Rejection:** Fever, flank pain, elevated creatinine, proteinuria three weeks post-transplant. These are classic signs of acute graft rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Acute tubular necrosis, interstitial inflammation with mononuclear cell infiltration. This confirms acute rejection.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Predominance of CD8+ T cells infiltrating graft tubules. This points towards a T-cell mediated rejection, specifically cytotoxic T lymphocyte (CTL)-mediated rejection.
- **Allorecognition Mechanism:** Recipient's CD8+ T cells recognize *intact, donor-derived HLA class I molecules presented on the surface of graft cells*. This is the key phrase describing the mechanism. It indicates *direct* allorecognition.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Graft Rejection:** The immune system recognizes the transplanted organ (graft) as foreign due to differences in HLA (Human Leukocyte Antigen) molecules, which are MHC (Major Histocompatibility Complex) molecules in humans.
- **HLA Molecules:** HLA class I (HLA-A, HLA-B, HLA-C) are expressed on almost all nucleated cells and present endogenous peptides to CD8+ T cells (CTLs). HLA class II (HLA-DR, HLA-DQ, HLA-DP) are primarily expressed on professional antigen-presenting cells (APCs) like dendritic cells, macrophages, and B cells, and present exogenous peptides to CD4+ T helper cells.
- **Allorecognition Pathways:**
    - **Direct Allorecognition:** Recipient T cells directly recognize intact, foreign (donor) MHC molecules expressed on the surface of donor APCs (e.g., dendritic cells) that migrate from the graft to the recipient's lymphoid organs, or directly on graft parenchymal cells. This pathway is particularly important for activating CD8+ T cells (recognizing donor HLA class I) and CD4+ T cells (recognizing donor HLA class II). The vignette specifically describes CD8+ T cells recognizing donor HLA class I directly on graft cells. This is the hallmark of direct allorecognition.
    - **Indirect Allorecognition:** Recipient APCs process donor MHC molecules (which are taken up as foreign proteins) and present peptides derived from these donor MHC molecules on their own MHC molecules (self-MHC) to recipient T cells. This pathway is similar to recognizing foreign antigens from pathogens. It primarily activates CD4+ T cells, which then help activate CD8+ T cells.
- **Cell Types Involved:**
    - **CD8+ T cells (CTLs):** Recognize foreign HLA class I molecules (direct pathway) or peptides from foreign HLA class I presented on self-MHC class I (indirect pathway). They kill target cells expressing the foreign MHC class I. In this case, the vignette highlights CD8+ T cells recognizing donor HLA class I directly on graft cells, causing tubular damage.
    - **CD4+ T cells (Helper T cells):** Recognize foreign HLA class II molecules (direct pathway) or peptides from foreign HLA class II presented on self-MHC class II (indirect pathway). They help activate CD8+ T cells and B cells.
    - **B cells:** Can recognize donor HLA molecules (especially class II) and produce antibodies against them (antibody-mediated rejection).
    - **NK cells:** Can be involved in rejection, particularly if graft cells downregulate HLA class I expression (missing self), making them targets for NK cell killing.
    - **γδ T cells:** Can recognize stress ligands or non-classical MHC molecules on graft cells.

- **Applying to the Vignette:** The patient has acute cellular rejection characterized by CD8+ T cell infiltration and recognition of *intact, donor-derived HLA class I molecules presented on the surface of graft cells*. This is the definition of direct allorecognition mediated by CD8+ T cells. Therefore, the recipient's CD8+ T cells are the primary effector cells responsible for this specific allorecognition process.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Recipient B cells recognizing donor MHC class II molecules presented by recipient APCs. This describes a component of the *indirect* pathway involving B cells recognizing processed donor MHC peptides presented by recipient APCs, or potentially antibody-mediated rejection if B cells produce antibodies against donor MHC II. However, the vignette specifically describes CD8+ T cells recognizing *intact* donor MHC class I molecules directly on graft cells, which is characteristic of the *direct* pathway. B cells are not the primary cells involved in direct allorecognition by T cells.
- (B) Recipient γδ T cells recognizing non-classical MHC molecules on graft endothelial cells. While γδ T cells can play a role in rejection, they typically recognize stress ligands or non-classical MHC molecules, not classical MHC class I or II molecules in the way described. The vignette specifically points to CD8+ T cells recognizing donor HLA class I.
- (C) Recipient CD4+ T cells recognizing donor MHC class II molecules presented by recipient APCs. This describes a component of the *indirect* pathway, where recipient APCs present peptides derived from donor MHC II. It could also describe the direct pathway involving CD4+ T cells recognizing intact donor MHC II on donor APCs. However, the vignette specifically highlights CD8+ T cells recognizing donor HLA class I, not CD4+ T cells recognizing donor HLA class II.
- (D) Recipient CD8+ T cells recognizing donor MHC class I molecules presented directly on graft cells. This perfectly matches the description in the vignette. CD8+ T cells are cytotoxic lymphocytes that recognize foreign MHC class I molecules (direct pathway) or peptides derived from them (indirect pathway). The vignette explicitly states that recipient CD8+ T cells recognize *intact, donor-derived HLA class I molecules presented on the surface of graft cells*, which is the definition of direct allorecognition by CD8+ T cells.
- (E) Recipient natural killer (NK) cells recognizing graft cells lacking recipient HLA class I molecules. NK cells are involved in rejection, particularly in cases where graft cells downregulate HLA class I expression ("missing self"). However, the vignette describes CD8+ T cells recognizing *intact* donor HLA class I molecules, not NK cells targeting cells lacking self-HLA class I.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Direct Allorecognition (Vignette)                                  | Indirect Allorecognition                                       | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR)                               | NK Cell Rejection ("Missing Self")                               |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Cells**       | CD8+ T cells (recognize MHC I), CD4+ T cells (recognize MHC II)   | CD4+ T cells (recognize MHC II peptides on self-MHC II)        | B cells (produce anti-donor HLA antibodies)                      | NK cells                                                           |
| **Target Antigen**      | Intact donor MHC molecules on donor APCs or graft cells           | Processed donor MHC peptides on recipient APCs (self-MHC)       | Intact donor HLA molecules (often MHC class I) on graft endothelium | Graft cells lacking recipient self-MHC class I molecules           |
| **MHC Class Involved**  | Donor MHC I (CD8+), Donor MHC II (CD4+)                             | Donor MHC II peptides on self-MHC II                            | Donor HLA (Class I & II)                                         | Recipient self-MHC I (absence triggers NK cell activation)        |
| **Timing**              | Early (days to weeks)                                               | Later (weeks to months), chronic rejection                     | Acute or chronic                                                 | Can occur early or late                                           |
| **Vignette Match**      | Yes (CD8+ T cells recognize intact donor MHC I on graft cells)     | No                                                             | No                                                               | No                                                               |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The key distinction is between direct and indirect allorecognition. Direct allorecognition involves recipient T cells recognizing intact foreign MHC molecules on donor cells, while indirect allorecognition involves recipient APCs processing foreign MHC molecules and presenting peptides on self-MHC. The vignette clearly describes direct allorecognition by CD8+ T cells recognizing donor HLA class I. Differentiate this from indirect pathways (primarily CD4+ T cells recognizing processed peptides) and antibody-mediated rejection (B cells/antibodies) or NK cell rejection (missing self).

<a id='question-127'></a>

### Question 127: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 27 (expressed)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying acute cellular rejection in organ transplantation, specifically the role of T lymphocytes (CD8+ cytotoxic T cells) in recognizing and destroying allogeneic MHC class I molecules on graft cells. (理解器官移植中急性细胞性排斥的免疫机制，特别是T淋巴细胞（CD8+细胞毒性T细胞）识别和破坏移植物细胞上异体MHC I类分子的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an A blood type, while the recipient is blood type O. He is started on standard immunosuppression including tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, and decreased urine output. Serum creatinine rises from a baseline of 1.2 mg/dL to 4.5 mg/dL. A renal biopsy reveals diffuse interstitial inflammation with lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular epithelial cell injury, and evidence of endothelialitis. Immunofluorescence staining shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the peripheral blood shows a significant increase in CD8+ T cells expressing granzyme B. The patient's crossmatch prior to transplant was negative.

Which of the following is the MOST likely mechanism directly responsible for the observed graft injury in this patient?

(A) Antibody-mediated rejection involving donor-specific antibodies (DSA) targeting the ABO blood group antigens on the graft endothelium.
(B) Acute cellular rejection mediated by recipient CD4+ T helper cells recognizing donor MHC class II molecules on graft antigen-presenting cells.
(C) Acute cellular rejection mediated by recipient CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) recognizing donor MHC class I molecules on graft cells.
(D) Chronic rejection characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes, typically occurring months to years post-transplant.
(E) Hyperacute rejection occurring within minutes to hours post-transplant due to pre-formed anti-ABO antibodies.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, ESRD due to diabetic nephropathy, received kidney transplant. This sets the stage for potential rejection.
- **Transplant Details:** Deceased donor, ABO mismatch (Donor A, Recipient O). This is a significant risk factor for antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), but the crossmatch was negative, suggesting pre-existing anti-A antibodies were not detected or were at low titer.
- **Immunosuppression:** Standard triple therapy (tacrolimus, MMF, prednisone). This indicates an attempt to prevent rejection, but rejection can still occur.
- **Timeline:** 3 weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is typical for acute rejection (cellular or antibody-mediated).
- **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, rising creatinine. These are classic signs of acute graft dysfunction, suggestive of rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Diffuse interstitial inflammation with lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular epithelial cell injury, endothelialitis. These findings are characteristic of acute rejection, particularly acute cellular rejection (ACR). Endothelialitis can be seen in both ACR and AMR.
- **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. C4d is a marker of complement activation, strongly suggesting antibody-mediated injury (AMR). However, C4d can also be present in some forms of ACR, especially those involving endothelialitis.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD8+ T cells expressing granzyme B in peripheral blood. Granzyme B is a cytotoxic protein released by CTLs. This finding points towards a significant activation of CD8+ cytotoxic T cells, which are key mediators of cellular rejection.
- **Negative Crossmatch:** Rules out pre-formed antibodies against donor HLA antigens as the primary cause of hyperacute or early acute rejection. However, *de novo* DSA can develop post-transplant.

**Synthesis:** The patient presents with signs and symptoms of acute graft dysfunction 3 weeks post-transplant, with biopsy findings suggestive of acute rejection (lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular injury, endothelialitis). The presence of C4d suggests an antibody-mediated component, but the significant increase in peripheral blood CD8+ T cells expressing granzyme B strongly implicates cellular rejection mediated by CTLs. The ABO mismatch could contribute to AMR, but the primary mechanism causing the observed cellular damage (tubular injury, endothelialitis) driven by activated cytotoxic lymphocytes is most consistent with acute cellular rejection mediated by CD8+ T cells targeting MHC class I. The negative crossmatch makes hyperacute rejection unlikely. Chronic rejection occurs much later. CD4+ T cells primarily recognize MHC class II, which is less directly cytotoxic to graft cells (though they orchestrate the immune response).

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** Primarily mediated by T lymphocytes recognizing donor MHC molecules (alloantigens) presented on graft cells.
    - **CD4+ T cells:** Recognize donor MHC class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells (APCs) within the graft. They release cytokines (e.g., IFN-γ, TNF-α) that activate macrophages and recruit other inflammatory cells, contributing to graft damage. They are central to orchestrating the rejection response.
    - **CD8+ T cells (CTLs):** Recognize donor MHC class I molecules expressed on virtually all nucleated cells of the graft (including tubular epithelial cells and endothelial cells). Upon recognition, CTLs directly kill graft cells by releasing cytotoxic granules containing perforin and granzymes (like granzyme B). This direct cytotoxicity is a major mechanism of graft cell destruction in ACR.
- **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** Mediated by antibodies (often DSAs) targeting donor HLA antigens or non-HLA antigens (like ABO antigens) on the graft endothelium. Antibody binding activates complement (leading to C4d deposition) and recruits inflammatory cells (e.g., neutrophils, macrophages), causing endothelial injury, microvascular inflammation, and thrombosis.
- **MHC Class I vs. Class II:** MHC class I molecules are expressed on all nucleated cells and present peptides to CD8+ T cells. MHC class II molecules are primarily expressed on APCs (dendritic cells, macrophages, B cells) and present peptides to CD4+ T cells.
- **Granzyme B:** A serine protease released by CTLs and NK cells that enters target cells through perforin pores and activates caspases, leading to apoptosis. Its presence in peripheral blood T cells indicates their cytotoxic potential.
- **C4d:** A stable breakdown product of the complement component C4b. Its deposition in peritubular capillaries is a histological marker of complement activation, strongly associated with AMR. However, it can sometimes be seen in ACR, particularly when endothelialitis is present.
- **ABO Incompatibility:** Can lead to pre-formed or *de novo* anti-A or anti-B antibodies, causing hyperacute or acute AMR. A negative crossmatch prior to transplant reduces the likelihood of significant pre-formed antibodies, but *de novo* DSAs can still develop.

##### 3. Role of CD8+ T cells in Graft Rejection
CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) play a crucial role in acute cellular rejection. They recognize foreign MHC class I molecules (allo-MHC class I) presented on the surface of graft cells. This recognition triggers the CTL to release cytotoxic granules containing perforin and granzymes (such as granzyme B). Perforin creates pores in the target cell membrane, allowing granzymes to enter and initiate apoptosis (programmed cell death). The flow cytometry finding of increased CD8+ T cells expressing granzyme B directly supports the activation and cytotoxic potential of these cells, implicating them as key mediators of the observed graft injury.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Antibody-mediated rejection involving donor-specific antibodies (DSA) targeting the ABO blood group antigens on the graft endothelium. While the ABO mismatch (A donor, O recipient) is a risk factor for AMR, and C4d deposition supports an antibody-mediated component, the primary finding pointing to the *mechanism directly responsible for graft cell injury* is the activated CD8+ CTLs (granzyme B expression). AMR primarily damages endothelial cells, leading to microvascular inflammation, while the biopsy also shows tubular epithelial cell injury, which is a hallmark of CTL-mediated cytotoxicity. Furthermore, the negative crossmatch makes hyperacute rejection (Option E) or pre-existing DSA-mediated acute rejection less likely as the *sole* explanation, although *de novo* DSAs are possible. This option focuses on the antibody mechanism, which is less directly supported by the granzyme B finding compared to the CTL mechanism.
- (B): Acute cellular rejection mediated by recipient CD4+ T helper cells recognizing donor MHC class II molecules on graft antigen-presenting cells. CD4+ T cells are indeed involved in ACR, orchestrating the response by releasing cytokines and activating other immune cells. However, they do not directly kill graft cells expressing MHC class I. The key finding of increased CD8+ T cells expressing granzyme B points specifically to the direct cytotoxic activity of CTLs as the primary mechanism of graft cell destruction in this case.
- (D): Chronic rejection characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes, typically occurring months to years post-transplant. The clinical presentation (3 weeks post-transplant) and biopsy findings (acute inflammation, lymphocytic infiltrates, endothelialitis, tubular injury) are characteristic of acute rejection, not chronic rejection, which involves progressive fibrosis and intimal hyperplasia of graft vessels.
- (E): Hyperacute rejection occurring within minutes to hours post-transplant due to pre-formed anti-ABO antibodies. Hyperacute rejection is characterized by immediate graft thrombosis and necrosis. The patient's presentation occurred at 3 weeks post-transplant, and the pre-transplant crossmatch was negative, making hyperacute rejection highly unlikely. While the ABO mismatch could lead to AMR, it wouldn't manifest as hyperacute rejection in this scenario.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR) - CD8+ Mediated | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) | Hyperacute Rejection | Chronic Rejection |
|-------------------------|-------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------|----------------------|-------------------|
| **Timeline**            | Days to weeks post-transplant                    | Days to weeks post-transplant      | Minutes to hours     | Months to years   |
| **Primary Mediators**   | CD8+ CTLs, CD4+ T cells                         | Antibodies (DSAs), Complement      | Pre-formed Abs       | T cells, Fibrosis |
| **Key Target Cells**    | Graft parenchymal cells (tubules, endothelium)   | Graft endothelium                 | Endothelium          | Vessels, Fibrosis |
| **Histology**           | Lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular injury, endothelialitis | Endothelialitis, C4d deposition, microvascular inflammation | Thrombosis, necrosis | Fibrosis, intimal hyperplasia |
| **Immune Markers**      | Increased CD8+ T cells, Granzyme B              | DSAs, C4d                           | Pre-formed Abs       | Variable          |
| **ABO Mismatch Role**   | Less direct role                                | Significant risk factor             | Significant risk factor | Less direct role  |
| **Crossmatch**          | Negative (usually)                               | Can be positive or negative        | Positive             | N/A               |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute cellular rejection is primarily mediated by T cells recognizing donor MHC molecules. CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) directly kill graft cells expressing MHC class I, while CD4+ T helper cells orchestrate the response. The presence of activated CTLs (e.g., increased CD8+ cells with granzyme B expression) is a key indicator of cellular rejection. Differentiating ACR from AMR is crucial, often relying on biopsy findings (C4d deposition favors AMR, tubular injury favors ACR) and immunological markers (DSAs favor AMR, activated CTLs favor ACR). Hyperacute rejection occurs immediately due to pre-formed antibodies, and chronic rejection involves late-stage fibrosis.

<a id='question-128'></a>

### Question 128: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 28 (codominantly.)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the concept of codominant expression of MHC molecules on hematopoietic cells derived from a donor in a bone marrow transplant recipient, and its implications for graft-versus-host disease (GVHD) and immune reconstitution. (理解骨髓移植中供者来源的造血细胞上MHC分子的共显性表达概念，及其对移植物抗宿主病 (GVHD) 和免疫重建的影响。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with acute myeloid leukemia (AML) undergoes allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (HSCT) from an unrelated donor. The donor is HLA-A*02:01, HLA-B*07:02, and HLA-DRB1*15:01 positive. The patient's pre-transplant conditioning regimen includes fludarabine and cyclophosphamide. Post-transplant, the patient develops severe skin rash, diarrhea, and cholestatic jaundice starting 3 weeks after transplantation. Peripheral blood analysis reveals donor-type neutrophils and monocytes, but the patient's T-cell population is predominantly donor-derived. Flow cytometry on donor-type neutrophils shows expression of both donor and recipient HLA-A, HLA-B, and HLA-DR molecules. Further investigation reveals that the patient's pre-transplant serum contained antibodies against HLA-DRB1*15:01. Which of the following immunological phenomena best explains the presence of both donor and recipient HLA molecules on the surface of donor-derived neutrophils?

(A) Somatic recombination of MHC genes in the donor hematopoietic stem cells.
(B) Expression of MHC class II molecules on donor-derived neutrophils.
(C) Codominant expression of MHC alleles inherited from both the donor and the recipient on donor-derived hematopoietic cells.
(D) Downregulation of MHC class I expression on donor-derived neutrophils due to graft-versus-host disease.
(E) Expression of MHC molecules derived from the recipient's residual leukemic cells contaminating the donor graft.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient:** 52-year-old male with AML undergoing allogeneic HSCT. This sets the stage for potential GVHD and immune reconstitution issues.
- **Donor HLA:** HLA-A*02:01, HLA-B*07:02, HLA-DRB1*15:01 positive. This is the donor's MHC haplotype.
- **Patient Symptoms:** Skin rash, diarrhea, cholestatic jaundice starting 3 weeks post-transplant. These are classic signs of acute GVHD.
- **Peripheral Blood Analysis:** Donor-type neutrophils and monocytes indicate successful engraftment. Predominantly donor-derived T-cells suggest immune reconstitution is occurring via the donor's immune system.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Donor-type neutrophils express *both* donor and recipient HLA-A, HLA-B, and HLA-DR molecules. This is the crucial finding. It indicates that the donor cells are expressing MHC molecules inherited from both the donor and the recipient.
- **Patient History:** Pre-transplant serum contained anti-HLA-DRB1*15:01 antibodies. This suggests the patient might have been previously sensitized (e.g., through transfusion or pregnancy) and could develop antibody-mediated rejection or GVHD.
- **Question:** Asks for the immunological phenomenon explaining the co-expression of donor and recipient HLA molecules on donor-derived neutrophils.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Allogeneic HSCT:** Involves transplanting hematopoietic stem cells (HSCs) from a donor to a recipient. The goal is to reconstitute the recipient's hematopoietic system after myeloablative therapy.
- **MHC Expression:** Major Histocompatibility Complex (MHC) molecules (HLA in humans) are crucial for antigen presentation and immune recognition. MHC class I (HLA-A, -B, -C) is expressed on almost all nucleated cells, while MHC class II (HLA-DR, -DQ, -DP) is primarily expressed on antigen-presenting cells (APCs) like dendritic cells, macrophages, and B cells, but can be induced on other cells under inflammatory conditions.
- **Hematopoietic Cell Differentiation:** HSCs differentiate into various blood cell lineages, including myeloid cells (neutrophils, monocytes) and lymphoid cells (T cells, B cells).
- **MHC Expression on Neutrophils:** Neutrophils express MHC class I molecules, which are important for presenting intracellular antigens to cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs). They do *not* typically express MHC class II molecules under normal physiological conditions.
- **Codominant Expression:** HSCs are pluripotent and contain the genetic material (including MHC genes) of both the donor (if allogeneic) and the recipient (if autologous). When donor HSCs engraft and differentiate in the recipient, the resulting cells (like neutrophils) express MHC molecules encoded by *both* the donor's and the recipient's MHC alleles. This is called codominant expression. For example, a neutrophil derived from a donor HSC will express both the donor's HLA-A*02:01 and the recipient's HLA-A*03:01 (if the recipient had that allele).
- **GVHD:** Graft-versus-host disease occurs when donor T cells recognize the recipient's tissues as foreign and mount an immune attack. The clinical presentation (rash, diarrhea, jaundice) and the timing (3 weeks post-transplant) are consistent with acute GVHD. The presence of donor T cells confirms this possibility.
- **Flow Cytometry Interpretation:** The finding that donor-derived neutrophils express both donor and recipient HLA molecules is a direct consequence of codominant expression of MHC alleles on these cells. This phenomenon is expected after allogeneic HSCT.

##### 3. Relevance to the Question Stem:
The question specifically asks for the explanation of *both* donor and recipient HLA molecules being present on donor-derived neutrophils. Codominant expression (Option C) is the precise immunological principle that accounts for this observation in the context of allogeneic HSCT.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Somatic recombination of MHC genes:** MHC genes are not subject to somatic recombination like immunoglobulin or T-cell receptor genes. MHC alleles are inherited intact from parents. Therefore, somatic recombination cannot explain the co-expression of different HLA alleles. This is incorrect.
- (B) **Expression of MHC class II molecules on donor-derived neutrophils:** While MHC class II expression can be induced on some cell types under certain conditions (like inflammation or viral infection), neutrophils typically do not express MHC class II constitutively or significantly. Furthermore, even if they did, this wouldn't explain the presence of *recipient* MHC class II alleles on donor-derived cells; it would only explain the presence of donor MHC class II alleles. The key finding is the presence of *both* donor and recipient alleles. This is incorrect.
- (D) **Downregulation of MHC class I expression on donor-derived neutrophils due to graft-versus-host disease:** GVHD involves donor T cells attacking recipient tissues. While GVHD can affect various organs, it doesn't typically cause widespread downregulation of MHC class I expression on donor-derived cells like neutrophils. In fact, MHC expression is crucial for immune recognition, and downregulation might be a mechanism to evade immune attack, but it's not the explanation for co-expression of donor and recipient alleles. This is incorrect.
- (E) **Expression of MHC molecules derived from the recipient's residual leukemic cells contaminating the donor graft:** While residual leukemic cells could theoretically contaminate the graft, they would not explain the expression of *both* donor and recipient MHC alleles on the *donor-derived* neutrophils. Donor-derived neutrophils originate from the donor HSCs, not recipient leukemic cells. Furthermore, the flow cytometry specifically identified the neutrophils as donor-type based on other markers (not mentioned but implied). This is incorrect.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Codominant MHC Expression (Correct)                                  | Somatic Recombination (A)             | MHC Class II on Neutrophils (B)        | MHC Downregulation in GVHD (D)        | Recipient Cells in Graft (E)           |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------ |
| **Mechanism**           | Expression of both donor & recipient MHC alleles on donor cells.      | Rearrangement of gene segments.       | Expression of MHC II on neutrophils. | Reduced MHC I expression.            | Recipient cells expressing MHC.       |
| **Cell Type**           | Donor-derived hematopoietic cells (e.g., neutrophils).               | Lymphocytes (T/B cells).              | Neutrophils (usually absent/low).     | Donor-derived neutrophils.            | Recipient leukemic cells.            |
| **MHC Class**           | Both Class I & Class II (codominant).                               | Primarily Class I & Class II (TCR/Ig). | Class II.                            | Class I.                            | Both Class I & Class II.             |
| **Genetic Basis**       | Inheritance of alleles from both donor & recipient.                  | Gene rearrangement.                   | Cell type specificity.                | Immune evasion strategy (not primary). | Contamination of donor graft.         |
| **Relevance to HSCT**   | Expected finding after allogeneic HSCT.                             | Not applicable to MHC.                | Not typical for neutrophils.          | Not the explanation for co-expression. | Not the explanation for donor cell MHC. |
| **Clinical Context**    | Explains MHC typing differences between donor & recipient tissues. | Explains diversity of TCR/Ig.         | Not relevant to co-expression.       | Not relevant to co-expression.       | Not relevant to co-expression.       |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Codominant expression of MHC alleles is a fundamental principle in allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation. Donor-derived cells express MHC molecules inherited from both the donor and the recipient, which is crucial for understanding immune reconstitution, graft-versus-host disease, and graft rejection. Differentiating this from other mechanisms like somatic recombination (which applies to Ig/TCR genes), aberrant MHC expression patterns, or graft contamination is key.
