<a id='question-143'></a>

### Question 143: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 43 (formed)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying acute cellular rejection in solid organ transplantation, specifically the role of T lymphocytes (CD8+ cytotoxic T cells) in directly damaging graft cells, and differentiating this from other types of rejection or infections. (理解实体器官移植中急性细胞性排斥的免疫机制，特别是CD8+细胞毒性T淋巴细胞直接损伤移植细胞的作用，并将其与其他类型的排斥反应或感染区分开来。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an unknown HLA match. He is started on standard immunosuppression consisting of tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals diffuse interstitial edema, tubular cell necrosis, and infiltration of mononuclear cells, predominantly lymphocytes, into the interstitium and tubules. Immunohistochemical staining shows a significant number of CD8+ T cells infiltrating the graft parenchyma, particularly targeting tubular epithelial cells. Flow cytometry analysis of the patient's peripheral blood shows a decrease in CD4+ T cells and an increase in CD8+ T cells compared to baseline. Serum levels of donor-specific antibodies (DSA) are negative.

Which of the following cellular mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for the observed graft injury in this patient?

(A) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells recognizing antibody-coated graft endothelial cells.
(B) Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) mediated by C4b deposition on graft cells leading to MAC formation.
(C) Direct cytotoxicity mediated by CD8+ T lymphocytes recognizing donor MHC class I molecules on graft cells.
(D) Macrophage activation and cytokine release triggered by CD4+ T helper cells recognizing donor MHC class II molecules.
(E) Neutrophil-mediated inflammation and tissue damage resulting from activation via Fc receptors binding to immune complexes.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for potential complications related to transplantation.
*   **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus (calcineurin inhibitor), mycophenolate mofetil (purine synthesis inhibitor), prednisone (corticosteroid). Standard regimen, but rejection can still occur.
*   **Timeline:** 3 weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is characteristic of acute rejection episodes, particularly acute cellular rejection.
*   **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine. Classic signs of acute kidney graft dysfunction.
*   **Biopsy Findings:** Diffuse interstitial edema, tubular cell necrosis, mononuclear cell infiltration (lymphocytes). Suggests inflammation and cellular damage within the graft.
*   **Immunohistochemistry:** Significant CD8+ T cell infiltration targeting tubular epithelial cells. This is a hallmark finding of acute cellular rejection, specifically T cell-mediated cytotoxicity.
*   **Flow Cytometry:** Decrease in CD4+ T cells, increase in CD8+ T cells. Consistent with an immune response targeting the graft, potentially involving cytotoxic T cells.
*   **DSA:** Negative. Rules out antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) as the primary mechanism, although AMR can coexist.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely cellular mechanism responsible for graft injury.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** Primarily mediated by T lymphocytes recognizing donor MHC molecules (alloantigens) on graft cells.
*   **Mechanism:**
    *   **Sensitization Phase:** Recipient T cells (CD4+ and CD8+) recognize donor MHC molecules presented by antigen-presenting cells (APCs) in the graft or draining lymph nodes. This leads to T cell activation and proliferation.
    *   **Effector Phase:** Activated T cells migrate to the graft.
        *   **CD4+ T cells:** Recognize donor MHC class II molecules on APCs. They release cytokines (e.g., IFN-γ, TNF-α) that activate macrophages and recruit other inflammatory cells, contributing to inflammation and tissue damage (indirect pathway).
        *   **CD8+ T cells:** Recognize donor MHC class I molecules expressed on graft parenchymal cells (e.g., tubular epithelial cells in the kidney). They directly kill these cells through cytotoxic mechanisms, including the release of perforin and granzymes, and Fas-FasL interactions (direct pathway).
*   **Vignette Specifics:** The biopsy showing CD8+ T cell infiltration targeting tubular epithelial cells strongly points towards direct CD8+ T cell-mediated cytotoxicity as the primary mechanism. The negative DSA further supports ACR over AMR. The timeline (3 weeks) is typical for ACR.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells recognizing antibody-coated graft endothelial cells.** ADCC involves antibodies (typically IgG) binding to target cells, which are then recognized and killed by effector cells like NK cells (via Fc receptors). This is a mechanism of antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), not typically the primary mechanism in ACR, especially with negative DSA. NK cells are involved in AMR, particularly in cases of HLA-mismatched transplants or pre-formed antibodies.
*   **(B) Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) mediated by C4b deposition on graft cells leading to MAC formation.** CDC is another mechanism of AMR, where donor-specific antibodies activate the classical complement pathway, leading to the formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC) and cell lysis. This requires the presence of DSA, which are negative in this patient.
*   **(D) Macrophage activation and cytokine release triggered by CD4+ T helper cells recognizing donor MHC class II molecules.** While CD4+ T cells play a role in ACR by activating macrophages and releasing cytokines (indirect pathway), the vignette specifically highlights the infiltration of CD8+ T cells targeting parenchymal cells (tubular epithelial cells). The direct cytotoxic effect of CD8+ T cells is considered the major effector mechanism in acute cellular rejection, especially against parenchymal cells expressing MHC class I. Option (D) describes part of the ACR process but doesn't capture the direct cytotoxic mechanism emphasized by the biopsy findings.
*   **(E) Neutrophil-mediated inflammation and tissue damage resulting from activation via Fc receptors binding to immune complexes.** Neutrophils are primarily involved in acute inflammation and bacterial infections. While they can contribute to tissue damage in severe rejection or infection, they are not the primary effector cells in typical acute cellular rejection. Immune complex deposition and neutrophil activation are more characteristic of certain types of vasculitis or glomerulonephritis, not the primary mechanism of ACR described here.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR)                                  | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR)                               |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Mediators**   | T lymphocytes (CD4+ and CD8+)                                  | Antibodies (DSA), Complement, NK cells, Macrophages              |
| **Target Antigens**     | Donor MHC Class I (CD8+), Donor MHC Class II (CD4+)             | Donor HLA antigens (endothelial cells, peritubular capillaries) |
| **Key Cells in Graft**  | CD8+ T cells infiltrating parenchyma, CD4+ T cells in interstitium | Endothelial cells, peritubular capillaries                     |
| **Biopsy Findings**     | T cell infiltrates, tubular cell damage, interstitial edema      | Endothelialitis, C4d deposition, thrombosis, glomerulitis       |
| **Diagnostic Markers**  | Positive DSA (sometimes), Increased CD8+ T cells in blood/graft   | Positive DSA, C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries        |
| **Mechanism Highlighted**| Direct CD8+ cytotoxicity                                        | ADCC, CDC, Complement activation                               |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute cellular rejection is primarily mediated by T cells, particularly CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes directly killing graft cells expressing donor MHC class I. Differentiating ACR from AMR is crucial, relying on biopsy findings (T cell infiltrates vs. endothelialitis/C4d deposition) and serology (DSA status). Remember that CD8+ T cells target parenchymal cells (like tubules in the kidney), while antibodies often target endothelial cells.

<a id='question-144'></a>

### Question 144: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 44 (antibodies)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of antibodies (specifically donor-specific antibodies, DSAs) in acute and chronic antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) following solid organ transplantation, including the target antigens and the resulting pathological consequences. (理解抗体（特别是供体特异性抗体，DSA）在实体器官移植后发生急性抗体介导的排斥反应（AMR）和慢性抗体介导的排斥反应中的作用，包括靶抗原和由此产生的病理后果。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease secondary to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor. His pre-transplant panel reactive antibody (PRA) screen was negative. Post-transplant, he is maintained on standard immunosuppression with tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Six months after transplantation, he presents with a gradual decline in graft function (serum creatinine rising from a baseline of 1.2 mg/dL to 2.8 mg/dL over 3 months), hypertension refractory to multiple agents, and proteinuria (3+ on dipstick). A repeat kidney biopsy reveals evidence of chronic active C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries, endothelialitis, and glomerulitis. Serum analysis shows elevated levels of donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) targeting HLA class I and class II antigens on the donor kidney endothelium. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood reveals a significant increase in CD19+ B cells and CD4+ T cells compared to his baseline pre-transplant levels, despite being on immunosuppression.

Which of the following is the MOST likely mechanism underlying this patient's graft dysfunction?

(A) T-cell mediated rejection involving cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) directly attacking graft tubular epithelial cells.
(B) Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) characterized by the deposition of complement C4d and IgG on the graft endothelium, leading to endothelial injury and microvascular inflammation.
(C) Chronic calcineurin inhibitor (CNI) toxicity, causing afferent arteriolar vasoconstriction and ischemic graft injury.
(D) Acute tubular necrosis (ATN) secondary to ischemia-reperfusion injury during the transplantation procedure.
(E) Cytomegalovirus (CMV) infection causing CMV-associated graft dysfunction, characterized by viral inclusions within endothelial and epithelial cells.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, diabetic nephropathy, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for potential complications post-transplant.
- **Timeline:** 6 months post-transplant. This timeframe is typical for chronic rejection or late acute rejection.
- **Symptoms:** Gradual decline in graft function (rising creatinine), refractory hypertension, proteinuria. These are classic signs of chronic graft dysfunction, often associated with antibody-mediated rejection (AMR).
- **Immunosuppression:** Standard regimen (tacrolimus, mycophenolate, prednisone). Despite this, rejection is occurring.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Chronic active C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries, endothelialitis, glomerulitis. C4d deposition is a hallmark of AMR, indicating complement activation on the graft endothelium. Endothelialitis and glomerulitis are also characteristic features.
- **Serology:** Elevated donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) targeting HLA class I and II. DSAs are antibodies formed by the recipient against donor HLA antigens, which are the primary mediators of AMR.
- **Cellular Analysis:** Increased CD19+ B cells and CD4+ T cells. This suggests ongoing immune activation, potentially contributing to DSA production (B cells) and T cell help for B cells (CD4+ T cells).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely mechanism. The constellation of clinical findings, biopsy results (C4d deposition, endothelialitis), and serology (DSAs) strongly points towards AMR.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** This is a major cause of late graft loss in solid organ transplantation. It is mediated by pre-formed or de novo DSAs.
- **Mechanism:** DSAs bind to donor HLA antigens expressed on the graft endothelium. This binding activates the classical complement pathway, leading to the deposition of complement components (like C4d) and the generation of anaphylatoxins (C3a, C5a). Complement activation recruits inflammatory cells (neutrophils, macrophages) and causes endothelial cell injury. Antibody binding also triggers Fc receptor-mediated effector functions, leading to antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) by NK cells and macrophages, and further endothelial damage.
- **Pathology:** Histological hallmarks include microvascular inflammation (glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis), endothelialitis, C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries, and sometimes thrombosis. Chronic AMR can lead to transplant glomerulopathy (double contours, mesangial expansion, transplant glomerulopathy).
- **HLA Antigens:** HLA class I (HLA-A, B, C) and HLA-II (HLA-DR, DQ, DP) are the primary targets for DSAs.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Can present as acute AMR (rapid graft dysfunction) or chronic AMR (gradual decline, hypertension, proteinuria).
- **Diagnosis:** Based on clinical presentation, rising creatinine, proteinuria, hypertension, positive DSAs, and characteristic biopsy findings (C4d deposition, microvascular inflammation).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR) involves direct cytotoxicity by CD8+ T cells (CTLs) against graft cells or indirect mechanisms involving CD4+ T cells activating macrophages. While TCMR is common, the key findings here (C4d deposition, DSAs, endothelialitis) are specific for AMR. TCMR typically shows interstitial inflammation, tubular injury, and sometimes lymphocytic infiltration into vessels, but not prominent C4d deposition or DSAs targeting endothelium.
- (C): Chronic calcineurin inhibitor (CNI) toxicity (e.g., tacrolimus) can cause chronic nephrotoxicity, leading to vasoconstriction (afferent arteriolar), interstitial fibrosis, tubular atrophy, and proteinuria. However, it does not typically cause C4d deposition or DSA formation. The biopsy findings described are not characteristic of CNI toxicity.
- (D): Acute tubular necrosis (ATN) is usually an early complication occurring shortly after transplantation due to ischemia-reperfusion injury. It presents with a rapid rise in creatinine and is characterized by tubular cell injury and necrosis on biopsy, not C4d deposition or DSAs. The timeline (6 months) and specific biopsy findings rule out ATN as the primary cause of the current dysfunction.
- (E): Cytomegalovirus (CMV) infection is a common complication post-transplant, especially in patients on high-dose immunosuppression. CMV can cause graft dysfunction (CMV nephritis) characterized by viral inclusions in endothelial and epithelial cells on biopsy. While CMV can trigger or exacerbate rejection, the specific findings of C4d deposition and DSAs point directly to AMR as the primary mechanism, although CMV could be a contributing factor or a concurrent issue. The question asks for the MOST likely mechanism based on the provided evidence, which overwhelmingly supports AMR.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) | T-Cell Mediated Rejection (TCMR) | CNI Toxicity | ATN | CMV Infection |
|----------------------|-----------------------------------|-----------------------------------|--------------|-----|---------------|
| **Primary Mediator** | Donor-Specific Antibodies (DSAs)  | T lymphocytes (CTLs, CD4+)       | Tacrolimus   | Ischemia | CMV Virus     |
| **Key Biopsy Finding**| C4d deposition, Endothelialitis   | Interstitial inflammation, Tubulitis | Vasoconstriction, Fibrosis | Tubular necrosis | Viral inclusions |
| **Serology**         | Positive DSAs                    | Negative DSAs                    | N/A          | N/A | Positive CMV PCR/Antigenemia |
| **Timeline**         | Acute or Chronic                  | Acute or Chronic                  | Chronic      | Early | Variable      |
| **Clinical Signs**   | Hypertension, Proteinuria, Rising Cr | Rising Cr, Proteinuria           | Rising Cr, Proteinuria | Rapidly rising Cr | Rising Cr, Fever, Malaise |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) is a critical cause of graft failure, characterized by DSAs binding to donor HLA antigens on the graft endothelium, activating complement (leading to C4d deposition), and causing endothelial injury. Differentiating AMR from T-cell mediated rejection (TCMR) and other causes of graft dysfunction (like CNI toxicity, ATN, infection) relies on recognizing specific clinical features, serological markers (DSAs), and histological findings (C4d deposition, microvascular inflammation). Key differentials include TCMR (lacks C4d, DSAs), CNI toxicity (lacks C4d, DSAs, different histology), ATN (early complication, different histology), and CMV infection (viral inclusions).

<a id='question-145'></a>

### Question 145: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 45 (transfusions,)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological basis of transfusion reactions, specifically acute hemolytic transfusion reactions mediated by pre-formed antibodies against red blood cell antigens, and differentiating them from other types of transfusion reactions or hemolytic anemias. (理解输血反应的免疫学基础，特别是由预先形成的红细胞抗原抗体介导的急性溶血性输血反应，并将其与其他类型的输血反应或溶血性贫血区分开来。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of chronic kidney disease requiring hemodialysis presents to the emergency department with acute onset of fever, chills, flank pain, and dyspnea shortly after receiving a unit of packed red blood cells (PRBCs) for treatment of anemia. His vital signs show a temperature of 38.5°C, heart rate of 110 bpm, blood pressure of 90/60 mmHg, and respiratory rate of 24 breaths/min. Physical examination reveals jaundice and hemoglobinuria (dark urine). Laboratory studies show a positive direct antiglobulin test (DAT, Coombs test), elevated lactate dehydrogenase (LDH), decreased haptoglobin, and evidence of schistocytes on peripheral blood smear. The patient has received multiple blood transfusions in the past due to his chronic kidney disease. A sample of the patient's plasma is tested against a panel of red blood cells, revealing antibodies reactive with the Kell blood group system antigens.

(A) Delayed hemolytic transfusion reaction due to anti-RhD antibodies.
(B) Febrile non-hemolytic transfusion reaction due to recipient antibodies against donor leukocytes.
(C) Acute hemolytic transfusion reaction due to pre-formed anti-Kell antibodies.
(D) Transfusion-associated circulatory overload (TACO) due to rapid infusion of PRBCs.
(E) Acute lung injury (ALI) due to transfusion-related acute lung injury (TRALI) caused by donor antibodies against recipient leukocytes.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with chronic kidney disease (CKD) on hemodialysis, history of multiple transfusions. This history increases the risk of alloimmunization (developing antibodies against foreign antigens, like blood group antigens).
- **Presentation:** Acute onset of fever, chills, flank pain, dyspnea *during or shortly after* a PRBC transfusion. This timing is characteristic of an acute transfusion reaction.
- **Symptoms & Signs:** Fever, chills, flank pain (suggesting kidney involvement/hemoglobinuria), dyspnea (suggesting systemic reaction/hypotension), jaundice (hemolysis), hemoglobinuria (dark urine, indicating free hemoglobin in urine due to intravascular hemolysis).
- **Vital Signs:** Tachycardia, hypotension, tachypnea, fever – signs of systemic inflammatory response and potential shock.
- **Laboratory Findings:**
    - Positive DAT (Coombs test): Indicates antibodies (IgG or IgM) or complement are bound to the patient's red blood cells, confirming an immune-mediated process affecting RBCs.
    - Elevated LDH: Marker of cell lysis, consistent with hemolysis.
    - Decreased haptoglobin: Haptoglobin binds free hemoglobin; decreased levels indicate increased free hemoglobin due to intravascular hemolysis.
    - Schistocytes on peripheral smear: Fragmented red blood cells, can be seen in microangiopathic hemolytic anemia but also in severe intravascular hemolysis.
- **Serology:** Antibodies reactive with the Kell blood group system antigens in the patient's plasma. This identifies the specific alloantibody responsible.
- **Diagnosis:** The constellation of acute symptoms during transfusion, signs of intravascular hemolysis (jaundice, hemoglobinuria, elevated LDH, decreased haptoglobin), positive DAT, and identification of specific anti-Kell antibodies points strongly to an acute hemolytic transfusion reaction (AHTR) mediated by pre-formed antibodies.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Acute Hemolytic Transfusion Reaction (AHTR):** Occurs when pre-formed antibodies in the recipient's plasma react with antigens on the transfused donor red blood cells. This leads to complement activation (classical pathway) and/or Fc receptor-mediated phagocytosis by macrophages (especially in the spleen and liver), resulting in rapid intravascular and/or extravascular hemolysis.
- **Mechanism:**
    1. **Antibody Binding:** Recipient antibodies (e.g., anti-Kell) bind to donor RBC antigens (e.g., Kell antigens).
    2. **Complement Activation:** IgM antibodies are potent activators of the classical complement pathway, leading to C3b deposition on RBCs, formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC, C5b-9), and rapid intravascular lysis. IgG antibodies can also activate complement, but less efficiently, or lead to extravascular hemolysis via Fc receptor-mediated clearance.
    3. **Hemolysis:** RBC destruction releases hemoglobin into the plasma.
    4. **Clinical Manifestations:** Intravascular hemolysis leads to hemoglobinuria, decreased haptoglobin, elevated LDH, and potential renal failure (due to hemoglobin precipitation in tubules). Complement activation and cytokine release cause fever, chills, hypotension, dyspnea, and potentially disseminated intravascular coagulation (DIC) and shock.
    5. **DAT:** The positive DAT confirms the presence of antibody or complement on the patient's RBCs (which are now coated with the donor RBC antigens and the recipient's antibodies).
- **Kell Blood Group System:** A complex system with multiple antigens (e.g., K, k, Kp, Kpc). Anti-Kell antibodies (often anti-K) are relatively common alloantibodies, especially in multiparous women and multiply transfused patients. They can cause severe AHTRs.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Delayed hemolytic transfusion reaction due to anti-RhD antibodies:** Delayed reactions occur days to weeks after transfusion, not acutely. They are typically extravascular and less severe than acute reactions. While anti-RhD can cause hemolysis, the *acute* presentation with fever, chills, hypotension, hemoglobinuria, and positive DAT points away from a delayed reaction. Also, anti-RhD is less likely to be the cause in a patient with CKD who has received multiple transfusions unless they were previously RhD-negative and sensitized, or if the donor was RhD-positive and the recipient was RhD-negative and had anti-D. The vignette specifically identifies anti-Kell antibodies.
- (B) **Febrile non-hemolytic transfusion reaction due to recipient antibodies against donor leukocytes:** This is the most common type of transfusion reaction. It presents with fever and chills, but *without* hemolysis (normal LDH, haptoglobin, negative DAT, no hemoglobinuria/jaundice). The patient's symptoms and lab findings clearly indicate hemolysis.
- (D) **Transfusion-associated circulatory overload (TACO) due to rapid infusion of PRBCs:** TACO presents with respiratory distress, hypertension (initially), tachycardia, and signs of fluid overload (e.g., pulmonary edema). It does *not* cause fever, chills, hemolysis, or a positive DAT. The patient's hypotension and signs of hemolysis rule out TACO.
- (E) **Acute lung injury (ALI) due to transfusion-related acute lung injury (TRALI) caused by donor antibodies against recipient leukocytes:** TRALI presents with acute respiratory distress, hypoxemia, and bilateral pulmonary infiltrates, typically within 6 hours of transfusion. It is caused by donor antibodies (anti-HLA or anti-HNA) reacting with recipient leukocytes, leading to neutrophil activation and pulmonary endothelial damage. While TRALI can cause dyspnea, it does *not* cause fever, chills, hemolysis, jaundice, hemoglobinuria, or a positive DAT. The patient's hemolytic picture rules out TRALI.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                     | Acute Hemolytic Transfusion Reaction (AHTR) | Febrile Non-Hemolytic Transfusion Reaction (FNHTR) | Delayed Hemolytic Transfusion Reaction (DHTR) | TACO                                  | TRALI                                     |
| :-------------------------- | :------------------------------------------ | :---------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------ | :--------------------------------------- |
| **Timing**                  | During/Immediately after transfusion         | During/Immediately after transfusion             | Days to weeks after transfusion             | During/Immediately after transfusion | Within 6 hours of transfusion            |
| **Symptoms**                | Fever, chills, flank pain, dyspnea, hypotension | Fever, chills                                   | Mild fever, jaundice                       | Dyspnea, hypertension (initially)       | Acute respiratory distress, hypoxemia    |
| **Hemolysis**               | Yes (Intravascular +/- Extravascular)       | No                                              | Yes (Extravascular)                        | No                                    | No                                       |
| **Hemoglobinuria**          | Yes                                         | No                                              | No                                         | No                                    | No                                       |
| **Jaundice**                | Yes                                         | No                                              | Yes                                        | No                                    | No                                       |
| **DAT (Coombs Test)**       | Positive                                    | Negative                                        | Positive (often weakly)                    | Negative                               | Negative                                 |
| **LDH**                     | Elevated                                    | Normal                                          | Elevated                                   | Normal                                | Normal                                   |
| **Haptoglobin**             | Decreased                                   | Normal                                          | Decreased                                  | Normal                                | Normal                                   |
| **Cause**                   | Pre-formed recipient antibodies vs. donor RBCs | Recipient antibodies vs. donor leukocytes       | Alloimmunization after prior exposure       | Fluid overload                          | Donor antibodies vs. recipient leukocytes |
| **Key Lab Finding**         | Positive DAT, Hemoglobinuria, ↓Haptoglobin, ↑LDH | Fever                                           | Positive DAT, ↑Indirect Bilirubin           | Signs of fluid overload                | Pulmonary infiltrates, hypoxemia        |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute hemolytic transfusion reactions are severe, immediate reactions caused by pre-formed antibodies against donor red blood cell antigens. Key features include fever, chills, hypotension, flank pain, jaundice, hemoglobinuria, positive DAT, elevated LDH, and decreased haptoglobin. Differentiating AHTR from other transfusion reactions (FNHTR, TACO, TRALI, DHTR) relies on recognizing the presence of hemolysis and the specific timing and clinical features. Remember that FNHTR lacks hemolysis, TACO involves fluid overload, TRALI involves acute lung injury, and DHTR occurs later and is usually less severe.

<a id='question-146'></a>

### Question 146: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 46 (parity,)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological basis of graft rejection, specifically the role of T cells (CD4+ and CD8+) and antibodies in acute cellular rejection and antibody-mediated rejection, and differentiating between them based on clinical presentation and histological findings. (理解移植物排斥的免疫学基础，特别是T细胞（CD4+和CD8+）和抗体在细胞性排斥和抗体介导性排斥中的作用，并根据临床表现和组织学发现区分它们。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an unknown HLA type. He is started on standard immunosuppression with tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and graft tenderness. Serum creatinine rises from a baseline of 1.2 mg/dL to 4.5 mg/dL. Urinalysis shows proteinuria and hematuria. A renal biopsy is performed. Histopathology reveals diffuse interstitial inflammation with lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular epithelial cell injury, and focal endothelialitis. Immunofluorescence staining shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a significant increase in activated CD4+ T cells and CD8+ T cells. Which of the following is the most likely mechanism primarily responsible for this patient's graft dysfunction?

(A) Antibody-mediated rejection involving donor-specific antibodies (DSA) targeting HLA class II antigens on endothelial cells.
(B) Acute cellular rejection mediated primarily by CD4+ T helper cells recognizing donor MHC class II antigens presented by recipient antigen-presenting cells.
(C) Acute cellular rejection mediated primarily by CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) directly recognizing donor MHC class I antigens on graft cells.
(D) Chronic antibody-mediated rejection characterized by transplant glomerulopathy and fibrosis, typically occurring months to years post-transplant.
(E) Hyperacute rejection mediated by pre-formed anti-HLA antibodies leading to immediate graft thrombosis and necrosis.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease (ESRD) from diabetic nephropathy, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for potential graft rejection.
- **Timing:** 3 weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is characteristic of acute rejection episodes (cellular or antibody-mediated). Hyperacute rejection occurs within minutes to hours, while chronic rejection occurs months to years later.
- **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, graft tenderness. These are classic signs of acute graft dysfunction, often associated with rejection.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Rising serum creatinine (1.2 to 4.5 mg/dL), proteinuria, hematuria. These indicate acute kidney injury and graft damage.
- **Histopathology:** Diffuse interstitial inflammation with lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular epithelial cell injury, and focal endothelialitis. This points towards an inflammatory process involving both tubules and interstitium, with some endothelial involvement. Lymphocytic infiltrates are characteristic of cellular rejection.
- **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. C4d is a marker of complement activation, often seen in antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), but can also be present in acute cellular rejection (ACR) with endothelialitis. However, the predominant histological features described (lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular injury) are more typical of ACR.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased activated CD4+ and CD8+ T cells. This strongly supports a T cell-mediated process, characteristic of ACR.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *most likely* mechanism.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Acute Rejection:** Occurs days to weeks after transplantation. Two main types:
    - **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** Primarily mediated by T lymphocytes.
        - **CD4+ T helper cells:** Recognize donor MHC class II molecules on recipient antigen-presenting cells (APCs) and graft cells. They secrete cytokines (IFN-γ, TNF-α) that activate macrophages and CD8+ T cells, contributing to inflammation and graft damage.
        - **CD8+ Cytotoxic T Lymphocytes (CTLs):** Recognize donor MHC class I molecules directly on graft cells (e.g., tubular epithelial cells, endothelial cells). They kill these cells via perforin/granzyme pathways or Fas/FasL interactions, leading to direct graft injury.
        - **Histology:** Interstitial inflammation with lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular injury, endothelialitis.
    - **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** Mediated by antibodies, primarily donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) targeting donor HLA antigens.
        - **Mechanism:** DSAs bind to donor endothelial cells, activating complement (leading to C4d deposition) and recruiting inflammatory cells (macrophages, neutrophils). This causes endothelial injury, microvascular inflammation (glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis), and thrombosis.
        - **Histology:** Glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis, C4d deposition, thrombosis.
- **Distinguishing ACR and AMR:** While C4d deposition can occur in both, the predominant histological features in this case (diffuse interstitial inflammation, lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular injury) and the significant increase in activated CD4+ and CD8+ T cells strongly favor ACR. The focal endothelialitis can be seen in both, but is often more prominent in AMR. However, the overall picture points more towards T-cell mediated damage.
- **Hyperacute Rejection:** Occurs within minutes to hours due to pre-formed antibodies against donor ABO or HLA antigens. Leads to immediate thrombosis and necrosis. Not consistent with the 3-week timeframe.
- **Chronic Rejection:** Occurs months to years post-transplant. Characterized by fibrosis, transplant glomerulopathy (in kidneys), and gradual loss of graft function. Not consistent with the acute presentation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Antibody-mediated rejection involving DSAs targeting HLA class II antigens on endothelial cells. While C4d deposition is present, the predominant histological findings (diffuse interstitial inflammation, lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular injury) and the strong evidence of activated T cells (CD4+ and CD8+) make ACR more likely than AMR as the *primary* mechanism. AMR typically features more prominent endothelialitis, glomerulitis, and peritubular capillaritis.
- (B): Acute cellular rejection mediated primarily by CD4+ T helper cells recognizing donor MHC class II antigens. CD4+ T cells are involved in ACR, but CD8+ CTLs also play a crucial role in directly killing graft cells, especially tubular epithelial cells. Option (C) better reflects the combined contribution of both major T cell subsets in typical ACR.
- (D): Chronic antibody-mediated rejection characterized by transplant glomerulopathy and fibrosis. This occurs much later (months to years) and has distinct histological features (fibrosis, glomerulopathy) not described in the vignette.
- (E): Hyperacute rejection mediated by pre-formed anti-HLA antibodies. This occurs immediately (minutes to hours) post-transplant, not at 3 weeks.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR) | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) | Hyperacute Rejection | Chronic Rejection |
|----------------------|---------------------------------|-----------------------------------|----------------------|-------------------|
| **Timing**           | Days to weeks                   | Days to weeks                     | Minutes to hours     | Months to years   |
| **Mediator**         | T cells (CD4+, CD8+)             | Antibodies (DSAs)                 | Pre-formed Abs      | Abs, T cells      |
| **Key Histology**    | Lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular injury, endothelialitis | Glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis, C4d, thrombosis | Thrombosis, necrosis | Fibrosis, glomerulopathy |
| **C4d Deposition**   | Possible, especially with endothelialitis | Characteristic                  | Not typical         | Possible         |
| **T Cell Activation**| Increased CD4+ & CD8+           | Less direct evidence             | Not primary         | Variable         |
| **Vignette Fit**     | **Best Fit**                    | Less likely primary mechanism    | No                  | No                |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute graft rejection typically occurs within the first few weeks post-transplant and can be either cellular (T-cell mediated) or antibody-mediated. Cellular rejection involves CD4+ and CD8+ T cells causing inflammation and direct cell killing, respectively, leading to lymphocytic infiltrates and tubular injury. Antibody-mediated rejection involves DSAs activating complement and causing endothelial injury. Differentiating between ACR and AMR relies on clinical presentation, timing, histological features (especially C4d deposition and specific vascular lesions), and evidence of T cell activation. This case, with its timing, histological findings (lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular injury), and increased activated T cells, points most strongly to acute cellular rejection involving both CD4+ and CD8+ T cells.

<a id='question-147'></a>

### Question 147: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 47 (previous)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying acute cellular rejection in solid organ transplantation, specifically the role of T lymphocytes (CD8+ cytotoxic T cells) and their recognition of alloantigens presented by MHC class I molecules on graft cells. (理解实体器官移植中急性细胞性排斥的免疫机制，特别是T淋巴细胞（CD8+细胞毒性T细胞）的作用以及它们对移植细胞表面MHC I类分子呈递的异体抗原的识别。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with two HLA mismatches at the A and B loci. Post-transplant, he is initiated on standard immunosuppression with tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks after transplantation, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals diffuse interstitial inflammation with lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular epithelial cell damage, and evidence of endothelialitis. Immunofluorescence staining shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the biopsy tissue shows a significant increase in CD8+ T cells infiltrating the graft parenchyma, particularly targeting tubular epithelial cells. Serum levels of donor-specific antibodies (DSA) are negative. Which of the following immunological mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for this patient's acute graft dysfunction?

(A) Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) involving complement activation and endothelial injury.
(B) Acute cellular rejection (ACR) mediated by CD4+ helper T cells recognizing donor MHC class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells.
(C) Acute cellular rejection (ACR) mediated by CD8+ cytotoxic T cells recognizing donor MHC class I molecules on graft cells.
(D) Chronic rejection characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes, typically occurring months to years post-transplant.
(E) Hyperacute rejection mediated by pre-formed anti-donor antibodies leading to immediate graft thrombosis.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for potential transplant complications.
- **Transplant Details:** Deceased donor, two HLA mismatches (A and B loci). HLA mismatches are a major risk factor for rejection.
- **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus (calcineurin inhibitor), mycophenolate mofetil (purine synthesis inhibitor), prednisone (corticosteroid). Standard regimen, but rejection can still occur.
- **Timeline:** Three weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is typical for acute rejection episodes.
- **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine. Classic signs of acute graft dysfunction.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Diffuse interstitial inflammation, lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular epithelial cell damage, endothelialitis. These are hallmarks of acute rejection.
- **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. While C4d deposition is often associated with AMR, it can also be seen in ACR, particularly in cases with significant inflammation and endothelial activation. However, the predominant cellular infiltrate and lack of DSA point away from AMR as the primary mechanism.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD8+ T cells infiltrating graft parenchyma, targeting tubular epithelial cells. This is a key finding pointing towards CD8+ T cell-mediated cytotoxicity.
- **DSA:** Negative. This makes AMR less likely as the primary driver, although DSAs can sometimes be present in ACR.

The combination of clinical signs of acute graft dysfunction, biopsy findings showing lymphocytic infiltrates and tubular damage, and the specific identification of increased CD8+ T cells targeting graft cells strongly suggests acute cellular rejection mediated by CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** Primarily mediated by T lymphocytes recognizing donor alloantigens.
    - **CD4+ T cells:** Recognize donor MHC class II molecules presented by host antigen-presenting cells (APCs) or donor APCs within the graft. They orchestrate the immune response by releasing cytokines (e.g., IFN-γ, TNF-α) that activate macrophages and CD8+ T cells, and by directly participating in cell-mediated cytotoxicity.
    - **CD8+ T cells:** Recognize donor MHC class I molecules expressed on virtually all nucleated cells of the graft (including tubular epithelial cells). Upon recognition, CD8+ T cells directly kill graft cells through mechanisms involving perforin/granzyme release and Fas/FasL interactions. This is often referred to as cytotoxic T lymphocyte (CTL)-mediated rejection.
- **MHC Class I vs. Class II:** MHC class I molecules (HLA-A, HLA-B, HLA-C) are expressed on all nucleated cells and present peptides derived from intracellular proteins (including viral antigens and alloantigens) to CD8+ T cells. MHC class II molecules (HLA-DR, HLA-DQ, HLA-DP) are primarily expressed on professional APCs (dendritic cells, macrophages, B cells) and present peptides derived from extracellular proteins (including alloantigens) to CD4+ T cells.
- **Pathogenesis in this case:** The biopsy shows CD8+ T cells infiltrating the graft parenchyma and targeting tubular epithelial cells. Tubular epithelial cells express MHC class I molecules. Therefore, the CD8+ T cells are recognizing donor MHC class I molecules (specifically HLA-A and HLA-B, given the mismatches) presented on these cells, leading to direct cytotoxicity and graft damage. This is the hallmark of CD8+ T cell-mediated ACR.
- **C4d Deposition:** C4d is a breakdown product of the complement C4 protein. Its deposition in peritubular capillaries is a marker of complement activation, often seen in antibody-mediated rejection (AMR). However, it can also occur in ACR due to endothelial activation and inflammation. The absence of DSAs makes AMR less likely as the primary cause, although C4d positivity can be seen in some ACR cases. The dominant finding of CD8+ T cell infiltration points strongly towards ACR.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) involves antibodies (often DSAs) binding to donor HLA antigens on endothelial cells, leading to complement activation, endothelial injury, inflammation, and thrombosis. While C4d deposition is seen in AMR, the key finding here is the prominent CD8+ T cell infiltrate targeting parenchymal cells (tubular epithelium), and negative DSAs. AMR typically affects peritubular capillaries and glomeruli more prominently, causing endothelialitis and microvascular inflammation (MVI). This option is incorrect because the primary mechanism identified is cellular, not antibody-mediated.
- (B): Acute cellular rejection (ACR) can be mediated by CD4+ T cells, which recognize donor MHC class II molecules. However, the vignette specifically highlights the increased infiltration of CD8+ T cells targeting tubular epithelial cells. While CD4+ T cells contribute to ACR, the dominant cytotoxic mechanism described in the biopsy findings points to CD8+ T cells. This option is incorrect because it focuses on CD4+ T cells, whereas the key finding is CD8+ T cell infiltration and cytotoxicity.
- (D): Chronic rejection is a slow process occurring months to years after transplantation, characterized by progressive fibrosis, vascular changes (e.g., transplant glomerulopathy, chronic active antibody-mediated rejection), and eventual graft failure. The timeframe (3 weeks) and acute inflammatory findings are inconsistent with chronic rejection. This option is incorrect due to the acute timeframe and pathological findings.
- (E): Hyperacute rejection occurs within minutes to hours after transplantation due to pre-formed antibodies against donor antigens (e.g., ABO blood group antigens, HLA antigens). It leads to rapid graft thrombosis and necrosis. The patient's presentation at 3 weeks rules out hyperacute rejection. This option is incorrect due to the timeframe.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Acute Cellular Rejection (CD8+) | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) | Chronic Rejection | Hyperacute Rejection |
|----------------------|---------------------------------|-----------------------------------|-------------------|----------------------|
| **Timeframe**        | Days to weeks                   | Days to weeks                     | Months to years   | Minutes to hours     |
| **Mediator**         | T cells (CD8+)                  | Antibodies (DSAs)                 | T cells, Abs      | Pre-formed Abs      |
| **Target**           | Graft parenchymal cells          | Endothelium, peritubular capillaries | Vessels, fibrosis | Endothelium         |
| **Key Pathology**    | Tubulitis, interstitial infiltrate | Endothelialitis, MVI, C4d+       | Fibrosis, vascular changes | Thrombosis, necrosis |
| **Biopsy Markers**   | CD8+ T cell infiltrate          | C4d deposition, DSAs              | Fibrosis, glomerulopathy | N/A                  |
| **DSA Status**       | Usually negative                | Usually positive                  | Variable          | N/A                  |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute cellular rejection in solid organ transplantation is primarily mediated by T lymphocytes recognizing donor alloantigens. CD8+ cytotoxic T cells recognize MHC class I molecules on graft cells, leading to direct cell killing. Differentiating ACR from AMR is crucial, often relying on biopsy findings (cellular infiltrate vs. endothelialitis, C4d deposition) and DSA status. Remember that ACR can occur early (weeks) or late, while chronic rejection is a late complication. Hyperacute rejection is immediate.

<a id='question-148'></a>

### Question 148: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 48 (transplants)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the immunological mechanisms underlying acute cellular rejection in solid organ transplantation, specifically the role of T lymphocytes (CD8+ cytotoxic T cells) and their recognition of alloantigens presented by MHC class I molecules on graft cells. (理解实体器官移植中急性细胞性排斥的免疫机制，特别是T淋巴细胞（CD8+细胞毒性T细胞）的作用及其对移植细胞表面MHC I类分子呈递的异体抗原的识别。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with two HLA mismatches at the A and B loci. Post-transplant, he is initiated on standard immunosuppression with tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks after transplantation, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals diffuse interstitial inflammation with lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular epithelial cell damage, and evidence of endothelialitis. Immunofluorescence staining shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the biopsy sample identifies a significant increase in CD8+ T cells infiltrating the graft parenchyma, particularly targeting tubular epithelial cells. Serum levels of donor-specific antibodies (DSA) are negative. Which of the following immunological mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for this patient's acute graft dysfunction?

(A) Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) involving complement activation and endothelial injury.
(B) Acute cellular rejection (ACR) mediated by CD4+ helper T cells recognizing donor MHC class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells.
(C) Acute cellular rejection (ACR) mediated by CD8+ cytotoxic T cells recognizing donor MHC class I molecules on graft cells.
(D) Chronic rejection characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes, typically occurring months to years post-transplant.
(E) Hyperacute rejection mediated by pre-formed anti-donor antibodies leading to immediate graft thrombosis.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for potential transplant complications.
- **Transplant Details:** Deceased donor, two HLA mismatches (A and B loci). HLA mismatches are a major risk factor for rejection.
- **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus (calcineurin inhibitor), mycophenolate mofetil (purine synthesis inhibitor), prednisone (corticosteroid). Standard regimen, but rejection can still occur.
- **Timeline:** Three weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is typical for acute rejection episodes.
- **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine. Classic signs of acute graft dysfunction.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Diffuse interstitial inflammation, lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular epithelial cell damage, endothelialitis. These are hallmarks of acute rejection.
- **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. C4d is a marker of complement activation, often seen in AMR, but can also be present in ACR, especially if there's some antibody involvement or overlap. However, the primary infiltrate is lymphocytic.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD8+ T cells infiltrating graft parenchyma, targeting tubular epithelial cells. This is a key finding pointing towards CD8+ T cell-mediated cytotoxicity.
- **DSA:** Negative. This makes pure AMR less likely, although DSA can be negative in some cases of AMR or in mixed rejection.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely immunological mechanism.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Acute Rejection:** Occurs within the first few months post-transplant. Can be cellular (ACR) or antibody-mediated (AMR), or mixed.
- **ACR:** Primarily mediated by T lymphocytes recognizing donor alloantigens.
    - **CD4+ T cells:** Recognize donor MHC class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells (APCs) within the graft. They release cytokines (e.g., IFN-γ, TNF-α) that activate macrophages and recruit other inflammatory cells, contributing to graft damage.
    - **CD8+ T cells:** Recognize donor MHC class I molecules expressed on virtually all nucleated graft cells (including tubular epithelial cells). Upon recognition, they directly kill graft cells via cytotoxic mechanisms (perforin/granzyme release, Fas/FasL interaction).
- **AMR:** Mediated by antibodies (DSA) directed against donor HLA or non-HLA antigens. Antibodies bind to endothelial cells, activating complement (leading to C4d deposition) and recruiting inflammatory cells (e.g., neutrophils, macrophages), causing endothelialitis and microvascular injury.
- **Vignette Analysis:** The biopsy shows lymphocytic infiltrates (consistent with ACR), tubular epithelial cell damage (consistent with CD8+ cytotoxicity), endothelialitis (can be seen in both ACR and AMR), and C4d deposition (more typical of AMR but not exclusive). Crucially, flow cytometry shows a significant increase in CD8+ T cells infiltrating the graft and targeting tubular epithelial cells. The negative DSA result further points away from pure AMR. Therefore, the most likely mechanism is CD8+ T cell-mediated ACR.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) involving complement activation and endothelial injury. While C4d deposition is present, the primary infiltrate identified by flow cytometry is CD8+ T cells targeting epithelial cells, and DSA are negative. AMR typically involves DSA, prominent endothelialitis, and often lacks a dominant CD8+ T cell infiltrate targeting parenchymal cells. This option focuses on the antibody-mediated pathway, which is less supported by the specific findings.
- (B): Acute cellular rejection (ACR) mediated by CD4+ helper T cells recognizing donor MHC class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells. CD4+ T cells are involved in ACR, but the key finding here is the prominent CD8+ T cell infiltrate directly targeting graft parenchymal cells (tubular epithelial cells). CD4+ T cells primarily activate APCs and macrophages, contributing to inflammation but not direct cytotoxicity against epithelial cells to this extent.
- (D): Chronic rejection characterized by fibrosis and vascular changes, typically occurring months to years post-transplant. The patient is only three weeks post-transplant, which is too early for chronic rejection. Chronic rejection involves progressive fibrosis, graft arteriosclerosis, and loss of graft function over a longer period.
- (E): Hyperacute rejection mediated by pre-formed anti-donor antibodies leading to immediate graft thrombosis. Hyperacute rejection occurs within minutes to hours of transplantation due to pre-existing antibodies against donor antigens (e.g., ABO incompatibility, prior sensitization). This patient is three weeks post-transplant, ruling out hyperacute rejection.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Acute Cellular Rejection (CD8+) | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR) | Hyperacute Rejection | Chronic Rejection |
|-------------------------|---------------------------------|-----------------------------------|----------------------|-------------------|
| **Timing**              | Days to months                  | Days to months                    | Minutes to hours     | Months to years   |
| **Mediator**            | T cells (CD8+)                   | Antibodies (DSA)                  | Pre-formed Abs      | T cells, Abs, Fibrosis |
| **Target**              | Graft Parenchymal Cells          | Endothelium                       | Endothelium          | Vessels, Fibrosis |
| **Key Histology**       | Lymphocytic infiltrate, cell damage | Endothelialitis, C4d deposition   | Thrombosis          | Fibrosis, Arteriolopathy |
| **Key Immune Cells**    | CD8+ T cells                     | Neutrophils, Macrophages          | N/A                 | T cells, Macrophages |
| **DSA Status**          | Usually Negative                 | Usually Positive                  | N/A                 | Variable          |
| **C4d Deposition**      | Variable, less prominent         | Characteristic                    | N/A                 | Variable          |
| **Patient Presentation** | Acute graft dysfunction          | Acute graft dysfunction          | Immediate graft loss | Gradual graft loss |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute cellular rejection is primarily mediated by T cells. CD8+ T cells directly kill graft cells expressing donor MHC class I, while CD4+ T cells orchestrate the inflammatory response. Distinguish ACR from AMR based on the predominant cell type, target tissue (parenchyma vs. endothelium), presence of DSA, and C4d deposition. Hyperacute rejection is immediate, and chronic rejection occurs much later with fibrosis.

<a id='question-149'></a>

### Question 149: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 49 (cytotoxic)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the cellular and molecular mechanisms of acute cellular rejection in solid organ transplantation, specifically the role of cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) and their interaction with graft cells, including the recognition of alloantigens presented by MHC class I molecules. (理解实体器官移植中急性细胞性排斥的细胞和分子机制，特别是细胞毒性 T 淋巴细胞 (CTL) 的作用及其与移植物细胞的相互作用，包括识别由 MHC I 类分子呈递的异体抗原。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with one HLA mismatch at the HLA-A locus. Post-transplant, he is maintained on immunosuppressive therapy including tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks after transplantation, he develops fever, flank pain, elevated serum creatinine (from baseline 1.2 mg/dL to 3.5 mg/dL), and decreased urine output. A renal biopsy reveals interstitial edema, inflammatory cell infiltrates primarily composed of lymphocytes, and evidence of tubular cell necrosis. Immunofluorescence staining shows deposition of C4d in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the peripheral blood lymphocytes shows an increased percentage of CD8+ T cells expressing the activation marker CD69 and the cytotoxic molecule perforin. Further analysis reveals that these CD8+ T cells recognize peptides derived from the donor HLA-A allele presented by MHC class I molecules on the surface of graft cells.

Which of the following cellular mechanisms is MOST directly responsible for the observed graft injury in this patient?

(A) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by NK cells recognizing donor HLA-specific antibodies.
(B) Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) mediated by donor-specific antibodies binding to donor MHC class II molecules on graft endothelial cells.
(C) Direct cytotoxicity mediated by recipient CD8+ T lymphocytes recognizing donor MHC class I molecules presenting alloantigens on graft cells.
(D) Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) involving the formation of anti-donor HLA antibodies leading to endothelial cell activation and injury.
(E) Macrophage-mediated inflammation driven by recipient CD4+ T helper cells secreting IFN-γ, activating macrophages to phagocytose graft cells.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the context for potential graft rejection.
- **Immunosuppression:** Tacrolimus (calcineurin inhibitor), mycophenolate mofetil (purine synthesis inhibitor), prednisone (corticosteroid). Despite immunosuppression, rejection occurs.
- **Timing:** Three weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is typical for acute rejection, particularly acute cellular rejection (ACR).
- **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, elevated creatinine, decreased urine output. These are classic signs of acute graft dysfunction, suggestive of rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Interstitial edema, lymphocyte infiltrates (primarily lymphocytes), tubular cell necrosis. These findings are characteristic of ACR.
- **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. While C4d deposition is classically associated with antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), it can also be seen in some cases of cellular rejection, particularly if there is overlap or if the biopsy is interpreted in the context of other findings. However, the primary histological findings point towards cellular rejection.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD8+ T cells, CD69 expression (activation marker), perforin expression (cytotoxic molecule). This strongly indicates activation and cytotoxic potential of CD8+ T cells.
- **Mechanism:** CD8+ T cells recognize peptides from donor HLA-A presented by MHC class I on graft cells. This is the hallmark of direct allorecognition by cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs).

These clues collectively point towards acute cellular rejection mediated primarily by CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes recognizing donor MHC class I molecules on graft cells.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** This is a T cell-mediated immune response against the transplanted organ. It typically occurs within the first few months post-transplant.
- **Cellular Players:** Primarily CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) and CD4+ helper T cells.
- **Mechanism of CD8+ CTLs:** CTLs recognize foreign MHC class I molecules (alloantigens in this case) presenting peptides derived from the donor organ on the surface of graft cells (e.g., tubular epithelial cells, endothelial cells). This recognition occurs via the T cell receptor (TCR) on the CTL.
- **MHC Class I Presentation:** MHC class I molecules are expressed on virtually all nucleated cells. They present endogenous peptides (including viral peptides or, in this case, donor alloantigens) to CD8+ T cells.
- **CTL Activation & Cytotoxicity:** Upon recognition of the alloantigen-MHC class I complex, the CTL becomes activated (indicated by CD69 expression). Activated CTLs kill target cells through several mechanisms, including:
    - **Perforin/Granzyme Pathway:** CTLs release perforin, which forms pores in the target cell membrane, and granzymes, which enter the target cell and induce apoptosis. The vignette specifically mentions increased perforin expression.
    - **Fas/FasL Pathway:** CTLs express Fas ligand (FasL), which binds to Fas (CD95) on target cells, triggering apoptosis.
- **Role of CD4+ T cells:** CD4+ helper T cells also play a role in ACR by recognizing donor MHC class II molecules and secreting cytokines (like IFN-γ) that activate macrophages and enhance the cytotoxic activity of CD8+ T cells. However, the direct killing of graft cells is primarily mediated by CD8+ CTLs.
- **Distinction from Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** AMR involves antibodies (typically anti-HLA) that bind to donor antigens on endothelial cells, leading to complement activation, endothelial damage, and inflammation. While C4d deposition can be seen in AMR, the predominant histological features and the specific findings of activated, cytotoxic CD8+ T cells recognizing MHC class I point towards ACR as the primary mechanism.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC)** involves antibodies binding to target cells, which are then recognized and killed by effector cells like NK cells expressing Fc receptors. While antibodies can be involved in rejection (AMR), the vignette emphasizes activated CD8+ T cells recognizing MHC class I, not ADCC mediated by NK cells. NK cells primarily recognize cells lacking MHC class I or expressing stress ligands, not specific alloantigens presented by MHC class I.
- (B): **Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC)** involves antibodies binding to target cells, activating the complement cascade, and leading to cell lysis via the membrane attack complex (MAC). This is a mechanism of AMR, typically involving antibodies against MHC class II. The vignette points to CD8+ T cell cytotoxicity recognizing MHC class I, not CDC mediated by antibodies against MHC class II.
- (D): **Antibody-mediated rejection (AMR)** is characterized by donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) targeting donor HLA antigens, primarily on endothelial cells. This leads to complement activation, endothelial injury, inflammation, and microvascular damage. While C4d deposition can be seen, the primary histological findings (lymphocyte infiltrates, tubular necrosis) and the specific identification of activated CD8+ CTLs recognizing MHC class I are more characteristic of ACR. AMR typically involves DSA binding to MHC class II on endothelial cells.
- (E): **Macrophage-mediated inflammation** is part of the inflammatory response in ACR, often driven by cytokines like IFN-γ secreted by CD4+ T cells. However, the *direct* killing of graft cells, as evidenced by the activated CD8+ T cells expressing perforin and recognizing MHC class I, is the most direct mechanism of injury described in the vignette. Macrophages contribute to inflammation and tissue damage but are not the primary cytotoxic effector cells in this specific scenario highlighted by the flow cytometry data.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR)                                  | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR)                                     |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Mediators**   | CD8+ Cytotoxic T Lymphocytes (CTLs), CD4+ Helper T cells        | Donor-Specific Antibodies (DSAs)                                       |
| **Target Antigens**     | Donor MHC Class I (recognized by CD8+ CTLs), MHC Class II (CD4+) | Donor HLA antigens, primarily on endothelial cells (MHC Class I & II) |
| **Mechanism of Injury** | Direct CTL cytotoxicity (perforin/granzyme, Fas/FasL)           | Complement activation, ADCC, endothelial cell activation/injury        |
| **Histology**           | Lymphocyte infiltrates, tubular cell necrosis                  | Endothelialitis, microvascular inflammation, C4d deposition            |
| **Key Markers**         | Increased CD8+ T cells, CD69, perforin                        | Donor-Specific Antibodies (DSAs)                                       |
| **Timing**              | Days to months post-transplant                                  | Can occur anytime post-transplant                                      |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute cellular rejection is primarily mediated by T lymphocytes, especially CD8+ CTLs, recognizing donor MHC class I molecules on graft cells. This leads to direct cytotoxicity and graft injury. Differentiating ACR from AMR is crucial, as the mechanisms and treatments differ. Key features of ACR include T cell infiltrates and CTL activation markers, while AMR involves DSAs and endothelial damage.

<a id='question-150'></a>

### Question 150: Transplantation: Part 3 Focus 50 (Hypersensitivity)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of acute cellular rejection in organ transplantation, specifically the role of T lymphocytes (CD8+ cytotoxic T cells) in directly damaging graft cells, and the immunological mechanisms underlying this process. (考察器官移植中急性细胞性排斥反应，特别是CD8+细胞毒性T淋巴细胞直接损伤移植细胞的免疫机制。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an unknown HLA type. He is started on standard immunosuppressive therapy, including tacrolimus, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals diffuse interstitial inflammation with lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular epithelial cell necrosis, and endothelialitis. Immunofluorescence staining shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the biopsy infiltrate shows a predominance of CD8+ T cells expressing granzyme B and perforin. The patient's serum creatinine continues to rise despite dose adjustments of immunosuppressants.

Which of the following is the MOST likely mechanism directly responsible for the observed graft injury in this patient?

(A) Antibody-mediated complement activation leading to endothelial damage and thrombosis.
(B) CD4+ T helper cell activation leading to macrophage recruitment and cytokine-mediated tubular injury.
(C) CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocyte-mediated direct killing of graft tubular epithelial cells.
(D) Neutrophil-mediated inflammation causing acute tubular necrosis.
(E) Pre-formed anti-donor antibodies binding to graft endothelium, causing complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC).

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for potential graft rejection.
- **Timeline:** 3 weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is characteristic of acute rejection, which can be cellular or antibody-mediated.
- **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine. These are classic signs of acute graft dysfunction, suggesting rejection.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Diffuse interstitial inflammation with lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular epithelial cell necrosis, endothelialitis. This points towards an inflammatory process involving lymphocytes directly attacking graft tissue.
- **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. While C4d deposition is often associated with antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), it can also be seen in acute cellular rejection (ACR), particularly in cases with mixed rejection or endothelial injury. However, the predominant cell type is key.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Predominance of CD8+ T cells expressing granzyme B and perforin in the infiltrate. This is the most crucial clue. CD8+ T cells are cytotoxic lymphocytes (CTLs) equipped with perforin and granzyme B, which are directly involved in killing target cells. Their presence in high numbers within the graft parenchyma strongly suggests cellular rejection.
- **Clinical Course:** Rising creatinine despite immunosuppression indicates ongoing rejection.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** Primarily mediated by T lymphocytes, specifically CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) and CD4+ T helper cells.
    - **CD8+ CTLs:** Recognize donor MHC class I molecules on graft cells (e.g., tubular epithelial cells, endothelial cells) as foreign. Upon recognition, they release perforin (forms pores in the target cell membrane) and granzyme B (enters through the pores and activates caspases, leading to apoptosis). This direct killing of graft cells is the hallmark of ACR.
    - **CD4+ T helper cells:** Recognize donor MHC class II molecules, primarily on antigen-presenting cells (APCs) within the graft. They release cytokines (e.g., IFN-γ, TNF-α) that activate macrophages and recruit other inflammatory cells, contributing to graft injury.
- **Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR):** Mediated by pre-formed or de novo antibodies against donor HLA antigens or other graft antigens.
    - **Mechanisms:**
        - **Complement-Dependent Cytotoxicity (CDC):** Antibodies bind to graft endothelium, activate the classical complement pathway, leading to membrane attack complex (MAC) formation and cell lysis.
        - **Antibody-Dependent Cell-Mediated Cytotoxicity (ADCC):** Antibodies bind to graft cells, and NK cells or other effector cells recognize the Fc portion of the antibody, leading to target cell killing.
        - **Complement-Independent Mechanisms:** Antibodies bind to endothelial cells, triggering inflammation, thrombosis, and endothelial dysfunction. C4d deposition is a marker of complement activation, often seen in AMR, but not exclusive to it.
- **The Vignette's Specifics:** The biopsy shows lymphocytic infiltrates (consistent with both ACR and AMR), tubular necrosis, and endothelialitis. However, the *predominance of CD8+ T cells expressing granzyme B and perforin* strongly points towards direct cytotoxic killing by CD8+ CTLs as the primary mechanism of injury. This is the defining feature of acute cellular rejection.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Antibody-mediated complement activation leading to endothelial damage and thrombosis. This describes a key mechanism of Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR). While C4d deposition is present, the flow cytometry showing predominant CD8+ T cells with cytotoxic markers makes AMR less likely as the *primary* mechanism described. AMR typically involves more prominent endothelial injury and thrombosis, often with fewer infiltrating lymphocytes directly within the parenchyma compared to ACR.
- (B) CD4+ T helper cell activation leading to macrophage recruitment and cytokine-mediated tubular injury. CD4+ T cells are involved in ACR, but the vignette specifically highlights the *predominance* of CD8+ T cells expressing cytotoxic molecules (granzyme B, perforin). While CD4+ cells contribute, the direct killing mechanism described points more strongly to CD8+ cells.
- (D) Neutrophil-mediated inflammation causing acute tubular necrosis. This describes acute tubular necrosis (ATN), which can be ischemic or toxic. While inflammation is present, the biopsy shows *lymphocytic* infiltrates, not predominantly neutrophils. Furthermore, the specific cytotoxic markers on CD8+ T cells point to a specific immune-mediated mechanism, not generic inflammation.
- (E) Pre-formed anti-donor antibodies binding to graft endothelium, causing complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC). This is another mechanism of AMR. Similar to option (A), while possible, the strong evidence for CD8+ T cell cytotoxicity makes this less likely as the *most* direct cause of the observed injury pattern, especially the tubular necrosis. Pre-formed antibodies are less common in deceased donor transplants compared to living donor or sensitized patients.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR)                                  | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR)                                     | Acute Tubular Necrosis (ATN)                                        |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Mediators**   | CD8+ Cytotoxic T cells, CD4+ T helper cells                     | Pre-formed/De novo Anti-donor Antibodies                             | Ischemia, Toxins, Neutrophils                                      |
| **Key Cells in Infiltrate** | Lymphocytes (predominantly CD8+ in this case)                 | Lymphocytes, Plasma cells, Neutrophils, Endothelial cells             | Neutrophils, Tubular epithelial cells                               |
| **Mechanism of Injury** | Direct cell killing (perforin/granzyme), Cytokine release       | CDC, ADCC, Complement-independent endothelial activation/injury      | Ischemic damage, Toxic damage, Inflammatory damage                 |
| **Biopsy Findings**     | Lymphocytic infiltrates, Tubular necrosis, Endothelialitis       | Endothelialitis, Glomerulitis, C4d deposition, Thrombosis             | Tubular cell necrosis, Casts, Flattened epithelium                 |
| **Flow Cytometry**      | CD8+ T cells (granzyme B+, perforin+) often predominant         | Variable, may show B cells, plasma cells, NK cells                   | Not typically used for diagnosis                                    |
| **C4d Deposition**      | Can be present, but less specific than in AMR                   | Often present, especially in complement-activating AMR                | Absent                                                             |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute graft rejection can be cellular (ACR) or antibody-mediated (AMR). ACR is primarily driven by T cells, particularly CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) that directly kill graft cells via perforin and granzyme release. AMR involves antibodies targeting graft endothelium, leading to complement activation and endothelial damage. Distinguishing between ACR and AMR is crucial for management. Key clues include the predominant cell type in the infiltrate (CD8+ T cells suggest ACR) and markers of antibody/complement activation (C4d deposition suggests AMR). Remember that mixed rejection can occur.
